# PREFACE

If the *Author* of these united *tracts* <note>original: "Tracts"; short treatises or pamphlets on a particular subject.</note> had been any *friend* to PREFACES, he would probably have made his *entrance* in that manner in one or another of the *five treatises* formerly published separately. But as for all *prefatory* or *dedicatory discourse*, he has told us his *mind* sufficiently in that *treatise* which he calls SOLILOQUY <note>Referring to "Soliloquy, or Advice to an Author," one of the works in this collection.</note>. Being satisfied, however, that there are many

# PREFACE.

*For those persons who consider these introductory pieces to be essential to the structure of a work, the author has seen fit, on behalf of his honest printer, to provide these lines under the title of* **A PREFACE**; *and to declare:*

" *That (according to his best judgment and authority) these presents <note>original: "these presents"; a legal term referring to the document currently being read</note> ought to pass, and be received, interpreted, and taken as a full and satisfactory substitute for all preliminary writing, dedications, or any direct or indirect requests for favor from the public, or from any private patron or party whatsoever.*

" *Nothing to the contrary appearing to him from the perspective of truth or reason.*" *Witness his hand, this fifth day of December, 1710.*

->A. A. C. A. N. A. Æ.<-
->C. M. D. C. L. X. X. J.<-
<note>These initials and Roman numerals likely represent the author's coded signature and the date or a specific Latin formula common in 18th-century texts.</note>

## A
# LETTER, &c.

**My Lord,** _September 1707._

Now that you have returned to . . . . . <note>The recipient is historically identified as John Somers, 1st Baron Somers, a leading Whig statesman.</note> and before the season arrives which must engage you in the weightier matters of state; if you care to be entertained for a while with a sort of idle thoughts—the kind that pretend only to amusement and have no relation to business or public affairs—you may cast your eye lightly on what you have before you. If there is anything inviting in it, you may read it over at your leisure.

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening of the letter, where the author offers the recipient a collection of light, philosophical reflections to read before the busy parliamentary or political season begins.</summary>

<keywords>Lord Somers, matters of state, amusement, introductory dedication, 1707</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page begins the first section of the letter. Shaftesbury addresses the artificiality of modern poets who imitate the ancient custom of invoking a Muse, leading into his central theme of "enthusiasm" and the necessity of truth in art.</meta>

# A LETTER

<margin>
Section 1.
<image-desc>Decorative flourish underline</image-desc>
</margin>
It has been an established custom for poets, at the beginning of their work, to address themselves to some *Muse*; and this practice of the ancients has gained so much reputation that even in our days we find it almost constantly imitated. I cannot help but imagine, however, that this imitation—which is so readily accepted by others—must at some time or other have given your Lordship pause. You are accustomed to examining things by a better standard than that of fashion or common taste. 

You must certainly have observed our poets under a remarkable constraint when they are obliged to assume this character; and you have wondered, perhaps, why that air of *enthusiasm* <note>original: "Enthusiasm." In the 18th century, this term referred to a state of divine inspiration or poetic fervor. Shaftesbury is exploring why this "inspired" style feels natural in ancient Greek and Roman poetry but forced and insincere in contemporary writing.</note>, which fits so gracefully with an *ancient*, should be so spiritless and awkward in a *modern*. But as to this doubt, your Lordship would have soon resolved it yourself; it could only serve to bring to mind a reflection you have often made on many other occasions: *that truth is the most powerful thing in the world*, since even *fiction* \* itself must be governed by it, and can only please by its resemblance to it. The appearance of reality is necessary to make any passion agreeably represented; and to be able to move others, we must first be moved ourselves, or at least seem to be so, based on some probable grounds. Now, what possibility

---

\* See below, p. 142, etc. and VOLUME III. p. 260, etc. <note>original: "Infra"</note>

<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<summary>Shaftesbury discusses why modern poets often fail when they imitate the "enthusiasm" of ancient writers, arguing that even fictional art must be grounded in truth and a sense of reality to be emotionally effective.</summary>
<keywords>Muse, ancients vs moderns, enthusiasm, truth in art, verisimilitude, Lord Somers</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Section 1 of the letter, examining why ancient poetic inspiration feels authentic compared to modern imitation, and how humans have a tendency to deceive themselves into genuine emotion through artifice.</meta>

# CONCERNING ENTHUSIASM

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
...is there that a modern writer—who is known never to have worshiped **Apollo** or acknowledged any such deity as the *Muses*—should persuade us to believe in his pretended devotion? Why should he move us with his feigned zeal for a religion that is long out of date? But as for the ancients, it is well known that they derived both their religion and their governance <note>original: "Polity."</note> from the *Muses'* art. Therefore, how natural it must have appeared for anyone, but especially a poet of those times, to address himself with raptures of devotion to those acknowledged patronesses of wit and knowledge <note>original: "Science." In this period, "science" referred generally to systematic knowledge or learning rather than specifically the natural sciences.</note>? In that context, a poet could convincingly fake an ecstasy, even if he felt none. And even if it were mere affectation, it would still appear natural and would certainly be pleasing to the reader.

But perhaps, my Lord, there was a deeper mystery in this matter. As your Lordship knows, people are wonderfully skilled at the faculty of deceiving themselves whenever they set their minds to it. A very small foundation of any passion is enough to allow us not only to act it out well but even to work ourselves into a state of emotion that is beyond our own control. Thus, through a little affectation in matters of love—and with the help of a romance or a novel—a fifteen-year-old boy or a serious man of fifty can certainly grow into a very natural fool <note>original: "Coxcomb." A term for a vain, shallow, or conceited person.</note> and feel that *fine passion* <note>original: "Belle Passion," a French term for intense romantic love or infatuation.</note> in good earnest. A man of reasonable good nature, who happens to be a

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that ancient poets were more convincing because their culture truly valued the Muses, whereas modern poets only pretend. He notes that humans have a psychological tendency to eventually believe in the emotions they initially only feign.</summary>

<keywords>Apollo, Muses, affectation, self-deception, poetry, Belle Passion, ancient vs modern, psychology of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how humans can work themselves into genuine states of belief or emotion through a "small inclination well improved," using the example of a Christian bishop believing in fairies to justify why ancient poets might have truly believed in the Muses.</meta>

# A LETTER

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
...slightly offended <note>original: "little piqu'd."</note> may, by nurturing his resentment, become a literal fury for revenge. Even a good Christian—one who insists on being overly good and thinks he can never believe enough—may, by a slight tendency when nurtured, expand his faith so greatly as to include within it not only all scriptural and traditional miracles but even a solid system of old wives' tales. If it were necessary, I could remind your Lordship of an eminent, learned, and truly Christian prelate <note>A high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop.</note> you once knew, who could have given you a full account of his belief in *fairies*. And this, I think, may serve to show how far an ancient poet's faith might possibly have been raised, along with his imagination.

But we Christians, who have such abundant faith ourselves, will allow nothing to the poor heathens. They must be infidels in every sense. We will not allow them to believe even their own religion, which we claim is too absurd to have been believed by anyone besides the common people <note>original: "the mere Vulgar." Referring to the uneducated masses.</note>. But if a respected Christian prelate can be such a "volunteer" in faith—going beyond the ordinary teachings <note>original: "Prescription."</note> of the Catholic Church to believe in *fairies*—why may not a heathen poet, in the ordinary practice of his religion, be allowed to believe in the *Muses*? For these, as your Lordship knows, were considered divine persons in the heathen creed,

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that human psychology allows for the cultivation of extreme belief from small beginnings, using a Christian bishop's belief in fairies to suggest that ancient poets were likely sincere in their devotion to the Muses.</summary>
<keywords>self-deception, faith, fairies, Muses, heathen religion, prelate, imagination, religious psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 6, which explained that the Muses were considered literal divine persons in ancient Greek and Roman religion, rather than just poetic metaphors.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>concerning ENTHUSIASM. 7</header>
<sig>B</sig>

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
...and were essential in their system of theology. These goddesses [the Muses] had their temples and worship, just like the other deities. To disbelieve in *the Holy Nine* <note>The nine Muses of Greek mythology: Calliope, Clio, Erato, Euterpe, Melpomene, Polyhymnia, Terpsichore, Thalia, and Urania.</note>, or their leader **APOLLO**, was the same as to deny **JUPITER** <note>original: "JOVE"</note> himself; it would have been considered equally profane and atheistical by the majority of sensible men. Now, what a mighty advantage it must have been for an ancient poet to be so "orthodox." Through his education and a bit of genuine willpower, he could work himself up to a belief in a divine presence and heavenly inspiration. It was certainly never the business of poets in those days to question *revelation*, especially when it clearly benefited their art so well. On the contrary, they could not help but strengthen their faith as much as possible, since by a single act of belief, well-enforced, they could elevate themselves into such angelic company.

We can observe how much the imagination of such a presence must elevate a genius <note>In this context, "genius" refers to a person's natural talent or the "spirit" of their creativity.</note> simply by looking at the influence that an actual presence has over men. Our modern intellectuals <term>original: "Wits." Here meaning writers, critics, or people of high mental ability.</term> are more or less inspired by the opinion they have of their company, and the idea they form of the people to whom they address their work. Even a common stage actor will tell us how much a full audience of the "better sort" <note>The upper classes or more educated members of society.</note> elevates him above his common

<summary>Shaftesbury explains that ancient poets benefited from a genuine religious belief in the Muses, which provided them with a sense of divine inspiration. He compares this psychological boost to the way a modern writer or actor is elevated by the presence of a sophisticated audience.</summary>
<keywords>Muses, Apollo, Jupiter, poetic inspiration, orthodoxy, genius, audience influence, psychology of belief</keywords>

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
...pitch. And you, my Lord, who are the noblest actor, playing the noblest role assigned to any human on this earthly stage, when you are working for **liberty** and **humanity**; does not the public presence—that of your friends and those who wish your cause well—add something to your thinking and your *genius*? <note>original: "Genius." In this context, it refers to a person's natural talent or creative spirit.</note> Or is that height of reason and power of eloquence, which you display in public, no more than what you are equally a master of in private? Can you command it at any time, when alone, or with unremarkable company, or in some relaxed or calm hour? This would indeed be more godlike; but I think ordinary human nature does not reach so high.

For my own part, my Lord, I have such a great need for a significant presence or company to elevate my thoughts on any occasion that, when I am alone, I must try to provide for this need through the power of my imagination <term>original: "Strength of Fancy." Here meaning the ability to visualize or mentally simulate a situation.</term>. For lack of a *Muse*, I must seek out some great man of extraordinary talent, whose imagined presence might inspire me with more than what I feel during ordinary hours. And so, my Lord, I have chosen to address myself to your Lordship, though without signing my name. This allows you, as a stranger, the total freedom to read no more than what you feel like reading; while I reserve for myself the privilege of imagining you

## SECTION II.

If knowing well how to expose any weakness or vice were a sufficient guarantee for the opposite virtue, what an excellent age we might be presumed to live in! Never was there a known time in our nation when folly and extravagance of every kind were more closely scrutinized or more cleverly ridiculed. And one might at least hope from this good symptom that our age was not in a state of decline; since whatever our social ailments <note>original: "Distempers." Referring to societal or political disorders likened to physical diseases.</note> are, we seem very willing to accept our remedies. For an individual to tolerate being told of their faults is the best sign of improvement. It is rare for the general public to be so inclined. For where political suspicion <term>original: "Jealousy of State." The government's protective suspicion or vigilance regarding its authority and secrets.</term>, or the immoral lives of powerful people, or any other cause is strong enough to restrict the *freedom of criticism* in any part, it effectively destroys the benefit of it for the whole. There can be no *impartial* and *free criticism* of manners where any particular custom or national opinion is set aside—not only exempted from criticism, but even flattered with the highest art. It is only in a free nation such as ours that deception <note>original: "Imposture." Referring to religious or political fraud and fake claims of authority.</note> has no privilege; and

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while modern society is good at ridiculing folly, true moral improvement requires the total freedom to criticize all aspects of life, including national customs and the powerful, without exception.</summary>
<keywords>criticism, social reform, freedom of speech, virtue, vice, national character, political suspicion, deception</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 9, completing the sentence about how in a free nation, no institution—whether the court, the nobility, or the church—is exempt from public scrutiny and criticism.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>A LETTER</header>

Section 2.
<image-desc>decorative horizontal scroll flourish</image-desc>
that neither the influence <note>original: "Credit."</note> of a Court, the power of a Nobility, nor the venerable authority <term>original: "Awfulness." Here meaning the quality of inspiring awe or deep reverence, rather than the modern sense of being "bad."</term> of a Church can give her protection, or hinder her from being criticized <note>original: "arraign'd."</note> in every shape and appearance. It is true, this liberty may seem to go too far. We may perhaps be said to make ill use of it. So everyone will say when he himself is affected <note>original: "touch'd."</note> and his opinion freely examined. But who shall be judge of *what* may be freely examined, and *what* may not? *Where* liberty may be used, and *where* it may not? What remedy shall we prescribe for this in general? Can there be a better one than that very liberty itself which is being complained of? If men are vicious, petulant, or abusive, the magistrate <term>A civil officer or judge who administers the law.</term> may correct them; but if they reason poorly, it is Reason itself that must teach them to do better. Correctness of thought and style, refinement in manners, good breeding, and politeness of every kind can come only from the trial and experience of what is best. Let the search go on freely, and the right standard for everything will soon be found. Whatever whim or trend <note>original: "Humour." Referring to a prevailing cultural mood or frame of mind.</note> has gained momentum—if it is unnatural, it cannot last; and *ridicule*, if misplaced at first, will certainly fall at last where it is deserved.

I have often wondered to see men of sense so greatly alarmed at the approach of anything like *ridicule* on certain sub-

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that the best remedy for the "ill use" of free speech is more freedom, asserting that reason and social refinement can only develop through open scrutiny and that misplaced ridicule will eventually correct itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 10 regarding the use of ridicule as a test for truth. It specifically addresses why people are often afraid to let serious subjects be questioned or joked about.</meta>

<header>concerning ENTHUSIASM.</header>

subjects; as if they mistrusted their own judgment. 
<margin>Section 2.</margin> 
For how can ridicule be used against reason? Or how can anyone with even the slightest clarity of thought tolerate ridicule that is misdirected? Nothing is more ridiculous than that itself. Ordinary people <note>original: "The Vulgar"</note> may indeed accept any crude joke, simple drollery, or buffoonery; but it requires a finer and more genuine wit to appeal to men of sense and refinement <note>original: "Breeding"</note>. 

How does it happen, then, that we seem like such cowards in our reasoning, and are so afraid to face the *test* of ridicule? "Oh!" we say, "the subjects are too serious <note>original: "grave"</note>." Perhaps they are. But let us first see whether they are actually serious or not. In the way we think about them, they might perhaps seem very serious and weighty in our imagination, while being very ridiculous and irrelevant <note>original: "impertinent"</note> in their own nature. 

*Gravity* <note>In this context, gravity refers to an air of extreme seriousness, solemnity, or formality.</note> is the very essence of deception <note>original: "Imposture"</note>. It not only causes us to misunderstand other things but is almost constantly prone to misunderstanding itself. For even in ordinary behavior, how difficult is it for a serious person to avoid crossing the line into being merely formal or stiff? We can never be too serious if we can be certain that we truly are what we believe ourselves to be. And we can never too greatly honor or revere anything for being serious if we are certain the thing is as serious as we perceive it. The main point is to always distinguish *true gravity from false gravity*. This can only be done by applying the rule 

<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: con-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that ridicule cannot harm true reason and that "gravity" or extreme seriousness is often a mask for deception. He suggests that we must distinguish between what is truly serious and what only appears so through a false sense of formality.</summary>
<keywords>ridicule, reason, gravity, imposture, wit, judgment, truth-testing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that ridicule is a necessary tool for testing whether something is truly serious or merely an empty, "formal" deception.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>A LETTER</header>

Section 2. constantly with us, and freely applying it not only to the things around us, but to ourselves. For if, unfortunately, we lose the measure within ourselves, we shall soon lose it in everything else. Now what rule or measure is there in the world, except in considering the real nature <note>original: "Temper"</note> of things, to find which are truly serious and which are ridiculous? And how can this be done, unless by *applying the ridicule*, to see whether it will withstand the test? But if we fear to apply this rule in *anything*, what security can we have against the deception <note>original: "Imposture"</note> of formality <note>In this context, "formality" refers to an empty or outward show of seriousness without any real substance.</note> in *all* things? We have allowed ourselves to be *formalists* in one point; and that same formality may rule us as it pleases in all others.

It is not in every state of mind <note>original: "Disposition"</note> that we are capable of judging things. We must beforehand judge our own temperament <note>original: "Temper"</note>, and accordingly judge other things which fall under our judgment. But we must never again pretend to judge things, or our own temperament in judging them, once we have given up our preliminary right of judgment; and, under a presumption of gravity, have allowed ourselves to be most ridiculous, and to admire profoundly the most ridiculous things in nature—at least, for all we know. For having resolved never to try them, we can never be sure.

This, my Lord, I may safely declare <note>original: "aver"</note>, is so true in itself, and so well known as truth by the cunning *formalists* <note>People who strictly adhere to outward forms, rituals, or appearances to hide a lack of substance.</note> of the age, that they can better tolerate having their deceptions <note>original: "Impostures"</note> railed against with all the bitterness and intensity imaginable, than to have them touched ever so gently in this other way (ridicule). They know very well that, just like fashions and trends, *opinions*—even if they are completely ridiculous—are kept alive by solemnity. They know that those formal notions which likely grew up in a bad mood, and were conceived in serious gloom, can never be removed except by a serious kind of cheerfulness and by a more easy and pleasant way of thinking. There is a *melancholy* <note>In the 1700s, "melancholy" was considered a physical and mental condition of gloominess or depression that was thought to lead to religious delusions.</note> which accompanies all enthusiasm. Whether it be *love* or *religion* (for there are enthusiasms in both), nothing can stop the growing harm of either until that melancholy is removed, and the mind is at liberty to hear what can be said against the ridiculousness of an extreme in either direction.

In the past, it was the wisdom of some wise nations to let people be fools as much as they pleased, and never to punish...

There are certain temperaments <note>original: "Humours" — in 18th-century medicine, these were bodily fluids believed to control health and mood.</note> in mankind which must necessarily find an outlet. Both the human mind and body are naturally subject to disturbances. Just as there are strange chemical agitations <note>original: "Ferments"</note> in the blood which, in many bodies, cause an unusual discharge, so too in our reason there are mismatched <note>original: "heterogeneous"</note> ideas that must be expelled through a kind of mental fermentation.

If doctors were to try to completely suppress these bodily agitations and push back the fluids <note>original: "Humours"</note> that appear in such skin breakouts, they might—instead of providing a cure—likely cause a plague. They might turn a mild seasonal fever <note>original: "Spring-Ague"</note> or an autumn overindulgence into a deadly, infectious fever. 

Those who would meddle with these mental outbursts in the state <note>original: "Body-Politick"</note> are surely just as bad as those doctors. Under the false pretense of healing this "itch" of superstition and saving souls from the infection of religious fanaticism <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note>, they would throw all of nature into chaos. They would turn a few harmless boils <note>original: "Carbuncles"</note> into a deadly inflammation and gangrene.

The hoarse bellowing of the caves, joined with the hideous appearance of such dark and desert places, raised such horror in the enemy that, in this state, their imagination helped them to hear voices and undoubtedly to see forms that were more than human; meanwhile, the uncertainty of what they feared made their fear even greater and spread it faster through silent looks than any spoken account could convey. And this was what in later times men called *a panic*. The story indeed gives a good hint of the nature of this passion, which can hardly exist without some mixture of fanaticism <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note> and horrors of a superstitious kind.

One may with good reason call every passion *panic* that is raised in a crowd <note>original: "Multitude"</note> and conveyed by looks, or as it were by contact or sympathy. Thus, popular fury may be called *panic* when the rage of the people—as we have sometimes seen—has put them beyond themselves, especially where religion has been involved. And in this state, their very looks are infectious. The fury flies from face to face, and the disease is no sooner seen than caught. Those who, in a better state of mind

Such is the force of * companionship <note>original: "Society"</note> in bad, as well as in good passions; and any *feeling* <note>original: "Affection"</note> is so much stronger for being *social* and *communicative*.

Thus, my Lord, there are many *panics* among mankind, besides merely that of fear. And thus religion is also a *panic* when *fanaticism* <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note> of any kind arises, as it often will on melancholy occasions. For vapors <note>depressive spirits or moods</note> naturally rise; and in bad times especially, when the spirits of men are low—whether in public calamities, or during periods of unwholesome air or diet, or when convulsions happen in nature, such as storms, earthquakes, or other amazing *prodigies*—at such seasons the *panic* must necessarily run high, and the magistrate of necessity must give way to it. For to apply a serious remedy and bring the sword, or *fasces* <note>a bundle of rods and an axe, symbols of the magistrate's power to punish</note>, as a cure, would only make the case more melancholy and increase the very cause of the disorder. THIS was ancient policy: and hence (as a notable * author of our nation expresses it), it is necessary for a people to have a *public leading* in religion. For to deny the magistrate a worship, or to take away a national church, is as much mere *fanaticism* <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note> as the notion which sets up persecution. For why should there not be public walks, as well as private gardens? Why not public libraries, as well as private education and home-tutors? Not only were the visionaries and fanatics <note>original: "Enthusiasts"</note> of all kinds tolerated by the ancients, as your lordship knows; but on the other hand, philosophy had as free a course and was permitted as a balance against superstition. And while some sects, such as the *Pythagorean* and later *Platonic*, joined in with the superstition and fanaticism <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note> of the times; the *Epicurean*, the *Academic*, and others, were allowed to use all the force of wit and mockery <note>original: "Raillery"</note> against it. And thus matters were happily balanced; reason had fair play; learning and science flourished. Wonderful was the harmony and temperament <note>original: "Temper"</note> which arose from all these contradictions. Thus superstition and fanaticism <note>original: "Enthusiasm"</note> were mildly treated; and being left alone, they never raged to that degree as to occasion bloodshed, wars, persecutions, and devastations in the world. But a new sort of policy, which extends itself to another world and considers the future lives and happiness of men rather than the present, has made us leap the bounds of natural humanity; and out of a supernatural charity, has taught us the way of plaguing one another most devoutly. It has raised an * antipathy which no temporal interest could ever do; and entailed

And now *uniformity in opinion* (a hopeful project!) is looked on as the only remedy against this evil. The *saving* of souls is now the heroic passion of exalted spirits; and has become, in a sense, the chief care of the magistrate, and the very purpose of government itself.

If the government <note>original: "Magistracy"</note> should deign to intervene this much in other sciences, I am afraid we would have as bad logic, as bad mathematics, and in every kind as bad philosophy as we often have divinity in countries where a precise orthodoxy is established by law. It is a difficult matter for a government to regulate wit <note>original: "settle Wit," meaning to fix or define the standard of intellect and reason</note>. If it merely keeps us sober and honest, it is likely we shall have as much ability in our spiritual affairs as in our worldly <note>original: "temporal"</note> ones; and if we can only be trusted, we shall have enough intelligence <note>original: "Wit"</note> to save ourselves when no prejudice stands in the way. But if honesty and intelligence are insufficient for this *saving* work, it is in vain for the magistrate to interfere with it; since, however virtuous or wise he may be, he may be just as easily mistaken as any other man. I am sure the only way to save people's common sense, or preserve intelligence at all in the world, is to give liberty to wit. Indeed, people who cannot tolerate a middle ground, but are entirely flighty and impulsive, know little of the doubts and hesitations of religion. They are safe from any immediate influence of *devout melancholy* or *enthusiasm*, both of which require more reflection and consistent practice to become fixed in one's character and become a habit. But whatever the habit may be, to be delivered from it at such a tragic cost as thoughtlessness or madness is something I would never wish for myself. I would rather face all the risks and challenges of religion than try to rid myself of thoughts about it through distraction. All I argue for is thinking about it *in the right frame of mind*; and I shall try to demonstrate that doing so is more than half the battle in thinking about it correctly.

GOOD HUMOR is not only the best security against *enthusiasm*, but the best foundation for *piety* and *true religion*. For if correct thoughts and worthy perceptions of the Supreme Being are fundamental to all true worship and adoration, it is more than probable that we will never go wrong in this regard, except through bad humor alone. It is true that the magistrate might possibly have been surprised by the novelty of a notion which he might claim, perhaps, not only destroyed the sacredness of his power but also treated him and all men as profane, impious, and damned if they did not participate in certain specific modes of worship; thousands of which had been established previously, all of them compatible and tolerant of one another until that time. However, such was the wisdom of some succeeding administrations that the severity of persecution was much reduced; and even that * prince, who was considered the greatest enemy of the Christian sect and who had been educated in it himself, was a great restrainer of persecution and would allow nothing further than a reclamation of church lands and public schools, without any attack on the property or persons even of those who criticized the state religion and made a virtue of insulting public worship.

It is well that we have the authority of a sacred author in our religion to assure us,

Section 3. choice Droll or Puppet-Show at Bartholomew Fair. There, doubtless, their strange voices and involuntary agitations are admirably well acted by the motion of wires and the blowing of pipes. For the bodies of the prophets, in their state of prophecy, not being in their own power but (as they say themselves) mere passive organs, actuated by an exterior force, have nothing natural or resembling real life in any of their sounds or motions: so that however awkwardly a puppet show may imitate other actions, it must certainly represent this passion to the life. And while Bartholomew Fair is in possession of this privilege, I dare stand security for our National Church that no sect of enthusiasts, no new vendors of prophecy or miracles, shall ever get the start of her, or put her to the trouble of trying her strength with them in any case.

HAPPY it was for us that when Popery <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholicism.</note> had gained possession, *Smithfield* <note>A site in London famous for both its summer fair and the public execution of heretics.</note> was used in a more tragical way. Many of our first Reformers, it is feared, were little better than enthusiasts: and God knows whether a warmth of this kind did not considerably help us in throwing off that spiritual tyranny. So that if the priests had not, as is usual, preferred the love of blood to all other passions, they might, perhaps, have evaded the greatest force in a merrier way.

I BELIEVE our great and learned apostle found less advantage from the easy treatment of his *Athenian* antagonists than from the surly and cursed spirit of the most persecuting *Jewish* cities. He gained less from the candor and civility of his *Roman* judges than from the zeal of the synagogue and the vehemence of his national priests. Though when I consider this apostle appearing either before the witty *Athenians* or before a *Roman* court of justice, in the presence of their great men and ladies, and see how handsomely he accommodates himself to the perceptions and temper of those more polite people: I do not find that he declines the way of *wit* or *good humor*; but, without suspicion of his cause, is willing generously to commit it to this proof, and try it against the sharpness of any ridicule which might be offered.

### SECTION IV.

IN SHORT, my Lord, the melancholy way of treating religion is that which, according to my understanding, renders it so tragic, and is the occasion of its acting in reality such dismal tragedies in the world. And my notion is, that provided we treat religion with good manners, we can never use too much *good humor*, or examine it with too much *freedom* and *familiarity*. For, if it be genuine and sincere, it will not only stand the test, but thrive and gain an advantage from this; if it be fake, or mixed with any imposture, it will be detected and exposed.

THE melancholy way in which we have been taught religion makes us unsuited to think of it in good humor. It is in adversity chiefly, or in ill health, under affliction, or disturbance of mind, or lack of composure, that we have recourse to it. Though in reality we are never so unfit to think of it as at such a heavy and dark hour. We can never be fit to contemplate anything above us, when

WE have a notable instance of this *freedom* in one of our sacred authors. As patient as JOB is said to be, it cannot be denied that he makes bold enough with GOD, and takes his Providence roundly to task. His friends, indeed, plead hard with him, and use all arguments, right or wrong, to patch up objections, and set the affairs of Providence upon an equal footing. They make a merit of saying all the good they can of GOD, at the very stretch of their reason, and sometimes quite beyond it. But this, in JOB'S opinion, is *flattering* GOD, *favoring* GOD'S *person*, and even *mocking him*. And no wonder. For, what merit can there be in believing in GOD, or his Providence, upon frivolous and weak grounds? What virtue is there in assuming an opinion contrary to the appearance of things, and resolving to hear nothing which may be said against it? **IT** is impossible that anyone besides an ill-natured man can wish against the existence of a **GOD**: for this is wishing against the public, and even against one’s private good too, if rightly understood. But if a man does not have any such ill-will to stifle his belief, he must surely have an unhappy opinion of **GOD**, and believe him to be not nearly as good as he knows **himself** to be, if he imagines that an impartial use of his reason, in any matter of speculation whatsoever, can make him run any risk in the afterlife; and that a cowardly *denial of his reason*, and a *pretense of belief* in any point too difficult for his understanding, can entitle him to any favor in another world. This is being *sycophants in religion*, mere *parasites of devotion*. It is using **GOD** as crafty *beggars* use those they address, when they are ignorant of their social rank. The novices among them may innocently come out, perhaps, with a *“Good Sir,”* or a *“Good Madam!”* But with the old pros, no matter whom they meet in a carriage, it is

<margin>Section 4.</margin>
To love the public, to study universal good, and to promote the interest of the whole world as far as lies within our power, is surely the height of goodness, and creates that disposition which we call *Divine*. In this disposition, my Lord (for surely you should know it well), it is natural for us to wish that others should share with us by being convinced of the sincerity of our example. It is natural for us to wish our merit should be known; particularly if it is our fortune to have served a nation as a good minister, or—as some prince or father of a country—to have rendered happy a considerable part of mankind under our care. But if it happened that among this number there should be some so ignorantly bred, and from so remote a province, as to have been beyond the hearing of our name and actions; or hearing of them, should be so puzzled by strange and contradictory stories told here and there concerning us, that they knew not what to think, or whether there really was any such person in the world as ourselves: should we not, in good truth, be ridiculous to take offense at this? And should we not be considered extravagantly morose and ill-humored if, instead of treating the matter with *raillery*, we should think in earnest

What shall we say then? Does it really deserve praise to be so concerned about it? Is doing good for *glory's* sake so divine a thing? Or is it not more divine to do good even where it may be thought inglorious—even to the ungrateful and to those who are completely unaware of the good they receive? How does it happen then, that what is so *divine* in us should lose its character in *the Divine Being*? And that according to how *the* DEITY is represented to us, he should more resemble the weak, womanish, and powerless part of our nature, than the generous, manly, and divine?

## S E C T. V.

ONE would think, my Lord, it were in reality no hard thing to know our own weaknesses at first sight, and distinguish the features of human frailty, with which we are so well acquainted. One would think it were easy to understand that provocation and offense, anger, revenge, and jealousy regarding honor or power, the love of fame, glory, and the like, belong only to limited be-

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 38 regarding the nature of the Deity and why human frailties like malice or jealousy are incompatible with a perfect, universal Being.</meta>

<header>concerning ENTHUSIASM.</header>

<margin>Section 5.</margin>
ings, and are necessarily excluded from a Being which is perfect and universal. But if we have never settled within ourselves any notion of what is *morally excellent*; or if we cannot trust that reason which tells us that nothing besides *what is so* can have a place in *the* DEITY; we can neither trust anything which others relate of him, or which he himself reveals to us. We must be satisfied beforehand that he is *good* and cannot deceive us. Without this, there can be no real religious faith or confidence. Now, if there is really something prior to revelation—some antecedent demonstration of reason to assure us that GOD *is*, and furthermore, that he is *so good* as not to deceive us—the same reason, if we will trust it, will demonstrate to us that GOD is *so good* as to exceed the very best of us *in goodness*. And in this manner, we can have no dread or suspicion to make us uneasy; for it is *malice* alone, and not *goodness*, which can make us afraid.

THERE is an odd way of reasoning—which in certain distempers of mind is very effective for those who can apply it—and it is this:

> “ but where interests are opposed. A 
> “ universal Being can have no opposing 
> “ interest; and therefore can have no

I think, my Lord, it would be well for us if, before † we ascended into the higher regions of *Divinity*, we would be willing to descend a little into *ourselves*, and bestow some humble thoughts upon plain, honest *morals*. When we had once looked into ourselves and distinguished well the nature of our own affections, we would probably be better judges of the *divineness* of a character, and discern better what affections were suitable or unsuitable to a *perfect Being*. We might then understand how to *love* and *praise*, once we had acquired some consistent notion of what was *laudable* or *lovely*. Otherwise, we might chance to do God little honor when we intended Him the most. For it is hard to imagine what honor can arise to the DEITY

Section 5. regarding the praises of creatures, who are unable to discern what is *praiseworthy* or excellent in their own kind.

---

IF a musician were praised to the skies by a certain set of people who had no ear for music, he would surely be put to the blush; and could hardly, with a straight face, accept the goodwill of his listeners until they had acquired a more competent understanding of him, and could by their own senses find something really good in his performance. Until this was brought about, there would be little *glory* in the matter; and the musician, though ever so vain, would have little reason to be satisfied.

THEY who desire praise the most would rather not be noticed at all than be pointlessly applauded. I do not know how it happens that HE who is always said to do good most selflessly should be thought to desire to be praised so lavishly, and be supposed to set so high a value upon so cheap and low a thing as *ignorant commendation* and *forced applause*.

It is not the same with *goodness* as with other qualities, which we may understand very well and yet not possess. We may have an excellent ear for *music*,

We may judge well of *Poetry*, without being poets or possessing the least of a poetic vein: but we can have no tolerable notion of *Goodness*, without being tolerably good. So that if the *Praise* of a Divine Being is so great a part of his worship, we should, I think, learn *Goodness*, were it for nothing else than that we might learn, in some tolerable manner, how to *praise*. For the praise of goodness from an unsound, hollow heart must certainly make the greatest dissonance in the world.

## S E C T. VI.

Other reasons, my Lord, there are, why this plain, homespun philosophy of looking into ourselves may do us wondrous service in rectifying our errors in religion. For there is a sort of second-hand enthusiasm. And when men find no original commotions in themselves—no prepossessing *Panic* which bewitches them—they are still liable, by the testimony of others, to be imposed upon and led credulously into the belief of many false miracles. And this habit may make them variable and of a very inconstant faith, easy to be carried away with every wind of doctrine and addicted to every upstart sect or superstition. But the knowledge of our passions in their very

Section 6. seeds—measuring well the growth and progress of enthusiasm, and judging rightly its natural force and what command it has over our very *senses*—may teach us to oppose more successfully those delusions which come armed with the specious pretext of moral certainty and *matter of fact*.

The new prophesying sect I mentioned above seems to claim, among many other miracles, to have had a most remarkable one, performed intentionally and with warning before many hundreds of people who actually testify to its truth. But I would only ask whether there was present among those hundreds any one person who, having never been of their *sect* or addicted to their way, will give the same testimony as them? I must not be satisfied to ask whether such a person had been wholly free of that particular enthusiasm, but whether, before that time, he was considered to have such sound judgment and a clear head as to be wholly free of *melancholy* and, in all likelihood, incapable of any other enthusiasm besides? It is so necessary to give way to this disorder of *enthusiasm* that even that philosopher who directed the whole force of his philosophy against *superstition* appears to have left room for visionary *fancy*, and to have indirectly tolerated *enthusiasm*. For it is hard to imagine that one who had as little religious faith as EPICURUS should have such vulgar credulity as to believe those accounts of armies and castles in the air, and such visionary *phenomena*. Yet he allows them; and then thinks he can solve them by his *effluvia*, and *aerial mirrors*, and I know not what other stuff—which his *Latin poet*, however, sets off beautifully, as he does everything.

It was a sign this philosopher believed there was a significant amount of *visionary spirit* originally in human nature. He was so satisfied that men were inclined to see visions that, rather than have them go without, he chose to provide them ready at hand. Notwithstanding he denied the principles of religion to be * *natural*, he was forced tacitly to allow there was a wondrous disposition in mankind toward *supernatural objects*; and that if these ideas were vain, they were yet in a manner *innate*, or such as men were really born to, and could hardly by any means avoid. From which concession, a theologian, it seems to me, might raise a good argument against him, for the *truth* as well as the *usefulness* of RELIGION. But so it is:

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>A LETTER</header>

<margin>Section 6.</margin>
Whether the matter of an apparition be true or false, the symptoms are the same, and the passion is of equal force in the person who is vision-struck. The *Lymphatici* of the *Latins* were the *Nympholepti* of the *Greeks*. They were persons said to have seen some species of divinity, such as either some rural *deity* or *nymph*, which threw them into such transports that it overcame their reason. The ecstasies expressed themselves outwardly in quakings, tremblings, tossings of the head and limbs, *agitations*, and (as *Livy* calls them) *fanatical throes* or convulsions, extemporary prayer, prophecy, singing, and the like. All nations have their *lymphatics* of some kind or another; and all churches, heathen as well as Christian, have had their complaints against *fanaticism*.

One would think the ancients imagined this disease had some relation to that which they called *hydrophobia*. Whether the ancient *lymphatics* had any way, like that of biting, to communicate the rage of their distemper, I cannot so positively determine. But there have been certain fanatics since the time of the ancients who have had a most successful faculty for communicating the appetite of the teeth. For since the snappish spirit first arose in religion, all sects have been at it, as the saying goes, *tooth and nail*;

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author describes how religious ecstasies and "fanatical" symptoms are physically the same regardless of whether a vision is real, comparing historical "lymphatics" to a contagious, aggressive madness.</summary>

<margin>Sect. 7.</margin>
spiration, by its outward marks, be easily distinguished from it. For *inspiration* is *a real feeling of the divine presence*, and *enthusiasm* is *a false one*. But the passion they arouse is much alike. For when the mind is absorbed in vision and fixes its view either on any real object or a mere specter of divinity; when it sees, or thinks it sees, anything prodigious and more than human; its horror, delight, confusion, fear, admiration, or whatever passion belongs to it or is dominant on this occasion, will have something vast, *monstrous*, and (as painters say) *beyond life*. And this is what gave rise to the name *fanaticism*, as it was used by the ancients in its original sense: an *apparition* transporting the mind.

SOMETHING there will be of extravagance and fury when the ideas or images received are too big for the narrow human vessel to contain. So that *inspiration* may be justly called *divine* ENTHUSIASM; for the word itself signifies *divine presence*, and was used by the philosopher <note>Plato</note> whom the earliest Christian fathers called *divine* to express whatever was sublime in human passions *. <page-num>54</page-num>
<header>A LETTER</header>

<margin>Sect. 7.</margin>
[This] was the spirit he allotted to *Heroes*, *Statesmen*, *Poets*, *Orators*, *Musicians*, and even *Philosophers* themselves. Nor can we help but ascribe a *noble ENTHUSIASM* of our own accord to whatever is greatly performed by any of *these*. Thus, almost all of us know something of this principle. But to know it as we should, and to discern its various kinds—both in ourselves and others—this is the great work, and by this means alone can we hope to avoid delusion. For *to judge the spirits to see whether they are from God*, we must first *judge our own spirit*: whether it is of *reason* and *sound sense*; whether it is fit to *judge* at all by being sedate, cool, and impartial; and free of every biasing passion, every giddy vapor, or melancholy fume. This is the first knowledge and previous *judgment*: “To understand *ourselves*, and know what *spirit we are of*.” Afterward, we may judge the *spirit* in others, consider what their *personal merit* is, and—

---

“I realized then, about poets too, in a short time, that they do not create through wisdom, but by some natural power and while inspired, like divine seers and fortune-tellers.” *Apology*. In particular, as to *Philosophers*, PLUTARCH tells us it was the complaint of some of the old *Romans*, when learning first came to them from *Greece*, that their youth grew *enthusiastic* with *philosophy*. For speaking of one of the philosophers of the *Athenian* embassy, he says, “He has instilled a terrible passion in the young, which causes them to abandon other pleasures and pastimes and become enthusiastic about philosophy.” *Plutarch, Life of Cato the Elder.*
<note>Regarding this passion in its nobler and higher sense, see more, VOL. II, pages 75, 76, 393, 394, etc., and VOL. III, pages 30, 33, 34, 37.</note>

### SECTION I.

I HAVE been considering (my friend!) what your notion was, to express such a surprise as you did the other day, when I happened to speak to you in praise of *Raillery*. Was it possible that you should suppose me such a serious man as to dislike *all* conversation of

<summary>The author questions why his friend was surprised by his support for raillery, wondering if he is perceived as being too serious to enjoy lighthearted conversation.</summary>
<keywords>raillery, wit, conversation, social behavior, humor</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's address to his friend regarding the use of humor and wit (raillery) in philosophical discussion. It follows the end of page 59, which concluded with a question about disliking conversation of a certain kind.</meta>

<margin>Part I.</margin>this kind? Or were you afraid I should not stand the trial, if you put me to it, by making the experiment in *my own* case?

I MUST confess, you had reason enough for your caution; if you could imagine me at the bottom to be such a true *zealot* that I could not bear the least raillery regarding my own opinions. It is the case, I know, with many. Whatever they think is grave or solemn, they suppose must never be treated in anything but a grave and solemn way; though what *another* thinks is so, they are content to treat otherwise, and are eager to try the edge of ridicule against any opinions besides *their own*.

THE question is, whether this is fair or not? And whether it is not just and reasonable to be as free with our *own* opinions as with those of *other people*? For to be sparing in this case may be looked upon as a piece of selfishness. We may perhaps be charged with willful ignorance and blind idolatry for having taken opinions on trust, and for having consecrated within ourselves certain *idol-notions* which we will never allow to be unveiled or seen in the open light. They may perhaps be monsters, and not divinities or sacred truths, that are kept so carefully in some dark corner of our minds; these specters may deceive us while we refuse

<summary>The author argues that one should be willing to subject their own opinions to the same ridicule and scrutiny they apply to the beliefs of others. He warns that protecting ideas from such raillery may lead to harboring false "idol-notions" out of willful ignorance.</summary>
<keywords>raillery, skepticism, self-examination, zealotry, ridicule, idol-notions, prejudice</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that opinions should not be hidden in darkness but must be examined from every angle to ensure they are not merely "idol-notions."</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>of Wit and Humour.</header>
<sig>[E 2]</sig>

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin>
fuse to turn them every way, and view their shapes and appearances in every light. <image-desc>horizontal decorative flourish</image-desc>
For that which can be shown only in a *certain light* is questionable. Truth, it is supposed, may bear *all lights*: and one of those principal lights or natural mediums, by which things are to be viewed, in order to a thorough recognition, is *ridicule* itself, or that manner of proof by which we discern whatever is liable to just raillery in any subject. So much, at least, is allowed by all who at any time appeal to this *criterion*. The gravest gentlemen, even on the gravest subjects, are supposed to acknowledge this: and can have no right, it is thought, to deny others the freedom of this appeal; while they are free to censure like other men, and in their gravest arguments make no scruple to ask, *Is it not ridiculous?*

Of this affair, therefore, I intend for you to know fully what my sentiments are. And by this means you will be able to judge me; whether I was sincere the other day in the defense of *raillery*, and can continue still to plead for those ingenious friends of ours, who are often censured for their humor of this kind, and for the freedom they take in such an airy way of conversation and writing.

## S E C T. II.

IN ALL seriousness, when one considers how this type of wit is sometimes used, and the excess to which it has risen lately among certain figures of the age, one may be a little startled and in doubt about what to think of the practice, or where this mocking humor will eventually lead us. It has passed from the men of pleasure to the men of business. Politicians have been infected with it, and the grave affairs of state have been treated with an air of *irony* and *banter*. The most capable negotiators have been known as the most notable *buffoons*, and the most celebrated authors as the greatest masters of *burlesque*.

THERE is indeed a kind of *defensive raillery* (if I may call it so) which I am willing enough to allow in affairs of any kind, especially when a spirit of curiosity would force the disclosure of more truth than can conveniently be told. For we can never do more harm to truth than by revealing too much of it on certain occasions. It is the same with minds as it is with eyes: for a certain size and build, only so much light is necessary, and no more. Whatever goes beyond that brings darkness and confusion.

<meta>catchword: IT IS</meta>

<summary>The author observes that irony and banter have moved beyond social circles into the serious realms of politics and business. He suggests that while this can be excessive, a "defensive raillery" is sometimes necessary to protect the truth from being prematurely or overly exposed to those who cannot handle it.</summary>
<keywords>wit, irony, banter, politics, defensive raillery, truth, statecraft, burlesque</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the appropriate use of wit, distinguishing between its use as a protective tool and its abuse as a means of malicious confusion.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>of Wit and Humour.</header>

<meta>catchword: its</meta>

### Sect. 2.

It is real humanity and kindness to hide strong truths from tender eyes. To do this through a pleasant diversion is easier and more civil than through a harsh denial or a notable reserve. But to go about industriously to confound people in a mysterious manner, and to take advantage or derive pleasure from the perplexity they are thrown into by such uncertain talk, is as improper in the way of raillery as when done with the greatest seriousness or in the most solemn way of deceit. It may be necessary, now as well as in the past, for wise men to speak in parables and with a double meaning, so that the enemy may be distracted, and only those who have ears to hear may hear. But it is certainly a mean, weak, and dull sort of wit that puzzles everyone alike and leaves even the most sensible person, and even a friend, equally in doubt and at a loss to understand what one’s real mind is upon any subject.

This is that *gross* sort of *raillery* which is so offensive in good company. And indeed, there is as much difference between one sort and another as between fair dealing and hypocrisy, or between the most genteel wit and the most scurrilous buffoonery. But through freedom of conversation, this illiberal kind of wit will lose

<image-desc>Horizontal decorative flourish</image-desc> Liberty and commerce bring it to its true standard. The only danger is laying an embargo. The same thing happens here as in the case of *trade*. Impositions and restrictions reduce it to a low ebb: nothing is so advantageous to it as a *free port*.

We have seen in our own time the decline and ruin of a false sort of wit, which so much delighted our ancestors that their poems and plays, as well as sermons, were full of it. All humor had something of the *quibble*. The very language of the court was *punning*. But it is now banished from the town and all good company: there are only some few traces of it in the country; and it seems at last confined to the nurseries of youth, as the chief entertainment of pedants and their pupils. And thus in other respects *wit* will improve in our hands, and *humor* will refine itself, if we take care not to tamper with it and bring it under constraint by severe usage and rigorous prescriptions. All politeness is due to liberty. We polish one another, and rub off our corners and rough sides by a sort of *amicable collision*. To restrain this is inevitably to bring a rust upon men’s understandings. It is a destruction of civility, good breeding, and

## S E C T. III.

To describe true *raillery* would be as hard a matter, and *perhaps* as little to the purpose, as to define *good breeding*. None can understand the theory, besides those who have the practice. Yet everyone thinks himself *well-bred*: and the most formal pedant imagines he can rally with good grace and humor. I have known some of those grave gentlemen undertake to correct an author for defending the use of raillery, who at the same time have at every turn made use of that weapon, though they were naturally so very awkward at it. And this, I believe, may be observed in the case of many zealots who have taken it upon themselves to answer our modern free-writers. The tragical gentlemen, with the grim aspect and mien of true *inquisitors*, have but an ill grace when they vouchsafe to quit their austerity and be jocose and pleasant with an adversary whom they would choose to treat in a very different manner. For to do them justice, had they their way, I do not doubt but that their conduct and mien would be pretty much of a piece. They would, in all probability, soon quit their farce and make a thorough tragedy. But

<meta>catchword: at</meta>

<summary>The author argues that true raillery is as difficult to define as good breeding and is often practiced poorly by "zealots" and "inquisitors." He suggests that these critics of wit use humor awkwardly and would likely prefer to suppress their opponents through "tragedy" rather than debate if given the power.</summary>

<keywords>raillery, good breeding, pedantry, zealots, free-writers, inquisitors, wit, humor, social conduct</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that religious zealots who attempt to use wit and humor in their polemical writings do so awkwardly and hypocritically, often resorting to state authority when their arguments fail.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

<margin>Part I.</margin>
At present, there is nothing so ridiculous as this *Janus-Face* of writers, who with one countenance force a smile, and with another show nothing besides rage and fury. Having entered the lists, and agreed to the fair laws of combat by *wit* and *argument*, they have no sooner tested their weapon than you hear them crying aloud for help and delivering their opponent over to the *secular arm*.

There cannot be a more preposterous sight than an *executioner* and a *Merry-Andrew* <note>A clown or buffoon</note> acting their parts upon the same stage. Yet I am persuaded anyone will find this to be the real *picture* of certain modern *zealots* in their *controversial writings*. They are no more masters of *gravity* than they are of *good humor*. The first always runs into harsh severity, and the latter into an awkward buffoonery. And thus between anger and pleasure, *zeal* and *drollery*, their writing has much the same grace as the play of humorous children, who, at the same instant, are both peevish and wanton, and can laugh and cry almost in one and the same breath.

How agreeable such writings are likely to prove, and what effect they have toward winning over or convincing those who are supposed to be in error, I need not go about to explain. Nor can I wonder, on

on this account, to hear those public lamentations of zealots, that while the books of their adversaries are so current, their answers to them can hardly find their way into the world, or be noticed in the least. *Pedantry* and *Bigotry* are millstones able to sink the best book, which carries even the smallest part of their dead weight. The temper of the pedagogue does not suit the age. And the world, however it may be *taught*, will not be *tutored*. If a *philosopher* speaks, men hear him willingly, while he keeps to his philosophy. So is a *Christian* heard, while he keeps to his professed charity and *meekness*. In a gentleman we allow of pleasantry and raillery, as being managed always with good breeding, and never gross or clownish. But if a mere scholastic, encroaching upon all these characters, and writing as it were by starts and rebounds from one of these to another, appears on the whole as little able to keep the temper of Christianity, as to use the reason of a philosopher, or the raillery of a man of breeding; what wonder is it, if the monstrous product of such a jumbled brain be ridiculous to the world?

If you think (my friend!) that by this description I have done wrong to these zealot-writers in religious controversy;

## S E C T. IV.

BUT now that I have said this much concerning authors and writings, you shall hear my thoughts, as you have desired, upon the subject of *conversation*, and particularly *a recent one* of a free kind, which you remember I was present at, with some friends of yours, whom you imagined I would, in great gravity, have condemned.

IT WAS, I must own, a very diverting one, and perhaps no less so for ending as abruptly as it did, and in such a sort of confusion as almost brought to nothing whatever had been advanced in the discourse before. Some particulars of this conversation may not perhaps be so proper to commit to paper. It is enough that I remind you of the conversation in general. A great many fine schemes, it is true, were destroyed; many grave reasonings overturned: but this being done without offense to the parties concerned, and with improvement to the good humor of the company, it set the appetite the keener for such conversations.

<summary>The author concludes his critique of religious controversy and begins a new section discussing the merits of free, informal conversation. He reflects on a recent gathering where intellectual schemes were dismantled in a way that was both entertaining and improved the company's mood.</summary>
<keywords>conversation, social discourse, religious controversy, schemes, reasoning, wit, humor</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument for free conversation, asserting that reasoning is a habit developed through pleasurable, open discourse rather than formal study.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>of Wit and Humour.</header>

<margin>Sect. 4.</margin>
And I am persuaded, that had *Reason* herself been to judge of her own Interest, she would have thought she received more advantage in the main from that easy and familiar way, than from the usual stiff adherence to a particular opinion.

But perhaps you may still be in the same humor of not believing me in earnest. You may continue to tell me, I affect to be paradoxical, in commending a conversation as advantageous to *Reason*, which ended in such a total uncertainty of what *Reason* had seemingly so well established.

To this I answer, That according to the notion I have of *Reason*, neither the written treatises of the learned, nor the formal discourses of the eloquent, are able of themselves to teach the use of it. It is the habit alone of reasoning, which can make a *Reasoner*. And men can never be better invited to the habit, than when they find pleasure in it. A freedom of raillery, a liberty in decent language to question everything, and an allowance of unraveling or refuting any argument, without offense to the arguer, are the only terms which can render such speculative conversations any way agreeable. For to say truth, they have been rendered burdensome to mankind by the strictness

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the ability to reason is a habit developed through pleasurable and free conversation rather than formal study. He maintains that intellectual growth requires the freedom to use raillery and to question ideas without causing personal offense.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, Reasoner, raillery, free conversation, intellectual habit, speculative discourse, informal education</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of why formal intellectual conversations have become burdensome, blaming the strict rules and the controlling nature of those who lead them.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>An ESSAY on the Freedom</header>

<margin>Part I.</margin>
of the laws prescribed to them, and by the prevailing pedantry and bigotry of those who reign in them, and take it upon themselves to be dictators in these provinces.

---

<margin>*Juvenal, Satires, Book 1.*</margin>
*“Am I always to be only a listener?”* is as natural a case of complaint in divinity, in morals, and in philosophy, as it was of old for *the satirist* in poetry. *Vicissitude* (or alternation) is a mighty law of discourse, and mightily longed for by mankind. In matters of reason, more is done in a minute or two by way of question and reply than by a continued discourse of whole hours. *Orations* are fit only to move the passions; and the power of *declamation* is to terrify, exalt, ravish, or delight, rather than to satisfy or instruct. A free conference is a close fight. The other way, in comparison to it, is merely a brandishing of weapons, or *beating the air*. To be obstructed, therefore, and manacled in conferences, and to be confined to hearing orations on certain subjects, must necessarily give us a distaste and render the subjects so managed as disagreeable as the managers. Men would rather reason upon trifles, provided they may reason freely and without the imposition of authority, than on the most useful and best subjects in the world where they are held under restraint and fear.

---

<summary>The author argues that true reasoning occurs through the back-and-forth of free conversation rather than through one-sided orations. He suggests that the rigid authority and pedantry of formal institutions make serious subjects distasteful, leading people to prefer discussing trifles freely.</summary>
<keywords>discourse, oratory, reasoning, freedom of speech, Juvenal, declamation, authority, pedantry</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the restriction of free thought and serious conversation leads to a decline in reasoning skills and the rise of alternative forms of expression like satire.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>of Wit and Humour.</header>

<margin>
Sect. 4.
</margin>

NOR is it a wonder that men are generally such weak reasoners and care so little to argue strictly on any trivial subject in company, when they dare so little to exert their reason in greater matters and are forced to argue lamely where they have need of the greatest activity and strength. The same thing, therefore, happens here as in strong and healthy bodies which are debarred from their natural exercise and confined in a narrow space. They are forced to use odd gestures and contortions. They have a sort of action and still move, though with the worst grace imaginable. For the animal spirits <note>In older physiology, "animal spirits" were believed to be the vital fluids or forces that animated the nerves and muscles.</note> in such sound and active limbs cannot lie dead or without employment. And thus the natural free spirits of ingenious men, if imprisoned and controlled, will find out other ways of movement to relieve themselves in their *constraint*; and whether it be in burlesque, mimicry, or buffoonery, they will be glad at any rate to vent themselves and be revenged on their *constrainers*.

If men are forbidden from speaking their minds seriously on certain subjects, they will do it ironically. If they are forbidden from speaking at all upon such subjects, or if they find it really dangerous to do so, they will then redouble their disguise, involve
<meta>catchword: them-</meta>

<summary>The author explains that when people are prevented from reasoning freely on serious matters, their intellectual energy is forced into distorted forms like irony, burlesque, and mimicry. He compares this mental restriction to the physical contortions of a healthy body confined in a small space.</summary>
<keywords>reason, constraint, irony, burlesque, buffoonery, animal spirits, intellectual freedom, censorship</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that when people are prevented from expressing their thoughts clearly due to fear of persecution, they resort to indirect forms of expression like raillery and buffoonery.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>An ESSAY on the Freedom</header>

<margin>
Part I.
<image-desc>A horizontal wavy flourish or bracket-like mark.</image-desc>

themselves in mysteriousness, and talk in a way that is hardly understood, or at least not plainly interpreted, by those who are disposed to do them harm. And thus *raillery* is brought more into fashion and runs to an extreme. It is the persecuting spirit that has raised the *bantering* one; and a lack of liberty may account for a lack of true politeness, and for the corruption or wrong use of pleasantry and humor.

If in this respect we strain the just measure of what we call *urbanity*, and are sometimes apt to adopt a buffoonish, rustic air, we may thank the ridiculous solemnity and sour humor of our *pedagogues*: or rather, they may thank themselves if they, in particular, meet with the heaviest of this kind of treatment. For it will naturally fall heaviest where the constraint has been the most severe. The greater the weight is, the bitterer the satire will be. The more intense the slavery, the more exquisite the buffoonery.

That this is really the case may be seen by looking at those countries where spiritual tyranny is highest. For the greatest buffoons are the ITALIANS; and in their writings, in their freer sorts of conversations, on their theaters, and in their streets, buffoonery and burlesque

<meta>catchword: are</meta>

## S E C T. V.

It is for this reason, I truly believe, that the ancients reveal so little of this spirit, and that there is hardly such a thing found as mere *burlesque* in any authors of the more polite ages. Indeed, the manner in which they treated even the gravest subjects was somewhat different from that of our day. Their treatises were generally in a free and familiar style. They chose to give us the representation of real discourse and conversation by treating their subjects in the way of * *dialogue* and free debate. The scene was commonly set at a table, or in the public walks or meeting places; and the usual wit and humor of their real conversations appeared in those of their own composing. And this was fair. For without wit and humor, *reason* can hardly be tested or distinguished. The magisterial voice

And that it is natural for me to defend liberty with this restriction, you may infer from the very notion I have of liberty itself.

It is surely a violation of the freedom of public assemblies for anyone to take the chair who is neither called nor invited to it. To start questions, or manage debates, which offend the public ear, is to be lacking in that respect which is due to common society. account, to be laughed at to its face; or so rebuked for its follies as to make it think itself despised. And what is contrary to good breeding is, in this respect, as contrary to liberty. It belongs to men of slavish principles to affect a superiority over *the vulgar* and to despise *the multitude*. Those who love mankind respect and honor the conventions and societies of men. And in mixed company, and places where people are met indiscriminately on account of diversion or business, it is an imposition and hardship to force them to hear what they dislike, and to treat matters in a language which many who are present have perhaps never been used to. It is a breach of the harmony of public conversation to pitch things in such a key as is above the common reach, which puts others to silence and robs them of their *privilege of taking turns*. But as for private society, and what passes in select companies where friends meet intentionally and with the very design of exercising their wit and looking freely into all subjects, I see no excuse for anyone to be offended at the way of raillery and humor, which is the very life of such conversations; it is the only thing which makes for good company and frees it from the formality of business and the lecturing and dogmatism of the schools.

### S E C T. VI.

TO return therefore to our argument: If the best of our modern conversations are apt to run chiefly upon trifles; if rational discourses (especially those of a deeper speculation) have lost their credit and are in disgrace because of their *formality*, there is reason for more allowance in the way of *humor* and *gaiety*. An easier method of treating these subjects will make them more agreeable and familiar. To dispute about them will be the same as about other matters. They need not spoil good company or take from the ease or pleasure of a polite conversation. And the oftener these conversations are renewed, the better their effect will be. We shall grow better *reasoners* by reasoning pleasantly and at our ease, taking up or laying down these subjects as we fancy. So that, upon the whole, I must admit to you that I cannot be scandalized at the raillery you took notice of, nor at the effect it had upon our company. The humor was agreeable, and the pleasant confusion which the conversation ended in is at this time as pleasant to me upon reflection; when I consider that instead of being discouraged from resuming the debate, we were so much the readier to meet again at any time and dispute upon

<sig>F 2</sig>

<summary>The author argues that treating serious subjects with humor and gaiety makes them more accessible and helps people become better reasoners. He reflects on a recent spirited conversation, noting that its playful nature made the participants more eager to revisit the debate rather than being discouraged by it.</summary>
<keywords>conversation, humor, raillery, reasoning, formality, social interaction, debate</keywords>

WE had been entertained for a long while, you know, on the subjects of *Morality* and *Religion*. And amidst the different opinions started and maintained by several of the parties with great life and ingenuity, one or another would every now and then take the liberty to appeal to COMMON SENSE. Everyone allowed the appeal and was willing to stand the trial. No one was anything but certain that *Common Sense* would justify him. But when issue was joined, and the cause examined at the bar, no judgment could be given. The parties, however, were no less eager to renew their appeal on the very next occasion that presented itself. No one would offer to call the authority of the court into question, until a gentleman, whose good understanding was never yet questioned, very gravely asked the company to tell him what *Common Sense* was.
