“What to one was an inconceivable mystery, to another was of easy comprehension. What to one was absurdity, to another was demonstration.

“As for **POLICY**; what sense or whose could be called common was equally a question. If plain *British* or *Dutch* sense were right, *Turkish* and *French* sense must certainly be very wrong. And as much like mere nonsense as *Passive Obedience* seemed; we found it to be the common sense of a great party among ourselves, a greater party in *Europe*, and perhaps the greatest part of all the world besides.

“As for **MORALS**; the difference, if possible, was still wider. For without considering the opinions and customs of the many barbarous and illiterate nations; we saw that even the few who had attained advanced learning and philosophy could never as yet agree on one and the same system, or acknowledge the same moral principles. And some even of our most admired modern philosophers had fairly told us, that *virtue* and *vice* had, after all, no other *law* or *measure*, than *mere fashion* and *vogue*.”

### Sect. 6.
---

It might have appeared perhaps unfair in our friends, had they treated only the graver subjects in this manner and allowed the lighter ones to escape. For in the gayer part of life, our follies are as solemn as in the most serious. The fault is, we carry the laugh only *half-way*. The false earnest is ridiculed, but the *false jest* passes secure, and becomes as complete a deceit as the other. Our diversions, our plays, our amusements become *solemn*. We dream of happinesses and possessions, and enjoyments in which we have no understanding, no certainty; and yet we pursue these as the best known and most certain things in the world. There is nothing so foolish and deluding as a \* *partial skepticism*. For while the doubt is cast only on one side, the certainty grows so much stronger on the other. While only one face of folly appears ridiculous, the other grows more solemn and deceiving.

But it was not so with our friends. They seemed better *critics*, and more ingenious and fair in their way of questioning received opinions and exposing the ridicule of things. And if you will allow me to carry on their humor, I will venture to make the experiment throughout; and try what certain knowledge or

### SECT. I.

If a native of **ETHIOPIA** were suddenly transported into **EUROPE**, and placed in either **PARIS** or **VENICE** during the **Carnival**, when the general face of mankind was disguised and almost every creature wore a **mask**, it is probable he would for some time be at a loss before he discovered the trick. He would not imagine that a whole people could be so whimsical as to agree, at an appointed time, to transform themselves with a variety of **costumes**, and make it a solemn practice to deceive one another by this universal confusion of characters and persons. Though he might at first perhaps have looked on this with a serious eye, it would be hardly possible for him to keep a straight face once he had per-

It is easy to see which of the two would be ridiculous. For he who laughs, and is himself ridiculous, bears a double share of ridicule. However, should it so happen that, in a fit of ridicule, our **ETHIOPIAN**—having his head still running upon *masks* and knowing nothing of the *fair complexion* and common *dress* of the **EUROPEANS**—should, upon the sight of a natural face and clothing, laugh just as heartily as before, would he not in his turn become ridiculous by carrying the jest too far? This would be the case if, by a silly presumption, he took *nature* for mere *art* and mistook perhaps a man of sobriety and sense for one of those ridiculous *mummers*?

THERE was a time when men were accountable only for their actions and behavior. Their opinions were left to themselves. They had liberty to differ in these, as in their faces. Every one took the air and look which was natural to him. But in process of time, it was thought decent to mend men's countenances, and render their intellectual complexions uniform and of one sort. Thus the magistrate became a *dresser*, and in his turn was *dressed*

But though in this extraordinary juncture it was agreed that there was only one *certain and true dress*, one *single peculiar air*, to which it was necessary all people should conform; yet the misery was that neither the magistrate nor the *tire-men* themselves could resolve which of the various modes was the *exact true one*. Imagine now what the *effect* of this must necessarily be, when men became persecuted thus on every side about their *air* and *feature*, and were put to their shifts how to adjust and compose their *mien* according to the right *mode*; when a thousand models, a thousand patterns of *dress* were current and altered every now and then, upon occasion, according to *fashion* and the *humor* of the times. Judge whether men's countenances were not likely to grow constrained, and the natural visage of mankind, by this habit, distorted, convulsed, and rendered hardly recognizable.

Part 2. The destruction of a similar political body of conjurers, the *Knights Templars*, who had almost become more powerful than the civil sovereign. Your indignation might perhaps have led you to propose the destruction of all monuments and memorials of these magicians. You might have resolved not to leave even their houses standing. But if it had happened that these magicians, during the time of their rule, had made any collection of books or written some themselves, in which they discussed *philosophy*, *morals*, or any other science or branch of *learning*; would you have carried your resentment so far as to have wiped these out as well, and condemned every opinion or doctrine they had supported, for no other reason than merely *because they had supported it?* Hardly a SCYTHIAN, a TARTAR, or a GOTH would act or reason so absurdly. Much less would you (my friend!) have carried on this <term>MAGOPHONY</term>, or *priest-massacre*, with such barbarous zeal. For, in all honesty, to destroy a philosophy out of hatred for a man suggests a notion as errantly *Tartar* as to destroy or murder a man in order to rob him of his wit and inherit his understanding.

And should we have tried to make them see themselves as far more monstrous and corrupt than it was ever possible for the worst of them to become, even with the worst intentions? --- This, you will say, would undoubtedly have been a very ridiculous role to play, and could only have been performed by petty spirits—those who had been held in awe and intimidated † by the MAGI.

AND yet, we know that an ‡ able and witty philosopher of our nation was recently

He did his utmost to show us, “That both in religion and morals we were imposed on by our governors; that there was nothing which by nature inclined us either way; nothing which naturally drew us to the love of what was without, or beyond *ourselves*:” Though the love of such great truths and sovereign maxims as he imagined these to be, made him the most laborious of all men in composing systems of this kind for our use; and forced him, notwithstanding his natural fear, to run continually the highest risk of being a martyr for our deliverance.

GIVE me leave therefore (my friend!) on this occasion, to anticipate your seriousness <note>In this context, "to prevent" or "anticipate" your seriousness means to address your potential concerns before you even raise them.</note>, and assure you, that there is no such mighty danger as we are apt to imagine from these fierce prosecutors of superstition, who are so suspicious of every religious or moral principle. Whatever *savages* they may appear in philosophy, they are in their common capacity as *civil persons*, as pleasant as one can wish. Their free communicating of their principles may witness for them. It is the height of sociableness to be thus friendly and communicative.

<image-desc>A calligraphic decorative flourish line.</image-desc>

IF the principles, indeed, were concealed from us and made a *mystery*, they might become significant. Things are often made so by being kept as *secrets* of a *sect* or *party*; and nothing helps this more than *the antipathy and shyness* of a contrary party. If we immediately fall into horror and consternation upon hearing maxims which are thought *poisonous*, we are in no disposition to use that familiar and easy part of reason, which is the best *antidote*. The only *poison* to reason is *passion*. For false reasoning is soon corrected where passion is removed. But if the *very* hearing of certain propositions of philosophy is enough to move our passion, it is plain that the *poison* has already gained ground on us, and we are effectively prevented from using our reasoning faculty.

WERE it not for prejudices of this kind, what should hinder us from amusing ourselves with the fancy of one of these *modern reformers* we have been speaking of? What should we say to one of these *anti-zealots* who, in the zeal of such a cool philosophy, should faithfully assure us, "That we were the most mistaken men in the world to imagine there was any such thing as natural faith or justice? ## S E C T I O N  II.

In reality (my friend!), a severe brow

sure to preach honesty, and go to church.

On the other hand, the gentlemen for whom I am making a defense cannot, however, be called *hypocrites*. They speak as poorly of themselves as they possibly can. If they have harsh thoughts about human nature, it is still a proof of their humanity that they give such a warning to the world. If they represent men as *treacherous* and *wild* by nature, it is out of care for mankind; lest by being too *tame* and *trusting*, they should easily be caught.

Impostors naturally speak the best of human nature, so that they may more easily abuse it. These gentlemen, on the contrary, speak the worst; they would rather be censured themselves along with the rest than have a *few* prevail over the *many* through deception. For it is the belief in *goodness* * which creates an ease of *trust*: and through *trust* we are betrayed to *power*; our very *reason* being thus captivated by those in whom we gradually come to have an *implicit faith*. But by assuming one another to be such *savages* by nature, we shall take care to place ourselves less in one another's power: and fearing *power* to be *insatiably coveted by everyone*, we shall better guard against the evil; not by putting everything into one hand (as the champion of this

Cause would have us) but, on the contrary, by a right division and balance of power, and by the restraint of good laws and limitations, which may secure the public liberty.

Should you therefore ask me whether I really thought these gentlemen were fully persuaded of the principles they so often advance in company? I should tell you that, although I would not absolutely question the gentlemen's sincerity, there was something of mystery in the case, more than was imagined. The reason, perhaps, why men of wit delight so much to espouse these paradoxical systems is not, in truth, that they are so fully satisfied with them; but with a view to better oppose some other systems which, by their fair appearance, have helped, they think, to bring mankind under subjection. They imagine that by this *general skepticism* which they would introduce, they shall better deal with the dogmatic spirit which prevails in some *particular subjects*. And when they have accustomed men to bear contradiction in the *main*, and hear the nature of things disputed *at large*, it may be safer (they conclude) to argue *separately* upon certain delicate points in which they are not altogether so well satisfied. So that from this, perhaps, you may still better understand why, in conversation,

### SECTION III.

But let whoever will condemn *the humor* thus described; for my part, I have no such fear of this skeptical kind of wit. Indeed, men may, in a serious way, be so manipulated and confounded by different modes of opinion, and different systems and schemes *imposed by authority*, that they may entirely lose any notion or comprehension of *truth*. I can easily understand what effect *awe* has over men's understandings. I can very well imagine that men may be frightened out of their wits; but I have no fear that they should be laughed out of them. I can hardly imagine that, in a pleasant manner, they should ever be talked out of their love for society, or reasoned out of humanity and *common sense*. A polite wit can hurt no cause or interest for which I am in the least concerned; and philosophical speculations, politely managed, surely can never render mankind more unsociable or uncivilized. This is not the direction from which I could possibly expect an invasion of savageness and barbarity. And by my best observation, I have learned that virtue is

He maintains that polite wit and philosophical speculation do not lead to barbarity but rather support a civilized society.</summary>

never such a sufferer from being *contested* as from being *betrayed*. My fear is not so much from its witty *antagonists*, who give it exercise and put it on its defense, as from its tender *nurses*, who are apt to smother it and kill it with an excess of care and cherishing.

I HAVE known a building which, by the over-eagerness of the workmen, has been so *shored up* and *screwed up* on the side where they claimed it was leaning, that it was at last turned the opposite way and overthrown. Something of this kind has perhaps happened in *morals*. Men have not been content to show the natural advantages of honesty and virtue. They have instead diminished these, the better—as they thought—to promote another foundation. They have made *virtue* such a mercenary thing, and have talked so much of its *rewards*, that one can hardly tell what there is in it, after all, that could be worth rewarding. For to be only bribed or terrified into an honest practice shows little real honesty or worth. We may, it is true, make whatever *bargain* we think fit, and may bestow as a favor whatever surplus we please. But there can be no excellence or wisdom in voluntarily rewarding what is neither estimable nor deserving. And if virtue is not really estimable

If the love of doing good is not, of itself, a *good* and *right* inclination, I know not how there can possibly be such a thing as *goodness* or *virtue*. If the inclination is *right*, it is a perverting of it to apply it solely to the *reward*, and make us conceive such wonders of the grace and favor which is to attend virtue, when there is so little shown of the intrinsic worth or value of the thing itself.

I could be almost tempted to think that the true reason why some of the most heroic virtues have so little notice taken of them in our holy religion is because there would have been no room left for *disinterestedness*, had they been entitled to a share of that infinite reward which Providence has, by revelation, assigned to other duties. * *Private friendship* and

### Sect. 3.
---

It indeed appears under the *Jewish* dispensation that each of these virtues had its illustrious examples, and was in some manner recommended to us as honorable and worthy of our imitation. Even SAUL himself, as bad a prince as he is represented, appears both in life and in death to have been respected and praised for the love he bore for his native country. And the love which was so remarkable between his son and his successor gives us a noble view of a disinterested friendship, at least on *one side*. But the heroic virtue of these persons had only the common reward of praise attributed to it, and could not claim a future recompense under a religion which taught no future state, nor exhibited any rewards or punishments besides those that were temporal and related to the written law.

And thus the *Jews*, as well as *heathens*, were left to their philosophy to be instructed in the sublime part of virtue, and induced by reason to that which was never enjoined on them by command. No reward or penalty being enforced in these cases, the disinterested part remained, the virtue was a *free choice*, and the magnanimity of the act was left entire. He who would be generous had the means. He who would freely serve his friend, or coun-

He cites the examples of Saul’s patriotism and the friendship between Jonathan and David to illustrate these disinterested virtues.</summary>
<keywords>Jewish dispensation, Saul, Jonathan, David, patriotism, disinterested friendship, future state, temporal rewards, virtue, philosophy, magnanimity, free choice</keywords>

# PART III.

---

## SECT. I.

THE Roman *Satirist* may be thought more than ordinarily satirical when, speaking of the *Nobility* and the *Court*, he is so far from allowing them to be the standard of *politeness* and *good sense* that he makes them, in a sense, the *reverse*.

> \* *Rarus enim fermè Sensus communis in illâ*
> *Fortunâ —*

Some of the † most ingenious commentators, however, interpret this very differently from how it is generally understood.

</margin>

They interpret this *Common Sense* of the poet, by a *Greek* derivation, to signify a *sense* of the *public welfare*, and of the *common interest*; a *love of the community* or *society*, natural *affection*, *humanity*, *obligingness*, or that sort of *civility* which rises from a just *sense* of the *common rights* of mankind, and the *natural equality* that exists among those of the same species.

AND indeed, if we consider the matter closely, it must seem somewhat harsh in the poet to have denied *wit* or *ability* to a court

> "...he consults, in a certain way, for the common good, and does not refer everything to his own advantage; he also has respect for those with whom he associates, thinking modestly and moderately of himself. But on the contrary, all those who are conceited and proud think themselves born only for themselves and their own interests, and they despise and neglect all others compared to themselves; and these are the ones who can rightly be said not to have *Common Sense*. For *Juvenal* understands *Common Sense* in this way, Satire 8: *For rarely indeed is there* COMMON SENSE, *etc.* *Galen* calls it *philanthropy* and *kindness*, which *Marcus*, speaking of himself, calls *Koinonoemosyne* <note>Social-mindedness or a sense of community</note>; and elsewhere, where he speaks of the same thing, he calls it *moderation* and *courtesy*, by which *Marcus* would have excused him from going to the *Germanic War* and following him." In the same manner *Isaac Casaubon* says: *Herodian* calls this *the moderate and the equal*. "But *Antoninus* adds, as if interpreting this word, *and to allow his friends neither to dine with him in every case, nor to travel with him by necessity*." This, I am persuaded, is the *Sensus Communis* of HORACE (Satire 3, book 1) which has been unobserved (as far as I can learn) by any of his commentators: it being remarkable, moreover, that in this early satire of HORACE, before his later days, and when his philosophy as yet inclined to the less rigid defenders of virtue, he puts this expression (as may be seen by the whole satire taken together) into the mouth of a *Crispinus*, or some ridiculous mimic of that severe philosophy to which the coinage of the word *Koinonoemosyne* properly belonged.

For so the poet again (*Sat.* 4. *v.* 77.) uses the word *SENSUS*, speaking of those who without *Sense of Manners*, or common society, without the least respect or deference to others, press rudely upon their friends, and upon all company in general, without regard to time or place, or anything besides their selfish and brutish humor:

as old *Lambin* interprets it, though without any other explanation; referring only to the *Sensus Communis* of Horace in that other satire. Thus Seneca (*Epist.* 105.) *You will avoid hatred resulting from offense by provoking no one for no reason; from which COMMON SENSE will protect you.* And Cicero accordingly, *It is the role of justice not to violate men; and of modesty, not to offend them.* Lib. 1. *De Officiis*. It may be objected possibly by some, particularly those versed in the philosophy mentioned above, that the *Koinos Nous* <note>Greek: Κοινὸς Νοῦς, common mind</note>, to which the *Koinonoemosyne* <note>Greek: Κοινονοημοσύνη, social-mindedness</note> seems to have relation, has a different meaning. But they will consider at the same time how small the distinction was in that philosophy between the *hypolepsis* <note>Greek: ὑπόληψις, conception or assumption</note> and the common *aisthesis* <note>Greek: αἴσθησις, perception</note>; how generally *Passion* was by those philosophers brought under the head of *Opinion*. And when they consider, besides this, the very formation of the word *Koinonoemosyne* upon the model of the other feminized virtues, the *Eugnomosyne* <note>goodwill/judgment</note>, *Sophrosyne* <note>temperance</note>, *Dikaiosyne* <note>justice</note>, etc., they will no longer hesitate on this interpretation. —— The reader may perhaps by this note see better why the Latin title of *Sensus Communis* has been given to this second treatise. He may observe, furthermore, how the same poet Juvenal uses the word *Sensus* in Satire 15: *This is the best part of our sense.*

Our poet, therefore, does not seem so immoderate in his censure, if we consider that it is the *heart*, rather than the *head*, he takes to task. When reflecting on a *court education*, he thinks it unsuited to raise any affection toward a *country*; and he looks upon young princes and lords as *the young masters of the world*, who, being indulged in all their passions and trained up in all manner of licentiousness, have that thorough contempt and disregard for mankind which mankind in a way deserves, where arbitrary power is permitted and a tyranny adored.

Now, there are none so far from being *partners* in this *sense*, or sharers in this *common affection*, as those who scarcely know an *equal*, nor consider themselves subject to any law of *fellowship* or *community*. is no real love of virtue, without the <margin>Section 1.</margin> knowledge of *public good*. And where absolute power is, there is no **PUBLIC**.

THOSE who live under a *tyranny*, and have learned to admire its power as *sacred* and *divine*, are corrupted as much in their religion as in their morals. *Public good*, according to their understanding, is as little the measure or rule of government in *the universe* as it is in *the state*. They have scarcely a notion of what is good or just, other than what mere *will* and *power* have determined. Omnipotence, they think, would hardly be itself were it not at liberty to <note>See below, page 298.</note> dispense with the laws of equity and change at pleasure the standard of moral rectitude.

BUT notwithstanding prejudices and corruptions of this kind, it is plain there is still something of a *public principle*, even where it is most perverted and depressed. The worst of *magistracies*, *the mere despotic kind*, can show sufficient instances of zeal and affection toward it. Where no other government is known, it seldom fails to have that allegiance and duty paid to it which is owed to a better form. The *Eastern countries*, and many barbarous nations, have been and still are examples of this kind. The personal love they bear their prince, however severe

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 107, arguing that the human impulse for order leads people to support even tyrants in the absence of a proper state, then contrasts this with the British constitutional model.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>

<margin>Part 3.</margin> towards them, may show how natural an affection there is towards government and order among mankind. If men *really* have no public parent, no magistrate in common to cherish and protect them, they will still *imagine* they have such a one; and, like newborn creatures who have never seen their mother, will fancy one for themselves, and apply (as prompted by nature) to some similar form for favor and protection. In the place of a *true foster-father and chief*, they will turn to a *false one*; and in the place of a *legal government* and *just prince*, obey even a *tyrant*, and endure a whole lineage and succession of such.

As for us **BRITONS**, thank Heaven, we have a better *sense* of government delivered to us from our ancestors. We have the notion of a **PUBLIC** and a **CONSTITUTION**; how a *legislative* and how *an executive* is modeled. We understand weight and measure of this kind, and can reason justly on the *balance of power* and *property*. The maxims we draw from here are as evident as those in *mathematics*. Our increasing knowledge shows us every day, more and more, what **COMMON SENSE** is in politics: and this must of necessity lead us to understand a similar *sense* in morals, which is the foundation.

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous discussion on government, moving from the specific British constitutional model to a broader philosophical critique of the "State of Nature" and the origins of moral obligation.</meta>

It is ridiculous to say there is any obligation on man to act sociably, or honestly, in a formed government; and not in that which is commonly called the *State of Nature*. For, to speak in the fashionable language of our modern philosophy:
> "Society being founded on a compact; the surrender made of every man's private unlimited right, into the hands of the majority, or such as the majority should appoint, was of free choice, and by a promise."

Now the *promise* itself was made in the *State of Nature*: And that which could make a *promise* obligatory in the State of Nature, must make *all other acts* of humanity just as much our real duty, and natural part. Thus *faith*, *justice*, *honesty*, and *virtue*, must have been as early as the State of Nature, or they could never have been *at all*. The civil union, or confederacy, could never make *right* or *wrong*, if they did not exist before. He who was free to commit any villainy before his contract, will, and ought to, make as free with his contract when he thinks fit. The *natural knave* has the same reason to be a *civil one*; and may dispense with his political capacity as often as he sees occasion: it is only *his word* that stands in his way. — A man is obliged to keep *his word*. Why? Because *he has given*

## S E C T. II.

BUT to pass by these cavils of a philosophy which speaks so much of *nature* with so little meaning; we may with justice surely establish it as a principle,
> “That if anything is *natural* in any creature, or any kind, it is that which is *preservative* of the kind itself and conducive to its welfare and support.”

If in original and pure nature it is wrong to break a promise or be treacherous, it is just as truly wrong to be in any respect inhuman, or in any way lacking in our natural role toward humankind. If *eating* and *drinking* are natural, *herding* is so too. If any *appetite* or *sense* is natural, the *sense of fellowship* is the same. If there is anything of nature in that *affection* which exists between the sexes, the *affection* is certainly as natural toward the resulting offspring; and so again among the offspring themselves, as kindred and companions, raised under the same discipline and economy. And thus a *clan* or *tribe* is gradually formed; a *public* is recognized; and besides the pleasure found in social entertainment, language, and dis-

<summary>The author argues that social instincts, such as fellowship and family affection, are just as natural to humans as the biological needs for food and drink. He suggests that these inherent social bonds naturally lead to the formation of tribes and the recognition of a public society.</summary>
<keywords>moral justice, civil government, state of nature, fellowship, social instinct, clan, tribe, natural law</keywords>

course, there is so apparent a necessity for continuing this good correspondence and union, that to have no *sense* or feeling of this kind, no *love of country*, *community*, or anything in common, would be the same as to be insensible even of the plainest means of *self-preservation*, and the most necessary condition of *self-enjoyment*.

How the wit of man should so puzzle this cause, as to make *civil government* and *society* appear a kind of invention, and creature of art, I know not. For my own part, it seems to me, this *herding principle*, and *associating inclination*, is seen so natural and strong in most men, that one might readily affirm, it was even from the violence of this passion that so much disorder arose in the general society of mankind.

**UNIVERSAL** good, or the interest of *the world in general*, is a kind of remote philosophical object. That *greater community* falls not easily under the eye. Nor is a national interest, or that of a whole people, or body politic, so readily apprehended. In smaller parties, men may be intimately conversant and acquainted with one another. They can there better taste society, and enjoy the *common good* and interest of a more contracted public. They view the whole compass and extent

of their community; and see, and know particularly whom they serve, and to what end they *associate* and *conspire*. All men naturally have their share of this *combining* principle: and those who possess the sprightliest and most active faculties have so large a share of it that, unless it is happily directed by right reason, it can never find exercise for itself in so remote a sphere as that of the *body politic* at large. For here, perhaps, not a thousandth part of those whose interests are concerned are scarcely even known by sight. No visible band is formed; no strict alliance: but the conjunction is made with different persons, orders, and ranks of men; not perceptibly, but *in idea*; according to that general view or notion of a *state* or *commonwealth*.

THUS the social aim is disturbed for want of a certain scope. The *close sympathy and conspiring virtue* is apt to lose itself, for want of direction, in so wide a field. Nor is the passion anywhere so strongly felt or vigorously exerted as in actual *conspiracy* or *war*; in which the highest geniuses are often known to be the most forward in employing themselves. For the most generous spirits are the most combining. They delight most to move in concert; and *feel* (if I may say so) in the strongest manner the force of the *confederating charm*.

### Sect. 2.
<image-desc>A decorative wavy line flourish.</image-desc>

It is strange to imagine that *War*, which of all things appears the most savage, should be the passion of the most heroic spirits. But it is in *War* that the knot of *fellowship* is closest drawn. It is in *War* that mutual help is most given, mutual danger run, and common affection most exerted and employed. For *heroism* and *philanthropy* are almost one and the same. Yet by a small misguidance of the affection, a lover of mankind becomes a ravager; a hero and deliverer becomes an oppressor and destroyer.

HENCE arise other divisions among men. Hence, in the way of peace and civil government, comes that *love of party*, and subdivision by *cabal*. For sedition is a kind of cantonizing <note>To "cantonize" is to divide a territory or group into smaller, often conflicting, districts or factions.</note> already begun within the state. To cantonize is natural when the society grows vast and bulky; and powerful states have found other advantages in sending colonies abroad than merely that of having elbow room at home, or extending their dominion into distant countries. Vast empires are in many respects unnatural, but particularly in this: that however well they are constituted, the affairs of many must, in such governments, depend upon a very few; and the relationship becomes less perceptible, and in a manner lost, between the magistrate and people, in a body so un-

<margin>Part 3. <image-desc>decorative flourish squiggle</image-desc></margin>wieldy in its limbs, and whose members lie so remote from one another and distant from the head.

It is in such bodies as these that strong factions are most likely to develop. The social spirits, for lack of exercise, form new movements and seek a narrower sphere of activity when they lack *action* in a larger one. Thus we have *wheels within wheels*. And in some national constitutions (notwithstanding the absurdity in politics), we have *one empire within another*. Nothing is so delightful as to form associations. *Distinctions* of many kinds are invented. *Religious societies* are formed. *Orders* are established; and their interests are embraced and served with the utmost zeal and passion. Founders and patrons of this kind are never lacking. Wonders are performed in this misguided social spirit by those members of separate societies. And the *social genius* of man is never better proven than in those very societies which are formed in opposition to the general community of mankind and to the real interest of the state.

In short, the very spirit of *faction* seems, for the most part, to be nothing other than the abuse or irregularity of that *social love* and *common affection* which is natural to mankind. For the opposite

You have heard it (my friend!) as a common saying, that *interest* governs the *world*. But I believe whoever looks narrowly into its affairs will find that *passion, humor, caprice, zeal, faction*, and a thousand other springs, which run counter to *self-interest*, play as significant a part in the movements of this machine. There are more wheels and *counterweights* in this engine than are easily imagined. It is of too complex a kind to fall under one simple view, or be explained thus briefly in a word or two. Those who study this *mechanism* must have a very partial eye to overlook all other motions besides those of the lowest and narrowest compass. It is hard that in the plan or description of this clockwork, no wheel or balance should be allowed for the better and more enlarged affections; that nothing should be understood to be

THERE have been in all times a sort of narrow-minded philosophers, who have thought to set this difference right, by conquering *nature* in themselves. An original father and founder among these saw well this power of *nature*, and understood it so far, that he earnestly exhorted his followers neither to beget children nor serve their country. There was no dealing with nature, it seems, while these alluring objects stood in the way. *Relations*, *friends*, *countrymen*, *laws*, *political constitutions*, *the beauty of order and government*, *and the interest of society and mankind*, were objects which, he clearly saw, would *naturally* raise a stronger affection than any which was grounded upon the narrow foundation of mere SELF. His advice, therefore, not to marry, nor engage at all in the public, was wise and suitable to his design. There was no way to be truly a disciple of this philosophy but to leave family, friends, country, and society, to adhere to it. —— And, in

Thus *love of one's country*, and *love of mankind*, must also be *self-love*. *Magnanimity* and *courage*, no doubt, are modifications of this universal *self-love*! For \* *courage* (says our modern philosopher) is *constant anger*. And all men (says † a witty poet) *would be cowards if they dared*.

THAT the poet and the philosopher were both *cowards* may perhaps be granted without dispute. They may have spoken to the best of their knowledge. But as for *true courage*, it has so little to do with *anger* that there is always the strongest suspicion against it where this passion is highest. *True courage* is *cool* and *calm*. The bravest of men have the least amount of brutal, bullying insolence; and in the very time of danger, they are found to be the most serene, pleasant, and free. Rage, we know, can make a coward forget himself and fight. But what is done in *fury* or *anger* can never be credited to *courage*. Were it otherwise, women might claim to be the *stoutest* sex; for their hatred and anger have always been acknowledged as the strongest and most lasting.

OTHER authors have existed of an even more inferior kind: a sort of *distributors* and petty *retailers* of this *wit*, who have played endless *variations* and *divisions* upon this topic of *self-love*. You see the very same thought spun out a hundred ways and drawn into mottoes and devices to express this riddle: that "no matter how disinterestedly or generously you please to act, *self* is still at the bottom, and nothing else." Now, if these gentlemen, who delight so much in the play of words but are cautious about grappling closely with definitions, would only tell us † what *self-interest* was, and define *happiness* and *good*, there would be an end to this enigmatic wit. For in this we would all agree: that *happiness* was to be pursued and, in fact, was always sought after. But whether it is found in *following nature* and giving way to *common affection*, or in suppressing it and turning every passion toward *private advantage*—

advantage, a narrow *self-end*, or the preservation of *mere life*; this would be the matter in debate between us. The question would not be, "Who *loved* himself, or who *not*;" but "Who loved and served himself the *best*, and in the truest manner."

It is the height of wisdom, no doubt, to be rightly *selfish*. And to value *life*, as far as life is good, belongs as much to courage as to discretion. But a wretched life is no wise man’s wish. To be without *honesty* is, in effect, to be without *natural affection* or *sociability* of any kind. And a life without *natural affection*, *friendship*, or *sociability* would be found a wretched one, if it were to be tried. It is as these feelings and affections are intrinsically valuable and worthy that *self-interest* is to be rated and esteemed. A man is defined by nothing so much as his *temper* and the *character* of his *passions and affections*. If he loses what is manly and worthy in these, he is as much lost to himself as when he loses his memory and understanding. The least step into villainy or baseness changes the character and value of a life. He who would preserve life at any cost must abuse *himself* more than anyone else can abuse him. And if life is not a precious thing indeed, he who has refused to live as a villain, and has

### S E C T. IV.

It is well for you (my friend!) that in your education you have had little to do with the * *philosophy* or *philosophers* of our days. A good poet and an honest historian may provide learning enough for *a gentleman*. And such a person, while he reads these authors for his diversion, will have a truer relish for their meaning and understand them better than a *pedant*, despite all his labors and the assistance of his volumes of commentators. I am aware that in the past it was the custom to send the youth of highest quality to *philosophers* to be formed. It was in their schools, in their company, and by their precepts and example that the illustrious pupils were inured to hardship and exercised in the severest courses of temperance and self-denial. By such early discipline, they were fitted for the command of others: to maintain their country's honor in war, rule wisely in the state, and fight against luxury and corruption in times of prosperity and peace. # P A R T IV.
---
## S E C T. I.

<margin>Part 4.</margin>
By this time (my friend!) you may possibly, I hope, be satisfied that as I am in earnest in defending *raillery*, so I can be sober too in the use of it. It is in reality a serious study to learn to temper and regulate that *humor* which Nature has given us as a more soothing remedy against vice, and a kind of specific <note>A specific remedy or medicine.</note> against superstition and melancholy delusion. There is a great difference between seeking how to raise a laugh from everything and seeking, in everything, what justly may be laughed at. For nothing is ridiculous except what is deformed; nor is anything proof against *raillery* except what is handsome and just. And therefore it is the hardest thing in the world to deny *fair* honesty the use of this weapon, which can never bear an edge against herself, and bears against everything contrary.

<summary>The author argues that raillery is a serious tool for regulating humor and serves as a remedy against vice, superstition, and delusion. He asserts that only what is deformed or unjust is truly susceptible to ridicule, while honesty and justice are immune to its edge.</summary>

<keywords>raillery, humor, vice, superstition, honesty, ridicule, moral philosophy, Nature, truth</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of ridicule, specifically focusing on how it applies to vice rather than virtue, asserting that true virtue and good breeding are inherently resistant to being made a jest.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>of Wit and Humour.</header>
<sig>I 4</sig>

### Sect. 1.

<image-desc>Horizontal calligraphic flourish</image-desc>

If even the *Italian Buffoons* were to give us the rule in these cases, we should learn from them that in their lowest and most scurrilous way of wit, there was nothing so successfully mocked as the passions of cowardice and avarice. One may defy the world to turn real *bravery* or *generosity* into ridicule. A glutton or mere *sensualist* is as ridiculous as the other two characters. Nor can genuine *temperance* be made a subject of contempt to anyone except the grossest and most contemptible of mankind. Now these *three* ingredients make up a virtuous character, just as the opposite three make up a vicious one. How, therefore, can we possibly make a jest of honesty? To laugh *both* ways is nonsensical. And if ridicule is directed at *sottishness*, *avarice*, and *cowardice*, you see the consequence. A man must be utterly ridiculous who, with all the wit imaginable, would attempt to ridicule *wisdom*, or laugh at honesty or good manners.

A man of thorough * *good breeding*, whatever else he may be, is incapable of doing a rude or brutal action. He never deliberates in this case, or considers the matter by prudential rules of self-interest and advantage. He acts from his nature, in a manner necessarily, and with—

---

<note>* Vol. III. p. 161, 162.</note>

<summary>The author argues that true virtues like bravery and temperance are immune to ridicule, whereas vices like cowardice and avarice are its natural targets. He also observes that a well-bred person acts virtuously by nature rather than through calculated self-interest.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that a well-bred man acts virtuously by nature. The text picks up from the previous page's discussion on acting without deliberation or narrow self-interest.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>An ESSAY on the Freedom</header>

...out reflection. And if he did not, it would be impossible for him to live up to his character, or be found to be that truly well-bred man on every occasion. It is the same with the *honest man*. He cannot deliberate in the *case* of a plain villainy. *A Plum* <note>A contemporary slang term for a large sum of money, often £100,000, typically acquired through corruption.</note> is no temptation to him. He likes and loves himself too well to trade hearts with one of those corrupt miscreants who, among themselves, gave that name to a large sum of *money* gained by the rapine and plunder of the commonwealth. He who would enjoy a *freedom of mind*, and be truly *possessor of himself*, must be above the thought of stooping to what is villainous or base. He, on the other side, who has a heart to stoop, must necessarily give up the thought of *manliness, resolution, friendship, merit, and a reputation with himself and others*. But to desire these enjoyments and advantages, together with the privileges of a licentious principle; to pretend to enjoy society and a *free mind* in the company of a *knavish heart*, is as ridiculous as the way of children who eat their cake and afterwards cry for it. When men begin to *deliberate* about dishonesty, and—finding it goes less against their stomach—ask slyly, "Why should they hesitate at a *good piece of knavery* for a *good sum?*" they should be told, as children, that *they cannot eat their cake and have it too.*

<summary>The author argues that true honesty and self-possession require an instinctive rejection of villainy, as one cannot maintain a virtuous character or mental freedom while indulging in corruption. He compares those who attempt to balance a dishonest heart with a desire for social merit to children who wish to both eat and keep their cake.</summary>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that true happiness requires consistency in character, contrasting the "accomplished knave" and the "honest man" against those who are wavering and inconsistent.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>of Wit and Humour. Sect. 1.</header>

When men, indeed, have become *accomplished knaves*, they are past *crying for their cake*. They *know themselves*, and are *known* by mankind. It is not *these* who are so much envied or admired. The *moderate* kind are more appealing to us. Yet if we had sense, we should consider that it is in reality the *thoroughly profligate knave*, the very *complete unnatural villain* alone, who *can in any way strive for happiness* alongside the *honest man*. True interest is wholly on *one side or the other*. All in between is inconsistency, irresolution, remorse, vexation, and a fever-fit: from hot to cold; from one passion to another quite contrary; a perpetual discord of life; and an alternating disquiet and self-dislike. The only rest or repose must be through *one* determined, deliberate resolution: which, once taken, must be courageously kept, and the passions and affections brought under obedience to it; the temperament steeled and hardened to the mind; the disposition to the judgment. Both must agree; otherwise, all must be disturbance and confusion. So that to think with

---

<note>Our author's *French* translator aptly cites, on this occasion, these verses from Horace, *Satires*, Book 2, Satire 7.

> ——————*Quanto constantior idem*
> *In vitiis, tanto leviùs miser, ac prior illo*
> *Qui jam contento, jam laxo fune laborat.*</note>

For an ordinary honest man, while left to himself, and undisturbed by philosophy and subtle reasonings about his interest, gives no other answer to the thought of villainy, than that *he cannot possibly find it in his heart to set about it, or conquer the natural aversion he has to it.* And this is *natural and just.*

THE truth is; as notions stand now in the world, with respect to morals, honesty is likely to gain little by philosophy, or deep speculations of any kind. In the main, it is best to stick to *Common Sense*, and go no further. Men’s first thoughts, in this matter, are generally better than their second: their natural notions better than those refined by study, or consultation with <term>casuists</term>. According to common speech, as well as common sense, *Honesty is the best policy*: But according to refined sense, the only *well-advised* persons, as to this world, are *arrant knaves*; and they alone are thought to serve themselves, who serve their passions, and indulge their loosest appetites and desires. ——— Such, it seems, are the *wise*, and such the *wisdom of this world!*

AN ordinary man talking of a vile action, in a way of *Common Sense*, says naturally and heartily, "He would not be guilty of such a thing for the whole world." But *speculative men* find many modifications in the case; many ways of evasion; many remedies; many alleviations. A good gift *rightly applied*; a *right* method of suing out a pardon; good almshouses and charitable foundations erected for *right worshippers*; and a good zeal shown for the *right belief* may sufficiently atone for *one wrong practice*, especially when it is such as raises a man to a considerable power (as they say) of *doing good and serving the true cause*.

MANY a good estate, many a high station has been gained upon such a basis as this. Some *crowns* too may have been purchased on these terms; and there have been some great * emperors of old (if I mistake not) who were much assisted by these or similar principles and, in return, were not ungrateful to the cause and party which had assisted them. **THE** admirers of beauty in the fair sex would laugh, perhaps, to hear of a *moral part* in their *amours*. Yet, what a fuss is made about a *heart*! What a careful search for *sentiments* and *tender thoughts*! What praises of a *humour*, a *sense*, a *je ne sais quoi* of *wit*, and all those *graces of a mind* which these *virtuoso lovers* delight to celebrate! Let them settle this matter among themselves and regulate, as they think fit, the proportions which these different *beauties* hold to one another; they must still allow there is a *beauty of the mind*, and one that is essential to the case. Why else is the very *air of foolishness* enough to repel a lover at first sight? If the subject were well examined, we would find, perhaps, that what we most admired, even in the shape of *outward* features, was only a mysterious expression and a kind of shadow of something *internal* in the temperament. When we were struck by a *majestic* air, a *lively* look, a bold *Amazonian* grace, or a contrary *soft* and *gentle* one, it was chiefly the imagination of these characters or qualities which worked upon us. Our imagination is busy forming beautiful shapes and images of this intellectual kind, which entertained the mind and held it in admiration, while other passions of a lower species were employed in another way. The preliminary advances, the declarations, the explanations, confidences, and clarifications; the dependence on something mutual, something felt by way of return; the *Spes animi credula mutui* <note>Latin: "The trustful hope of a mutual soul."</note>: all these become necessary ingredients in the affair of love and are officially established by the men of elegance and art in this realm of passion.

Nor can men of cooler passions and more deliberate pursuits withstand the force of *beauty* in other subjects. Decorum of things will force its way.

### Sect. 2.

Those who refuse to give it scope in the nobler subjects of a rational and moral kind will find its prevalence elsewhere, in an *inferior order of things*. Those who overlook the *main* springs of action and despise the thought of numbers and proportion in a *life at large* will, in the trivial *particulars* of it, be no less occupied and engaged; whether in the study of common arts or in the care and cultivation of mere mechanical beauties. The models of houses, buildings, and their accompanying ornaments; the plans of gardens and their sections; the arrangement of walks, plantations, avenues; and a thousand other symmetries will take the place of that happier and higher symmetry and order of a mind. The † *species of the fair*, *noble*, and *handsome* will reveal itself on a thousand occasions and in a thousand subjects. The *specter* will still haunt us in some shape or other; and when driven from our cool thoughts and frightened from the *closet* <note>A private room for study or meditation.</note>, it will meet us even *at court* and fill our heads with dreams of grandeur, titles, honors, and a false magnificence and beauty, to which we are ready to sacrifice our highest pleasure and ease, and for the sake of which we become the merest drudges and most abject slaves.

The men of pleasure, who seem the greatest despisers of this philosophical beauty, are often forced to confess her charms. They can commend *honesty* as heartily as others and are just as much struck with the beauty of a *generous action*. They admire the thing itself, though not the means. And, if possible, they would order things so as to make integrity and luxury agree. But the rules of harmony will not permit it. The dissonances are too strong. However, attempts of this kind are not unpleasant to observe. For though some of the voluptuous are found to be sordid advocates for baseness and corruption of every sort, yet others, more generous, endeavor to maintain a standard of honesty; and understanding pleasure better, they are for bringing it under some rule. They condemn *this manner*; they praise *the other*. “So far was *right*; but further, *wrong*. Such a case was allowable; but such a one was not to be admitted.” They introduce a *justice* and an *order* in their pleasures. They would bring *reason* over to their side, account in some manner for their lives, and form themselves to some kind of consistency and agreement. Or should they find this impracticable on certain terms, they would choose to sacrifice their other pleasures to those which arise from a generous behavior, a regu-

### S E C T. III.

AND thus, after all, the most natural beauty in the world is *honesty* and *moral truth*. For all *beauty* is TRUTH. *True features* make the beauty of a face, and true proportions make the beauty of architecture, just as *true measures* make that of harmony and music. In poetry, which is all fiction, *truth* is still the perfection. * The <term>τὸ Ευσύνοπτον</term>; as the great Master of Arts calls it in his *Poetics*, chapter 23, but particularly chapter 7, where he shows, "That <term>τὸ Καλὸν</term>, the Beautiful, or the Sublime, in these aforementioned arts, arises from the expression of *Greatness* with *Order*: that is to say, exhibiting the *Principal* or *Main* of what is designed in the very largest proportions in which it is capable of being viewed. For when it is gigantic, it is in a manner out of sight and can be no way comprehended in that simple and *united View*. As, on the contrary, when a piece is of the miniature kind—when it runs into the *Detail* and precise delineation of every little particular—it is, as it were, invisible for the same reason: because the *summary Beauty*, the *WHOLE itself*, cannot be comprehended in that ONE *united View*, which is broken and lost by the necessary attraction of the eye to every small and subordinate part. In a poetic system, the same regard must be had to the memory as in painting to the eye. The dramatic kind is confined within the convenient and proper time of a spectacle. The epic is left more at large. Each work, however, must aim at *Vastness* and be as *great* and of as long a duration as possible, but so as to be comprehended (as to the main of it) by one easy *Glance* or *Retrospect* of memory. And this the philosopher calls, accordingly, the <term>τὸ Εὐμνημόνευτον</term>." I cannot better translate the passage than as I have done in these explanatory lines. For besides what relates to mere art, the

Now the variety of nature is such as to distinguish everything she forms by a *peculiar original character*; which, if strictly observed, will make the subject appear unlike anything else existing in the world. But the good poet and painter diligently seek to prevent this effect. They hate *minuteness* and are afraid of *singularity*, which would make their images or characters appear capricious and fantastical. The mere face-painter, indeed, has little in common with the poet; but, like the mere historian,

---

philosophical sense of the original is so majestic, and the whole treatise so masterly, that when I find even the *Latin* interpreters fall so short, I would be presumptuous to attempt anything in our own language. I would only add a small remark of my own, which may perhaps be noticed by the students of sculpture and painting: that the greatest of the ancient as well as modern artists were always inclined to follow this rule of the philosopher; and when they erred in their *designs* or *sketches*, it was on the side of *greatness*, by running into the oversized and gigantic, rather than into the minute and delicate. Of this, Michelangelo, the great beginner and founder among the moderns, and Zeuxis, the same among the ancients, may serve as instances. See Pliny, *Book* 35, *Chapter* 9 concerning Zeuxis, and the notes of Father Hardouin in his edition *in usum Delphini*, p. 200, on the words, *Deprehenditur tamen Zeuxis, etc.* And again Pliny himself upon Euphranor, in the same book, *Chapter* 11, p. 226. *Docilis, ac laboriosus, ante omnes, & in quocumque genere excellens, ac sibi æqualis. Hic primus videtur expressisse Dignitates Heroum, & usurpasse Symmetriam. Sed fuit universitate corporum exilior, capitibus articulisque grandior. Volumina quoque composuit de Symmetriâ & Coloribus, etc.* See *below*, p. 340, 341, 342 in the notes.

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 144 regarding how a "mere face-painter" or historian focuses on singular details, whereas a true artist seeks general truths.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>Of Wit and Humour.</header>

<margin>Section 3.</margin>
copies what he sees, and minutely traces every feature and odd mark. It is otherwise with the men of invention and design. It is from the *many objects* of nature, and not from *a particular one*, that those geniuses form the idea of their work. Thus, the best artists are said to have been indefatigable in studying the best statues, as they esteemed them a better rule than the most perfect human bodies could afford. And thus, some considerable wits have recommended the best poems as preferable to the best of histories, as better teaching the *truth* of characters and the nature of mankind.

Nor can this criticism be thought far-fetched. Though few confine themselves to these rules, few are insensitive to them. Whatever leniency we may give to our flawed poets, or other composers of irregular and short-lived works, we know very well that the lasting pieces of good artists must be formed in a more uniform way. Every just work of theirs comes under those natural rules of proportion and *truth*. The creature of their brain must be like one of nature's formations. It must have a body and parts

Determination of what commentators, translators, paraphrasts, grammarians, and others have, on this occasion, delivered to us; in the midst of such variety of style, such different readings, such interpolations and corruptions in the originals; such mistakes of copyists, transcribers, editors, and a hundred such accidents to which ancient books are subject: it becomes, upon the whole, a *matter of delicate speculation*; considering, moreover, that the reader, though an able linguist, must be supported by so many other helps from chronology, natural philosophy, geography, and other sciences.

And thus many previous *truths* are to be examined and understood in order to judge rightly of *historical truth*, and of the past actions and circumstances of mankind, as delivered to us by ancient authors of different nations, ages, and times, who differ in their characters and interests. There are, moreover, some *moral* and *philosophical truths* so evident in themselves that it would be easier to imagine half of mankind to have run mad and joined precisely in one and the same species of folly, than to admit anything as *truth* which should be advanced against such *natural knowledge, fundamental reason*, and *common sense*.

# ADVICE, etc.

## PART I.

### SECTION I.

I HAVE often thought how ill-natured a *maxim* it was, which, on many occasions, I have heard from people of good understanding:
“That,
“as to what related to private conduct,
“no one was ever the better for ADVICE.”
But upon further examination, I have resolved within myself that the *maxim* might be admitted without any violent prejudice to mankind. For in the manner *advice*

<sig>Vol. I.</sig>
<meta>catchword: was</meta>

<summary>The author introduces a common saying that no one actually benefits from advice regarding their private conduct. He concludes that this maxim may be true, depending on the way in which the advice is given.</summary>
<keywords>advice, maxim, private conduct, moral philosophy, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the reception of advice, specifically addressing the dynamic between the giver and the receiver.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>A D V I C E to an Author.</header>

<margin>Part 1.</margin>
was generally given, there was no reason, I thought, to wonder it should be so ill received. Something there was which strangely inverted the case, and made *the giver* to be the only gainer. For by what I could observe in many occurrences of our lives, that which we called *giving advice* was, properly, taking an occasion to show our own wisdom at another’s expense. On the other side, to be *instructed*, or *to receive advice* on the terms usually prescribed to us, was little better than tamely to afford another the occasion of building a reputation for himself from our defects.

In reality, however able or willing a man may be *to advise*, it is no easy matter to make ADVICE a *free gift*. For to make a gift free indeed, there must be nothing in it which takes from another to add to oneself. In all other respects, *to give* and *to dispense* is generosity and goodwill; but to bestow wisdom is to gain a mastery which cannot so easily be allowed us. Men willingly learn whatever else is taught them. They can bear *a master* in *mathematics*, in *music*, or in any other *science*; but not in *understanding and good sense*.

It is the hardest thing imaginable for an AUTHOR not to appear assuming in this respect. For all *authors* at large are,

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>The author argues that giving advice is often an act of self-aggrandizement where the giver displays their wisdom at the receiver's expense. He notes that while people accept instruction in sciences like music or math, they resent being told how to use their own understanding.</summary>
<keywords>advice, wisdom, ego, instruction, mastery, human nature, authorial vanity</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the difficulty authors face in advising their readers without appearing arrogant, transitioning into the historical role of poets as teachers of morality.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin>
in a manner, professed *Masters of Understanding* to the age. And for this reason, in early days, *Poets* were looked upon as authentic *Sages* for dictating rules of life and teaching manners and good sense. How they may have lost their claim, I cannot say. It is their peculiar happiness and advantage not to be obliged to make their claim openly. And if, while they profess only to *please*, they secretly *advise* and give instruction, they may now perhaps, as well as formerly, be esteemed, with justice, the best and most honorable among authors.

Meanwhile: 
> "If *dictating* and *prescribing* are of such a dangerous nature in *other authors*, what must his case be who *dictates to authors themselves*?"

To this I answer: that my claim is not so much to *give advice* as to consider the *way* and *manner* of *advising*. My science, if it is any at all, is no better than that of a *language master* or a *logician*. For I have taken it strongly into my head that there is a certain knack or *legerdemain* in argument by which we may safely proceed to the dangerous part of *advising* and ensure the good fortune of having our advice accepted, if it is worth anything.

<meta>catchword: MY</meta>

<summary>The author explains that poets were historically regarded as sages because they taught morality while appearing only to entertain. He clarifies that his own objective is not to dictate to others, but to study the most effective methods and logic for offering advice.</summary>
<keywords>poets, sages, authorship, advice, rhetoric, logic, instruction, legerdemain</keywords>

For company is an extreme provocative to fancy; and, like a hotbed in gardening, is apt to make our imaginations sprout too fast. WE HAVE an account in history of a certain nation, who seem to have been extremely apprehensive of the effects of this frothiness or ventosity <note>windiness or inflated speech</note> in speech, and were accordingly resolved to provide thoroughly against the evil. They carried this *remedy* of ours so far that it was not only their custom, but their religion and law, to speak, laugh, use action, gesticulate, and do everything in the same manner when by themselves as when they were in company. If you had stolen upon them unawares at any time when they were alone, you might have found them in high dispute, arguing with themselves, reproving, counseling, haranguing themselves, and in the most florid manner accosting their own persons. It is remarkable in all great wits, that they have owned this practice of ours, and generally described themselves as a people liable to sufficient ridicule for their great loquacity when alone, and their profound silence in company. If other authors find nothing which invites them to these *Recesses*, it is because their genius is not strong enough: Or even if it is, they may imagine their character will hardly support them. ## Part 1.
<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish</image-desc>

> \* *Aut insanit Homo, aut versus facit—* <note>"Either the man is insane, or he is making verses."</note>

WRITING and Raving, as we see, must necessarily bear a resemblance. And as for those authors who deal in systems and airy speculations, they have commonly passed for a sort of *prose-poets*. Their secret practice and habit has been frequently noted:

> † *Murmura cum secum & rabiosa silentia rodunt.* <note>"They chew on their own murmurs and rabid silences within themselves."</note>

Both these types are happily indulged in this method of venting. They are thought to act naturally, and in their proper way, when they assume these odd manners. But of other authors, it is expected they should be better bred. They are obliged to preserve a more sociable habit, which is no small misfortune to them. For if their meditation and reverie are obstructed by the fear of an unconventional appearance in conversation, they may happen to be so much the worse *authors* for being finer *gentlemen*. Their intensity of imagination may possibly be as strong as either the philosopher’s or the poet’s. But being denied an equal benefit of discharge, and withheld from the wholesome manner of relief in private;

---

->\* Horace, Satires, book 2, satire 7.      † Persius, Satires, satire 3.<-

<summary>Shaftesbury compares the act of writing to a form of raving, noting that while poets and philosophers are socially permitted to talk to themselves to clear their minds, other authors are often hindered by the pressure to maintain a refined, social appearance.</summary>

</margin>
it is no wonder if they appear with so much froth and scum in public.

It is observable that the writers of **MEMOIRS** and **ESSAYS** are chiefly subject to this frothy distemper. Nor can it be doubted that this is the true reason why these gentlemen entertain the world so lavishly with what relates to *themselves*. For having had no opportunity to converse privately with themselves, or to exercise their own *genius*, so as to make acquaintance with it or prove its strength; they immediately fall to work in the wrong place, and exhibit on the stage of the world that *practice* which they should have kept to themselves, if they intended that either they, or the world, should be the better for their moralities. Who indeed can endure to hear *an empiric* <note>A self-taught practitioner or quack doctor.</note> talk of his own constitution, how he governs and manages it, what diet agrees best with it, and what his practice is with himself? *The proverb, no doubt, is very just: Physician, cure yourself.* Yet I think one would have a bad time to be present at these bodily operations. Nor is the reader, in truth, any better entertained when he is obliged to assist at the experimental discussions of his practicing author, who all the while is in reality doing no better than taking his medicine in public.

<summary>Shaftesbury criticizes authors of memoirs and essays who publish their unrefined thoughts without prior self-reflection, comparing the display to a doctor taking medicine or treating himself in public.</summary>
<keywords>authorship, Memoirs, Essays, self-reflection, empiric, morality, public discourse</keywords>

Part I.

For this reason, I hold it very indecent for anyone to publish his *Meditations, Occasional Reflections, Solitary Thoughts*, or other such exercises that fall under the notion of this *self-discoursing practice*. And the most modest title I can conceive for such works would be that of a certain author who called them his *crudities*. It is the misfortune of those wits who conceive suddenly, but without being able to carry to full term, that after many miscarriages and abortions, they can bring nothing well-shaped or perfect into the world. They are not, however, any less fond of their offspring, which in a sense they beget in public. For they are so public-spirited that they can never afford themselves the least time to think in private for their own particular benefit and use. For this reason, though they are often retired, they are never *by themselves*. The world is always a party to it. They have their *author-character* in view and are always considering how this or that thought would serve to complete some set of *contemplations* or furnish the *commonplace book* from which these treasured riches are to flow in plenty upon the needy world.

But if our candidates for authorship happen to be of the *sanctified* kind, it is

But I should esteem it unfair to proceed upon such a hypothesis as this: when the very most the wise ancients ever meant by this *Daemon-Companion*, I conceive to have been no more than enigmatically to declare, “That we had each of us a patient in *ourself*; that we were properly our own subjects of practice; and that we then became proper practitioners when, by virtue of an intimate *recess*, we could discover a certain *duplicity* of soul, and divide ourselves into *two parties*.” One of these, as they supposed, would immediately prove himself a venerable *Sage*; and with an air of authority erect himself our counselor and governor; while the other party, who had nothing in him besides what was base and *servile*, would be contented to follow and obey.

According therefore as this *recess* was deep and intimate, and the *Dual Number* practically formed in us, we were supposed to advance in morals and true wisdom. This, they thought, was the only way of composing matters in our breast, and establishing that subordination, which alone could make us agree with ourselves, and be consistent *within*. Part I. this to be a more religious work than any prayers, or other duty in the temple. And this they advised us to carry there, as the best offering which could be made:

<margin>* Persius, Satire 2.</margin>
->* *Compositum jus, fasque animi, sanctosque*<-
->*recessus*<-
->*Mentis.*<-

---

THIS was, among the ancients, that celebrated *Delphic* inscription, RECOGNIZE YOURSELF: which was as much as to say, *Divide yourself*, or *Be Two*. For if the division were rightly made, all *within* would of course, they thought, be rightly understood and prudently managed. Such confidence they had in this *home-dialect* of SOLILOQUY. For it was accounted the specialty of *philosophers* and wise men to be able to *keep themselves in conversation*. And it was their boast on this account, "That they were never less *alone*, than when *by themselves*." A *knave*, they thought, could never be *by himself*. Not that his conscience was always sure of giving him disturbance; but he had not, they supposed, so much influence with himself as to exert this generous faculty and raise himself *a companion*; who, being fairly admitted into partnership, would quickly improve his partner and set his affairs on a right footing.

<meta>catchword: ONE</meta>

<summary>The author describes the Delphic command "Recognize Yourself" as a call to internal soliloquy, where one divides themselves into two to achieve self-governance. This internal dialogue was considered the hallmark of wise men and a tool for moral self-improvement that even a "knave" could benefit from if they dared to be alone.</summary>

### Section 2.
<image-desc>A small decorative horizontal squiggle.</image-desc>

ONE would think there was nothing easier for us than to know our own minds and understand what our main *scope* was; what we plainly drove at, and what we proposed to ourselves as our *end* in every occurrence of our lives. But our thoughts generally have such an obscure, implicit language that it is the hardest thing in the world to make them speak out distinctly. For this reason, the right method is to give them voice and accent. And this, because of our own failure, is what the *moralists* or *philosophers* endeavor to do for us; when, as is usual, they hold out to us a kind of *vocal looking-glass*, draw sound out of our breast, and instruct us to personate ourselves in the plainest manner.

<margin>* Persius, Satire 2.</margin>
> \* *Illa sibi introrsum, et sub lingua immurmurat: o si*
> *ebullit patrui praeclarum funus!*
<note>She murmurs to herself inwardly and under her breath: "Oh, if only my uncle’s magnificent funeral would finally happen!"</note>

close retirement and inward recess. It is the grand artifice of *villainy* and *lewdness*, as well as of *superstition* and *bigotry*, to put us on terms of greater distance and formality with ourselves, and to evade our proving method of SOLILOQUY. And for this reason, however specious the instruction and doctrine of *formalists* may be, their very manner itself is a sufficient *blind*, or *remora* <note>a hindrance or obstruction</note> in the way of honesty and good sense.

I AM aware that should my reader happen to be a *lover*, in the more profound and solemn way of love, he would be apt to conclude that he was no stranger to our proposed method of *practice*; being conscious that he has often made vigorous excursions into those solitary regions mentioned above, where *soliloquy* is maintained with the most advantage. He may chance to remember how he has many times addressed the woods and rocks in audible, articulate sounds, and seemingly expostulated with himself in such a manner as if he had really formed the required *distinction*, and had the power to entertain himself in due form. But it is very apparent that, even if everything we have supposed here were true, it can in no way reach the case before us. For a passionate lover, whatever solitude he may affect, can never be truly by *himself*.

<margin>Sect. 2.</margin>
His case is like the *Author's* who has begun his courtship of the public, and is embarked in an intrigue which sufficiently amuses <note>occupies or distracts the mind</note> and takes him out of himself. Whatever he meditates on alone is still interrupted by the imagined presence of the mistress he pursues. Not a thought, not an expression, not a sigh is purely for himself. All is appropriated, and all is devoutly tendered to the object of his passion. Inasmuch as there is nothing ever so trivial or accidental of this kind which he is not desirous should be witnessed by the party whose grace and favor he solicits.

It is for the same reason that the imaginary saint or *mystic* is kept from being capable of this entertainment. Instead of looking closely into his own nature and mind, so that he may be no longer a mystery to himself, he is occupied with the contemplation of other mysterious natures which he can never explain or comprehend. He has the specters of his zeal before his eyes; and is as familiar with his modes, essences, personages, and exhibitions of **DEITY** as the *conjurer* is with his different forms, species, and orders of **GENII** or **DÆMONS**. So that we do not hesitate to assert that not even a recluse religionist, a votary, or a *hermit* was ever truly by himself. And thus

A VIRTUOUS young Prince of a heroic soul, capable of love and friendship, made war upon a tyrant who was in every respect his opposite. ### Sect. 2.
<image-desc>wavy horizontal line ornament</image-desc>

With this, the young nobleman departed to execute his commission: and immediately took such care of the captive princess and her household that she seemed as perfectly obeyed, and had everything which belonged to her in as great splendor now, as in her principality and in the height of her fortune. He found her deserving in every respect, and saw in her a generosity of soul which went beyond her other charms. His diligence in pleasing her and softening her distress made her, in return, desire to express a gratitude which he easily perceived. She showed on every occasion a real concern for his interest; and when he happened to fall ill, she took such tender care of him herself, and through her servants, that he seemed to owe his recovery to her friendship.

From these beginnings, gradually and by natural degrees (as may easily be imagined), the youth fell desperately in love. At first, he did not attempt to make the slightest mention of his passion to the princess. For he scarcely dared tell it to himself. But afterward he grew bolder. She received his declaration with genuine trouble and concern, speaking to him as a friend to dissuade him as much as possible from such an extravagant attempt. But when he spoke to her of force, she immediately

Despite her attempts to dissuade him as a friend, his feelings grow so bold that he eventually threatens the use of force.</summary>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Part I.</margin>
She sent away one of her faithful servants to the Prince to implore his protection. The Prince received the message with the appearance of more than ordinary concern; he sent instantly for one of his first ministers and told him to go with that servant to the young nobleman and let him understand: "That *force* was not to be offered to such a lady; *persuasion* he might use, if he thought fit."

The minister, who was no friend to the young nobleman, did not fail to intensify the message. He denounced him publicly on this occasion and reproached him to his face as a traitor and a dishonorer of his prince and nation, along with everything else that could be said against him for being guilty of the highest sacrilege, treachery, and breach of trust. Consequently, the youth looked upon his case as desperate, fell into the deepest melancholy, and prepared himself for that fate which he thought he well deserved.

In this condition, the Prince sent to speak with him alone; and when he saw him in the utmost confusion, "I find," *said he*, "my friend, I have now become dreadful to you indeed, since you can neither see me without shame nor imagine me to be without resentment. But put away all those thoughts from..."

<summary>After the princess appeals to the Prince for protection, he sends a minister to warn the nobleman against using force; the minister’s harsh public shaming drives the nobleman into a suicidal despair. The Prince later meets the nobleman privately to address his overwhelming guilt and shame.</summary>
