<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>
Part I.
</margin>

“...rendered both to them and to me.”

Such a hint was sufficient to revive the spirits of our despairing youth. He was overjoyed to think that his misfortunes could be turned in any way to the advantage of his Prince; he entered with joy into the scheme the Prince had laid for him, and appeared eager to depart and execute what was assigned to him. “Can you then,” said the Prince, “resolve to leave the charming Princess?”

“O Sir!” replied the youth, “I am now well satisfied that I have in reality within me *two distinct, separate souls*. I have learned this lesson of philosophy from that villainous sophist **Love**. For it is impossible to believe that, having one and the same soul, it should be actually both good and bad, passionate for virtue and vice, and desirous of contraries. No. There must of necessity be *two*: and when the *good* prevails, it is then we act handsomely; when *the evil*, then we act basely and villainously. Such was my case. For lately *the evil soul* was wholly master. But now *the good* prevails, by your assistance; and I am clearly a new creature, with quite another *apprehension*, another *reason*, and another *will*.”

<vocab>Philosophy, Love, Souls, Virtue, Vice, Apprehension, Reason, Will</vocab>

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

### Sect. 2.
---

THUS it may appear how far a *Lover*, by his own natural strength, may reach the chief principle of *philosophy*, and understand our *doctrine of two persons* in one individual *self*. Not that our courtier, we suppose, was able, of himself, to form this *distinction* justly and according to *art*. For could he have accomplished this, he would have been able to cure himself without the assistance of his *Prince*. However, he was wise enough to see in the end that his *independence* and *freedom* were mere *glosses*, and *resolution* a nose of wax. For let *will* be ever so free, *humor* and *fancy*, we see, govern it. And these, as free as we suppose them, are often changed, we know not how, without asking our consent or giving us any account. If <note>See below, p. 324. And VOL. III. p. 198, 199.</note> *opinion* be that which governs and makes the change, it is itself as liable to be governed and varied in its turn. And by what I can observe of the world, *fancy and opinion* stand pretty much upon the same foundation. So that if there is no certain *inspector* or *auditor* established within us to take account of these opinions and fancies in due form, and minutely to observe their several growths and habits, we are as unlikely to remain for a day in the same *will* as a tree, during a summer, is to remain in the same *shape*.

---

<summary>The author explains that while a lover may perceive the dual nature of the self, they cannot master it without external help because the will is constantly shifted by unstable fancies and opinions. He argues that without an internal "inspector" to monitor these changing thoughts, a person's character remains as inconsistent as a growing tree.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy, dualism, self-governance, will, humor, fancy, opinion, internal auditor, consistency, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy from the previous page, comparing the human mind to a tree that cannot maintain its shape without the gardener's care.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

Part 1. without the gardener’s assistance and the vigorous application of the shears and pruning knife.

---

As cruel as *the Inquisition* appears, it seems there must be one just as formidable established within ourselves if we would claim that uniformity of opinion which is necessary to hold us to *one will*, and preserve us in the same mind from one day to another. Philosophy, at this rate, will perhaps be thought *little better than persecution*; and *a supreme judge* in matters of inclination and appetite must necessarily go exceedingly against the heart. Every pretty fancy is disturbed by it; every pleasure interrupted by it. The course of good humor will hardly allow it, and the pleasantry of wit almost absolutely rejects it. It appears, besides, like a kind of pedantry to be thus magisterial with ourselves; thus strict over our imaginations, and with all the airs of a real pedagogue to be anxiously occupied with the sour care and tutorage of so many boyish fancies, unlucky appetites, and desires, which are perpetually playing truant and need correction.

WE hope, however, that by our method of practice, and the help of the grand *arcanum*, which we have professed to reveal, this *regimen* or *discipline of the*

<summary>The author compares internal self-discipline to an "Inquisition" of the mind, acknowledging that such strict governance over one's own desires and fancies can feel like harsh persecution. He notes that while this magisterial self-correction is necessary for a consistent will, it is often rejected by wit and good humor as being overly pedantic.</summary>
<keywords>internal discipline, philosophy, self-governance, the Inquisition, uniformity of opinion, fancies, appetites, arcanum</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the internal discipline required for self-governance, shifting from the discomfort of the process to its ultimate goal of creating a consistent identity.</meta>

<page-num>187</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Sect. 2. <image-desc>decorative flourish</image-desc></margin>
Fancies may not in the end prove so severe or mortifying as is imagined. We hope also that our *Patient* (for such we naturally suppose our *Reader*) will consider duly within himself that what he endures in this operation is for no insignificant end: since it is to gain him a *Will* and ensure for him a *certain Resolution*, by which he shall know where to find himself; be sure of his own meaning and design; and, as to all his desires, opinions, and inclinations, be guaranteed to be *one and the same person* today as yesterday, and tomorrow as today.

THIS, perhaps, will be thought a miracle by one who well considers the nature of mankind and the growth, variation, and inflection of *appetite* and *humor*. For APPETITE, which is the elder brother to REASON, being the lad of stronger growth, is sure in every contest to take the advantage of drawing everything to his own side. And *Will*, so highly boasted, is at best merely a top or football between these youngsters, who prove very unfortunately matched—until the youngest, instead of now and then a kick or lash bestowed to little purpose, forsakes the ball or top itself and begins to strike out at his elder brother. It is then that the scene changes. For the elder, like a complete coward upon this treatment, soon grows civil and allows the younger as fair play afterward as he can desire.

<meta>catchword: AND</meta>

<summary>The author argues that internal discipline leads to a consistent "Will" and a stable identity, allowing a person to remain the same from day to day. He uses an analogy of two brothers, Appetite and Reason, where the younger Reason must eventually confront the stronger Appetite to gain control over the Will.</summary>
<keywords>self-governance, identity, consistency, Will, Appetite, Reason, internal conflict, psychology</keywords>

<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>
Part I.
</margin>

AND here it is that our sovereign remedy and gymnastic method of SOLILOQUY takes its rise: when, by a certain powerful figure of inward rhetoric, the mind *apostrophizes* its own FANCIES, raises them in their proper *shapes* and *personages*, and addresses them familiarly, without the least ceremony or respect. By this means it will soon happen that two formed *parties* will erect themselves *within*. For the imaginations or fancies, being thus directly treated, are forced to declare themselves and take sides. Those on the side of the elder brother APPETITE are strangely subtle and insinuating. They always have the faculty to speak by nods and winks. By this practice they conceal half their meaning and, like modern politicians, pass for deeply wise, and adorn themselves with the finest pretexts and most specious glosses imaginable; until, being confronted with their fellows of a plainer language and expression, they are forced to quit their mysterious manner and reveal themselves as mere *sophists* and *impostors*, who have nothing at all to do with the party of REASON and good sense.

ACCORDINGLY, we might now proceed to exhibit distinctly, and in due method, the form and manner of this *probation*,

<meta>catchword: bation,</meta>

<summary>The author introduces "soliloquy" as a mental exercise where one confronts their own internal "fancies" to distinguish between deceptive appetites and the clarity of reason. This internal dialogue forces the mind's various impulses to reveal their true nature, exposing irrational desires as sophistical impostors.</summary>
<keywords>soliloquy, internal rhetoric, appetite, reason, self-examination, fancies, psychology, sophistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the method of self-examination or "probation," applying the need for internal exercise specifically to the profession of the author.</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Section 2.</margin>
probation, or *Exercise*, as it regards all people in general. But the case of *Authors*, in particular, being, as we understand it, the most urgent; we shall apply our rule in the first place to these gentlemen, for whom it is so highly important to know themselves, and understand the natural *strength* and *powers*, as well as the *weaknesses* of a human mind. For without this understanding, the *historian's* judgment will be very defective; the *politician's* views very narrow and fanciful; and the *poet's* brain, however stocked with fiction, will be but poorly furnished, as we shall make appear in the following sections. He who deals in *characters* must of necessity know *his own*, or he will know nothing. And he who would give the world a profitable entertainment of this sort should be sure to profit, first, by himself. For in this sense, *wisdom* as well as *charity* may be honestly said to begin at home. There is no way of estimating *manners*, or appraising the different *humors*, *fancies*, *passions*, and *perceptions* of others, without first taking an inventory of the same kind of goods within ourselves, and surveying our domestic fund. A little of this *home-practice* will serve to make great discoveries.

> *Live with yourself, and you will know how meager your own furnishings are.*
> ->Persius, Satire 4.<-

->S E C T.<-

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that authors, historians, and poets must possess deep self-knowledge to effectively portray others, as wisdom and the understanding of human nature must begin with a "domestic inventory" of one's own mind. He asserts that without this internal practice, an author's work will be narrow, defective, and poorly furnished.</summary>
<keywords>self-knowledge, authorship, historians, politicians, poets, self-examination, Persius, human nature, domestic fund, wisdom</keywords>

<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>Advice to an Author.</header>

<margin>Part I.</margin>

## S E C T. III.

WHOEVER has been an observer of *action* and *grace* in human bodies must of necessity have discovered the great difference in this respect between such persons as have been taught by nature only, and such as by reflection and the assistance of art have learned to form those motions which through experience are found the easiest and most natural. Of the former kind are either those good *rustics* who have been bred far from the organized societies of men, or those plain *artisans* and people of lower rank who, living in cities and places of resort, have nevertheless been necessitated to follow lowly employments and lacked the opportunity and means to form themselves after the better models. There are some persons indeed so happily formed by nature herself that, with the greatest simplicity or rudeness of education, they still have something of a natural grace and comeliness in their action; and there are others of a better education who, by a wrong aim and injudicious affectation of grace, are of all people the farthest removed from it. It is undeniable, however, that the perfection of grace and comeliness in action and behavior can be found only among the people of a liberal education. And even among

<meta>catchword: among</meta>

<summary>Shaftesbury compares natural behavior to refined "action," noting that while some individuals possess innate grace, true perfection in movement and behavior is typically the result of reflection, art, and a liberal education.</summary>
<keywords>grace, action, education, nature vs art, rustics, artisans, social rank, behavior</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy between physical grace and literary skill, arguing that true excellence in both fields requires formal training under masters rather than mere imitation.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>
<sig>Vol. I. N</sig>

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin>
among the graceful of this kind, those still are found the most graceful who early in their youth have learned their exercises and formed their motions under the best *masters*.

Now just as these *masters* and their lessons are to a *fine gentleman*, so are *philosophers* and *philosophy* to an *author*. The case is the same in the *fashionable* and in the *literate* world. In the former of these, it is remarked that by the help of good company and the force of example alone, a decent carriage is acquired, with such appropriate motions and such a freedom of limbs as may enable the party to demean himself like a gentleman on all ordinary occasions. But when upon further occasion a trial is made in an extraordinary way—when exercises of the more refined kind are to be performed in public—it will easily appear who among the pretenders have been formed by basic principles and had masters *in private*, and who, on the other side, have contented themselves with bare imitation and learned their part casually and by rote. The parallel is easily made on the side of *writers*. They have at least as much need of learning the several motions, counterpoises, and balances of the mind and passions as the other students have of those of the body and limbs.

<meta>catchword: Scribendi</meta>

<summary>Shaftesbury compares the training of a gentleman to that of an author, suggesting that just as physical grace requires private instruction from masters, true writing requires a philosophical understanding of the mind's passions.</summary>
<keywords>Advice to an Author, Philosophy, fine Gentleman, Writers, carriage, imitation, motions, training, grace, passions</keywords>

**THE** gallant, no doubt, may pen a letter to his mistress, as *the courtier* may a compliment to *the minister*, or the minister to *the favourite* above him, without going into such vast depths of learning or philosophy. <margin>Sect. 3.</margin>
them to hold the rank of *Authors*, or be styled *Writers* by way of excellence in their kind. Should their ambition lead them into such a field, they would be obliged to come otherwise equipped. They who enter the public lists must come duly trained and exercised, like well-equipped *Cavaliers*, expert in arms and well-instructed in the use of their weapon and the management of their steed. For to be well-accoutered and well-mounted is not sufficient. The horse alone can never make the *horseman*; nor limbs the *wrestler* or the *dancer*. No more can a genius alone make a *poet*, or good talents a *writer* of any significant kind. The skill and grace of writing is founded, as our wise poet tells us, in *knowledge* and *good sense*: and not barely in that knowledge which is to be learned from common authors or the general conversation of the world, but from those particular rules of art which philosophy alone presents.

THE philosophical writings to which our poet in his *Art of Poetry* refers were in themselves a kind of *poetry*, like the \* *mimes* or dramatized pieces of early times, before *philosophy* was in vogue and when dramatic imitation was as yet scarcely formed; or at least, in many parts, not

They were either real *dialogues* or *recitals* of such *impersonated discourses*, where the persons themselves had their characters preserved throughout; their manners, humors, and distinct turns of temper and understanding maintained according to the most exact *poetical truth*. It was not enough that these pieces treated fundamentally of *morals* and, in consequence, pointed out *real characters and manners*: they exhibited them alive and set the countenances and complexions of men plainly in view. And by this means they not only taught us to know *others* but, what was principal and of highest virtue in them, they taught us to know *ourselves*.

THE *philosophical hero* of these *poems*, whose name they carried both in their *body* and *front*, and whose *genius* and *manner* they were made to represent, was in himself a *perfect character*; yet, in some respects, so veiled and in a *cloud* that to the unattentive *surveyor* he often seemed to be very different from what he really was; and this chiefly by reason of a certain exquisite and refined *raillery* which belonged to his *manner*, and by

So that in this genius of writing, there appeared both the *heroic* and the *simple*, the *tragic*, and the *comic vein*. However, it was so arranged that, notwithstanding the oddness or mysteriousness of the principal character, the *under-parts* or *secondary characters* showed human nature more distinctly and to the life. We might here, therefore, as in a *looking-glass*, discover ourselves, and see our minutest features nicely delineated, and suited to our own apprehension and cognizance. No one who was even for a short while an observer could fail to become acquainted with his own heart. And, what was of particular note in these *magical glasses*, it would happen that by constant and long inspection, the parties accustomed to the practice would acquire a peculiar *speculative habit*; so as virtually to carry about with them a sort of *pocket-mirror*, always ready and in use. In this, there were *two faces* which would naturally present themselves to our view: *one of them*, like the commanding genius, the leader and chief mentioned above; the *other* like that rude, undisciplined and headstrong creature, whom we ourselves in our natural capacity most exactly resembled. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Homer's mastery of the dramatic method, specifically how his invisible presence as an author allows his characters to appear as living beings.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>

*Dialogues*, which turn upon *one* remarkable *Catastrophe* or event. He describes no qualities or virtues; censures no manners; makes no encomiums, nor gives characters himself; but brings his actors always into view. It is they who show themselves. It is they who speak in such a manner as distinguishes them in all things from all others, and makes them always like themselves. Their different compositions and alloys, so justly made and equally carried on through every particle of the action, give more instruction than all the comments or glosses in the world. The Poet, instead of giving himself those dictating and masterly airs of wisdom, makes hardly any figure at all, and is scarcely discoverable in his poem. This is being truly *a master*. He paints so as to need no inscription over his figures to tell us what they are, or what he intends by them. A few words let fall, on any slight occasion, from any of the parties he introduces, are sufficient to denote their manners and distinct character. From a finger or a toe, he can represent to our thoughts the frame and fashion of a whole body. He needs no other help of art to portray his heroes and make them living. There was no more left for *Tragedy* to do after him, than to erect a stage and draw his dialogues and characters into scenes; turning, in the same manner, upon one principal action

FROM this, possibly, we may form a notion of that resemblance which on so many occasions was previously remarked between the Prince of Poets and the Divine Philosopher, who was said to rival him, and who, together with his contemporaries of the same school, wrote wholly in that manner of *dialogue* described above. From this, too, we may perhaps comprehend why the study of dialogue was previously thought so advantageous to *writers*, and why this manner of writing was judged so difficult, which at first sight, it must be owned, appears the easiest of any.

I HAVE formerly wondered, indeed, why a *manner* which was familiarly used in

But I later perceived that, besides the difficulty of the *manner* itself and that *mirror-faculty* which we have observed it to possess in respect to *ourselves*, it also necessarily proves to be a kind of mirror or looking-glass to *the age*. If so, you will say that it should consequently be all the more agreeable and entertaining.

True—if the real view of ourselves is not perhaps displeasing to us. But why is it more displeasing to us than to the ancients? Perhaps because they could, with good reason, bear to see their natural countenances represented. And why can we not do the same? What should discourage us? For are we not as handsome, at least *in our own eyes*? Perhaps not; as we shall see when we have considered a little further what the power of this *mirror-writing* is, and how it differs from that more compliant, fashionable way in which an author, instead of presenting us with other natural characters, displays his own with the utmost art and buys his reader's favor through every imaginable compliance and condescension.

All turns upon their two persons. And as in a love affair, or an exchange of love letters, so here the author has the privilege of talking eternally of himself, dressing and sprucing himself up, while he is making diligent court and working upon the humor of the party to whom he addresses his work. This is the coquetry of a modern author, whose dedicatory epistles, prefaces, and addresses to the reader are so many affected graces designed to draw the attention from the subject toward *himself*; and make it be generally observed not so much *what he says* as *what he appears* or *is*, and what figure he already makes, or hopes to make, in the fashionable world.

THESE are the airs which a neighboring nation gives themselves, more particularly in what they call their *memoirs*. Their very essays on politics, their philosophical and critical works, their comments upon ancient and modern authors—all their treatises are *memoirs*. MUCH more is this the case in DIALOGUE. For here *the author* is annihilated; and *the reader*, being in no way addressed, stands for nobody. The self-interested parties both vanish at once. The scene presents itself as if by chance and without design. You are not only left to judge coolly, and with indifference, of the *sense* delivered, but of the *character*, *genius*, *eloquence*, and *manner* of the persons who deliver it. These two are mere strangers, in whose favor you are in no way engaged. Nor is it enough that the persons introduced speak pertinent and good sense at every turn. It must be seen *from what foundation* they speak; from what *principle*, what *stock* or *fund* of knowledge they draw; and what kind or species of understanding they possess. For the understanding here must have its mark, its characteristic note, by which it may be distinguished. It must be *such and such an understanding*; as when we say, for instance, *such or such a face*: since Nature has characterized tempers and minds as uniquely as faces. And for an artist who draws

Now, as a painter who draws battles or other actions of *Christians*, *Turks*, *Indians*, or any distinct and unique people, must of necessity draw the several figures of his piece in their proper and real proportions, gestures, clothing, arms, or at least with as fair a resemblance as possible; so in the same manner, whatever writer among us moderns shall venture to bring his fellow-moderns into *dialogue* must introduce them in their proper manners, character, behavior, and temperament. And this is the *mirror* or *looking-glass* described above.

FOR instance, let us suppose a dialogue is framed after the manner of our ancient authors. In it, a poor philosopher of a humble appearance accosts one of the most powerful, witty, handsome, and rich noblemen of the time, as he is walking leisurely towards the temple.

Consider how many bows and simpering faces! How many preludes, excuses, and compliments! Now put *compliments* and *ceremony* into a *dialogue*, and see what the effect will be!

This is the plain *dilemma* against that ancient manner of writing, which we can neither well imitate nor translate, whatever pleasure or profit we may find in reading those *originals*. For what shall we do in such a circumstance? What if the whim takes us and we resolve to try the experiment on modern subjects? See the consequence! If we avoid ceremony, we are unnatural; if we use it and appear as we naturally are—as we salute, meet, and treat one another—we hate the sight. What is this but *hating our own faces*? Is it the *painter's fault*? Should he paint falsely or affectedly, mix modern with ancient, join shapes preposterously, and betray his art? If not, what middle ground is there? What remains for him but to throw away the *paintbrush*?

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rules of art and the importance of ancient models, transitioning from the critique of modern manners to the specific study of "Exemplars" or classic Greek drafts.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
<header>Advice to an Author.</header>

<margin>Part 1.</margin>
Mirrors, the *models* he bids us place
before our eyes:

> * ————Vos *Exemplaria Græca*
> *Nocturnâ versate manu, versate diurnâ.*

AND thus *poetry* and the *writer’s art*, as it resembles in many respects the *sculptor's* and the *painter's*, so in this more particularly: that it has its original *drafts* and *models* for study and practice; not for *ostentation*, to be shown abroad, or copied for public view. These are the ancient *busts*; the *trunks* of statues; the *pieces* of *anatomy*; the masterful rough *drawings* which are kept inside, as the secret learning, the *mystery*, and fundamental knowledge of the art. There is this essential difference, however, between the artists of each kind: those who design merely after *bodies*, and form the *graces* of this sort, can never, with all their accuracy or correctness of design, be able to reform themselves or grow a bit more shapely in their persons. But for those artists who copy from another life, who study the graces and perfections of *minds*, and are real masters of those rules which constitute this latter science, it is impossible they should fail of being themselves improved and amended in their *better part*.

I MUST confess there is hardly anywhere to be found a more insipid race of mortals than those whom we moderns are content to call *poets*, for having attained the rhyming talent of a language with an injudicious, random use of *wit* and *fancy*. But for the man who truly and in a proper sense deserves the name of *poet*, and who as a real master or architect of the craft can describe both *men* and *manners* and give to an *action* its proper body and proportions, he will be found, if I mistake not, to be a very different creature. Such a poet is indeed a second *maker*; a true PROMETHEUS under JOVE. Like that sovereign artist or universal plastic nature, he forms *a whole*, coherent and proportioned in itself, with due subjection and subordination of constituent parts. He notes the boundaries of the passions and knows their exact *tones* and *measures*, by which he justly represents them, marks the *sublime* of sentiments and action, and distinguishes the *beautiful* from the *deformed*, the *amiable* from the *odious*. The moral artist who can thus imitate the Creator, and is thus knowledgeable in the inward form and structure of his fellow creature, will hardly, I presume, be found ignorant of *himself* or at a loss in those rhythms which make the harmony of a mind. For *knavery* is

* This maxim will hardly be disproved by fact or history, either in respect to philosophers themselves or others who were the great geniuses or masters of the liberal arts. The characters of the two best *Roman* poets are well known. Those of the ancient *tragedians* are no less so. And the great epic master, though from an obscurer and more remote age, was always presumed to be far from a vile or knavish character. The *Roman* as well as the *Grecian* orator was true to his country and died, in like manner, a martyr for its liberty. And those historians who are of the highest value were either approved as good *men* in private life or noted as such by their actions in public. As for poets in particular (says the learned and wise STRABO):

<page-num>209</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author. Sect. 3.</header>

BUT having entered thus seriously into the concerns of *authors*, and shown their chief foundation and strength, their preparatory discipline, and qualifying method of *self-examination*; it is fit, before we disclose this *mystery* any further, that we should consider the advantages or disadvantages our authors may possibly meet with *from abroad*: and how far their genius may be depressed or raised by any external causes arising from the humor or judgment of the *world*.

WHATEVER it is which influences in this respect must proceed either from the GRANDEES and *men in power*, the CRITICS and *men of art*, or the PEOPLE themselves—*the common audience* and *mere vulgar*. We shall begin therefore with the *grandees* and pretended masters of the world: taking the liberty, in favor of authors, to bestow some *advice* also on these high persons; if possibly they are disposed to receive it in such a familiar way as this.

# PART II.

## SECTION I.

<margin>Part 2.</margin>
As common as it is for mankind to act purely by will and pleasure, without regard for counsel or the rigid method of rule and principle, it must nevertheless be acknowledged that the good and commendable custom of *asking advice* is still upheld and kept in fashion as a matter of good reputation and honorable appearance. Indeed, even monarchs and absolute princes themselves do not disdain, we see, to claim they follow this practice.

It is, I presume, for this reason that royal persons are pleased, on public occasions, to use the well-known style of WE and US. This is not because they are supposed to have any *conversation with themselves*, as if they were granted the privilege of becoming *plural* and expanding their capacity in the manner described above. Single and absolute individuals in government,

He suggests that the "royal we" serves as a formal nod to this tradition of counsel rather than reflecting a literal plurality of the ruler's person.</summary>

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
I am aware, they can hardly be considered as anything other than *single* and *absolute* in morals. They have no *internal controller* to argue with them, or dispute their pleasure. Nor have they, from any practice *abroad* in the world, been able at any time to learn the way of being free and familiar with themselves *at home*. INCLINATION and WILL in people like these allow for as little restraint or check in private meditation as in public company. The world, which serves as a tutor to persons of an inferior rank, is submissive to these *royal pupils*; who from their earliest days are used to see even their *instructors* bend before them, and hear everything applauded which they themselves perform.

Therefore, for fear that their mere humor, or the caprice of some favorite, should be presumed to influence them when they reach the years of princely discretion and are advanced to the helm of government, it has been esteemed a necessary decency to summon certain *professional advisers* to assist as attendants to the *single person*, and be joined with him in his written edicts, proclamations, letters patent, and other instruments of royal power. For this purpose, *Privy Counselors* have been established; who, being persons of considerable importance and wise appearance, cannot be supposed to stand as statues or mere

<margin>Part 2.</margin> nonentities in the government, and leave the royal acts erroneously and falsely described to us in the plural number; when, fundamentally, a *single will* or *whim* was the sole source and motive.

**Foreign princes** indeed, most of them have that unhappy prerogative of acting *unadvisedly* and *willfully* in their national affairs; but it is known to be far otherwise with the legal and just princes of our island. They are surrounded by the best of *counselors*, the **laws**. They administer civil affairs through legal officers, who have the direction of their public will and conscience; and they annually receive *advice* and *aid*, in the most effective manner, from their good people. To this wise spirit of our constitution, we may be justly said to owe our wisest and best princes, whose high birth or royal education could not alone be supposed to have given them that happy disposition; since by experience we find that those very princes, from whose conduct the world abroad, as well as we at home, have reaped the greatest advantages, were those who had the most disputed titles, and in their youth had stood in the more distant prospects of royal power, living closest to a private life.

<summary>The author contrasts absolute foreign monarchs, who rule by personal whim, with British princes who are guided by the law and public counsel. He argues that the best rulers are often those who lived as private citizens before ascending to the throne through disputed successions.</summary>

WE have had other princes who, though perhaps difficult in receiving counsel, have been eminent in the practice of applying it to others. They have enlisted themselves as *advisers* in form, and by publishing their admonitory works, have added to the number of those whom we have presumed to criticize in this treatise. But since our criticism is also an apology for *authors* and a defense of the literate tribe, it cannot be considered wrong for us to join the royal with the plebeian writers in this common cause.

But as the greatest advantages of this world are not bought at low prices, we are still at this moment spending both our blood and treasure to secure for ourselves this priceless purchase of our *free government* and *national constitution*. The British Muses, in this din of arms, may well lie abject and obscure, especially being as yet in their mere infant state. They have so far scarcely arrived at anything of shapeliness or person. They lisp as if in their cradles; and their stammering tongues, which nothing besides their youth and rawness can excuse, have until now spoken in wretched puns and quibbles. Our dramatic Shakespeare, our Fletcher, Jonson, and our epic Milton preserve this style. And even a later generation, hardly free of this infirmity and aiming at a false *sublime* with crowded *similes* and mixed *metaphors* (the hobbyhorse and rattle of the Muses), entertain our raw fancy and unpracticed ear, which has not yet had the leisure to form itself and become *truly* *musical*.

But those revered bards, rude as they were according to their time and age, have nonetheless provided us with the richest ore. To their eternal honor, they have also been the first Europeans who, since the Gothic *model* of poetry, attempted to throw off the horrid discord of jingling rhyme. They have asserted

Their *two* most accomplished, who came last and closed the scene, were plainly such as had seen the days of liberty and felt the sad effects of its departure. Nor would these have ever been brought into play, except through the friendship of the famous *Maecenas*, who turned a * prince naturally cruel and barbarous toward the love and courtship of the MUSES. These *tutoresses* formed in their royal pupil a new *nature*. They taught him how to charm mankind. They were more to him than his arms or military virtue; and, more than *Fortune* herself, they assisted him in his greatness and made his usurped dominion so enchanting to the world that it could see, without regret, its chains of bondage firmly riveted. The corrupting sweets of such a poisonous government were not, indeed, long-lived. The bitter soon followed. And, in the end, the world was forced to bear with patience those natural and genuine tyrants who succeeded this specious machine of arbitrary and universal power.

Gothicism had already entered into the arts before the savages had made any impression on the Empire. All the advantage that a lucky and almost miraculous succession of good princes could provide for their highly favored arts and sciences was no more than to preserve, during their own reigns, those * perishing remains which had for a while with difficulty survived after the decline of *Liberty*. Not a statue, not a medal, nor a tolerable piece of architecture could show itself afterward. Philosophy, wit, and learning—in which some of those good princes had themselves been so renowned—fell with them; ignorance and darkness overspread the world and prepared it for the *chaos* and ruin that ensued.

WE ARE now in an age when LIBERTY is once again in its ascendant. And we are ourselves that happy nation which not only enjoys it at home but, through our greatness and power, gives life and vigor to it abroad; we are the head and chief of the EUROPEAN *League*, founded on this *common cause*. Nor can it, I presume, be justly feared that we should lose this noble ardor or grow faint under the glorious toil; though, like ancient GREECE, we should be contending for succeeding ages

Let a nation remain ever so unrefined or barbarous, it must have its *poets, rhapsodists, historiographers, and antiquaries* of some kind or another, whose business it will be to recount its remarkable transactions and record the achievements of its *civil* and *military* heroes. And though the *military type* may happen to be the furthest removed from any acquaintance with *literature* or the **MUSES**, they are yet, in reality, the most interested in the cause and party of these chroniclers. The greatest share of fame and admiration falls naturally on the *armed worthies*. The great men *in council* are second in the **MUSES’** favor. But if worthy poetic *geniuses* are not found, nor able writers raised up to recount the lives and celebrate the high actions of great men, they must be misrepresented by such *chroniclers* as chance presents. We have few modern heroes who, like **XENOPHON** or **CAESAR**, can write their own *commentaries*. And the raw *memoir* writings and unformed pieces of modern statesmen, full of their self-interested and private views, will in another age be of little service to support their memory or name, since already the world begins to grow weary of the genre. It is the learned, the able, and disinterested *historian* who

But in another sort of painting, there is great danger lest the hand should disgrace the subject. Vile *encomiums* and wretched *panegyrics* are the worst of *satires*: and when sordid and low geniuses make their court successfully in one way, the noble and able are most likely to revenge it in another.

ALL THINGS considered, as to the interest of our *potentates* and **GRANDEES**, they appear to have only this choice left to them: either wholly, if possible, to suppress *letters*, or give a helping hand towards their support. Wherever the *author-practice* and *liberty of the pen* has in the least prevailed, the governors of the state must be either considerable gainers or sufferers by its means. So that it would become them either, by a right *Turkish* policy, to strike directly at the *profession* and overthrow the very *art* and *mystery* itself, or with alacrity to support and encourage it, in the right manner, by a generous and *impartial* regard to merit.

It will be in vain for our ALEXANDERS to give orders that no one besides a LYSIPPUS should make their statue, nor anyone besides an APELLES should paint their portrait. Insolent intruders will take it upon themselves to try their hand at the features of these heroes. And a vile CHÆRILUS, after all, shall—perhaps even with their own consent—take the place of a deserving and noble artist.

In a government where *the people* are sharers in power, but not distributors or dispensers of rewards, they expect it of their *princes* and *great men* that they should play the generous part, and bestow honor and advantages on those from whom the nation itself may receive honor and advantage. It is expected that those who are high and eminent in the *state* should not only provide for its necessary safety and survival, but omit nothing which may contribute to its dignity and honor. *The arts* and *sciences* must not be left *patronless*. The public itself will join with the talented wits and judges in the resentment of such a neglect—

It is no small advantage, even in *an absolute government*, for a ministry to have *wit* on its side, and to engage men of merit in this field to be its well-wishers and friends. And in those *states* where ambitious leaders often contend for supreme authority, it is a considerable advantage to the bad cause of such pretenders when they can obtain a reputation and influence among men of letters. The good Emperor TRAJAN, though he was no great scholar himself, received his due recognition just as an AUGUSTUS did; and he was as highly celebrated for his munificence and just encouragement of every art and virtue. And CAESAR, who could write so well himself and maintained his cause by wit as well as by arms, knew from experience what it was to have even a CATULLUS as his enemy; and though he was lashed so often in Catullus’s lampoons, he continued to forgive and court him. The traitor knew the importance of this *mildness*. May no one who has the same designs understand the advantages of such conduct so well! I would have required only this one defect in CAESAR’S generosity to have been certain of his never rising to greatness or enslaving his native country. If he had shown a roughness and austerity toward free geniuses, or a neglect or contempt toward men of wit; if he had trusted only to his *arms* and declared himself against *arts* and

I know it is the belief of some who are called *Great Men* that, in regard to their high status, they may be considered to have paid a sufficient tribute to Letters and fulfilled their own particular duty if they indiscriminately choose any subject for their generosity, and are pleased to confer their favor either on some pretender to art or indiscriminately on those among the tribe of writers whose chief ability has been in courting them well and managing to be introduced to their acquaintance. They think this is enough to install them as *Patrons of Wit* and masters of the *literary order*. But this method, more than any other, will least serve their interests or designs. Misplacing rewards is a double injury to merit; and in any cause or interest, it is considered worse than simple indifference or neutrality. There can be no excuse for making a poor choice. Merit of every kind is easily discovered when it is sought. The public itself does not fail to give sufficient indication; and it points out those *geniuses* who only need support and encouragement to become significant. A talented man never starves in obscurity; and *Great Men* must close their eyes tightly, or it would

## S E C T. II.

What judgment, therefore, we are to form concerning the influence of our *grandees* in matters of art and letters will easily be gathered from the reflections already made. It may appear, from the very freedom we have taken in censuring these *men of power*, what little reason authors have to plead them as their excuse for any failure in the improvement of their art and talent. For in a free country, such as ours, there is no order or rank of men more free than that of *writers*: who, if they have real ability and merit, can fully right themselves when injured; and are already furnished with means sufficient to make themselves considered by the men in highest power.

Nor should I suspect the genius of our writers, or charge them with meanness and insufficiency on account of this low-spiritedness which they reveal, were it not for another sort of fear, by

It is strange to see how differently the vanity of mankind runs in different times and seasons. It is currently the boast of almost every enterpriser in the <term>Muses</term>' art, "that by his genius alone, and a natural rapidity of style and thought, he is able to carry all before him; that he plays with his business, does things in passing, by chance, and in the shortest period of time." In the days of <term>Attic</term> *elegance*, as works were then truly of another form and style, so craftsmen were of another temperament, and had their vanity of a quite contrary kind. They became rather affected in trying to reveal the pains they had taken to be correct. They were glad to suggest how laboriously, and with what expense of time, they had brought their smallest work (as perhaps a single *ode* or *satire*, an oration or *panegyric*) to its perfection. When they had so polished their piece, and rendered it so natural and easy that it seemed only a lucky flight, a hit of thought, or flowing vein of humor; they were then chiefly concerned lest it should in reality be mistaken for such, and their artifice remain undiscovered. They were willing it should be known how serious their play was; and how elaborate

SUCH accuracy of workmanship requires a CRITIC'S eye. It is lost upon a common judgment. Nothing grieves a *real artist* more than that indifference of the public, which allows *work* to pass uncriticized. Nothing, on the other hand, rejoices him more than the precise view and inspection of the accurate *examiner* and *judge of work*. It is the *inferior genius*, the slovenly performer, who, knowing nothing of *true workmanship*, endeavors by the best outward gloss and dazzling show to turn the eye away from a direct and steady survey of his piece.

Let his Hearers be of what *Character* they please: be they naturally austere, morose, or rigid; no matter, as long as they are *Critics*, able to judge, remark, and test every *Harmony* and *Symphony*. What is there that mortifies the good *Painter* more than when, amidst his admiring spectators, there is not one present who has been used to comparing the hands of different masters, or has an *Eye* to distinguish the advantages or *Defects* of every *Style*? Through all the inferior orders of *craftsmen*, the rule is found to hold the same. In every science and every art, the real *Masters* or *Experts* rejoice in nothing more than in the thorough search and examination of their performances by all the rules of art and the most precise *Criticism*. Why, therefore (in the *Muses'* name!), is it not the same with our pretenders to the *Writing Art*, our *Poets*, and *Prose-Authors* of every kind? Why in this profession are we found to be such *Critic-Haters*, and indulged in this unlearned aversion, unless it be taken for granted that, as wit and learning stand at present in our nation, we are still on the level of *quacks* and *charlatans*?

IN THE weaker and more imperfect societies of mankind, such as those composed of federated *tribes* or mixed *colonies* barely settled in their new homes, it might be considered sufficient good fortune if the people proved to be only enough masters of language to be able to understand one another, in order to discuss their needs and provide for their common necessities. Their exposed and poor state could not be expected to afford them either that full leisure or easy disposition which was required to raise them to any curiosity of speculation. Those who were neither safe from violence nor secure in plenty were unlikely to engage in unnecessary arts. Nor could it be expected that they should turn their attention toward the rhythm of their language and the harmonious sounds which they accidentally produced. But when, in the course of time, the affairs

**It** may be easily perceived from this that *the* Goddess PERSUASION must have been, in a sense, the mother of *poetry*, *rhetoric*, *music*, and the other kindred arts. For it is apparent that where chief men and leaders had the strongest *interest to persuade*, they used their highest endeavors *to please*. So, in such a *state* or *polity* as has been described, not only the best order of thought and turn of imagination, but the softest and most inviting rhythms must have been employed to charm the *public ear* and to incline the *heart* through the agreeableness of expression.

**Almost** all the ancient *masters* of this sort were said to have been MUSICIANS. And *tradition*, which soon grew legendary, could not better represent the first *founders* or *establishers* of these larger societies than as real *songsters*, who by the power of their

Voice and lyre could charm the wildest beasts, and draw the wild forests and rocks into the form of the fairest cities. Nor can it be doubted that the same *artists*, who so industriously applied themselves to study the rhythms of *speech*, must have made proportional improvements in the study of mere sounds and *natural harmony*; which, of itself, must have considerably contributed toward softening the rude manners and harsh temper of their new people.

If therefore it so happened in these *free communities*, made by consent and *voluntary association*, that after a while, the power of *one*, or of a *few*, grew prevalent over the rest; if FORCE took place, and the affairs of the society were administered without their concurrence, by the influence of *awe* and *terror*: it followed that these moving sciences and arts of speech were little cultivated, since they were of little use. But where PERSUASION was the chief means of guiding the society; where the people were to be convinced before they acted; there *elocution* became significant; there *orators* and *bards* were heard; and the chief geniuses and *sages* of the nation devoted themselves to the study of those arts, by which the people were rendered more tractable through reason and understanding, and more subject to be led by

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that free nations foster the development of arts and sciences because their leaders must rely on persuasion and public approval. In contrast, despotic empires fail to produce refined culture because they rely on force and coercion rather than the cultivation of public taste.</summary>
<keywords>freedom, arts, persuasion, government, science, erudition, despotism, public taste, ingenuity, politeness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the development of the arts, specifically how certain geniuses chose to refine public taste rather than seek power, leading to the rise of critics and sophists.</meta>

<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>
Part 2.
<image-desc>horizontal decorative flourish</image-desc>
</margin>
selves with the *contemplation* merely of these enchanting arts. These they would enjoy all the better, the more they refined their *taste* and cultivated their *ear*. For to all music there must be a proportional ear. An art of *hearing* must be found before the performing arts can have their due effect, or anything exquisite of its kind be felt or comprehended. The skilled performers, therefore, in each art would naturally be the most desirous of improving and refining the public ear; which they could in no other way so well effect as by the help of those later *geniuses*, who were in a manner their *interpreters* to the people; and who by their example taught the public to discover what was just and excellent in each performance.

HENCE was the origin of CRITICS; who, as arts and sciences advanced, would necessarily come into repute as well; and being heard with satisfaction in their turn, were at length tempted to become *authors* and appear in public. These were honored with the name of *Sophists*: a character which in early times was highly respected. Nor did the gravest *philosophers*, who were censors of manners and CRITICS of a higher degree, disdain to exert their *criticism* in the inferior arts, especially in those relating to *speech*,

<summary>Shaftesbury explains that the refinement of the arts requires a cultivated audience, which led to the emergence of critics and "Sophists" as intermediaries between artists and the public. These figures, including philosophers, helped define excellence in performance and the art of speech.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rise of critics and the refinement of public taste, specifically focusing on how the presence of learned critics and philosophers prevented the public from being deceived by superficial or pretentious art.</meta>

<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>
<sig>Q 2</sig>

and the power of *Argument* and *Persua-sion*. <margin>Sect. 2.</margin>

<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish or squiggle underline beneath the section label.</image-desc>

When such a *generation* as this had once arisen, it was no longer possible to deceive mankind with what was deceptive and pretentious. The public would not be satisfied with false wit or superficial eloquence. Where the *learned* CRITICS were so well received, and *philosophers* themselves did not disdain to be among their number, there could not fail to arise *critics* of a lower order, who would subdivide the various provinces of this empire. *Etymologists*, *philologists*, *grammarians*, *rhetoricians*, and others of considerable note and eminence in their field would appear everywhere and justify the truth and justice of their art by revealing the hidden beauties which lay in the works of true *performers*, and by exposing the weaknesses, false ornaments, and affected graces of mere *pretenders*. Nothing of what we call *sophistry* in argument or *bombast* in style; nothing of the effeminate kind, or of the false *tender*, the pointed *witticism*, the disjointed *thought*, the crowded *simile*, or the mixed *metaphor* could pass even for the common ear, while the NOTARIES, the EXPOSITORS, and PROMPTERS mentioned above were everywhere at hand and ready to reject the *unnatural style*.

<meta>catchword: 'Tis</meta>

<summary>This page describes how the rise of a critical class, including grammarians and rhetoricians, protected the public from superficial art and deceptive logic. These experts identified genuine beauty in performance while exposing the "unnatural style" and false ornaments used by pretenders.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of how literary tastes develop, specifically addressing the early human preference for the "Sublime" or the "Miraculous" over more refined or subtle styles.</meta>

<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>
<page-num>242</page-num>

<margin>
Part 2.
<image-desc>A horizontal wavy line with curled ends.</image-desc>
</margin>

It is easy to imagine that, among the several styles and manners of discourse or writing, the easiest attained and earliest practiced was the *miraculous*, the *pompous*, or what we generally call the SUBLIME. *Astonishment* is, of all other passions, the easiest raised in raw and inexperienced mankind. Children in their earliest infancy are entertained in this manner; and the known way of pleasing such as these is to make them wonder and to lead the way for them in this passion by a feigned surprise at the miraculous objects we set before them. The best *music* of *barbarians* consists of hideous and astonishing sounds. And the fine sights of *Indians* are enormous figures, various odd and glaring colors, and whatever else of that sort is beheld with amazement, with a kind of horror and consternation.

In poetry and studied prose, the *astonishing* part, or what commonly passes for the *sublime*, is formed by the variety of figures, the multiplicity of * metaphors, and

<meta>catchword: de</meta>

---

<note>The excellence of diction is to be clear and not base. The clearest diction is that made of ordinary words, but it is base. . . . Dignified and elevated above the common style is that which uses unfamiliar language. By unfamiliar, I mean a rare word, a metaphor, an elongated form, and anything that deviates from the standard. But if someone makes all such choices at once, it will be either a riddle or a barbarism. If from metaphors, it is a riddle; if from rare words, it is a barbarism. Aristotle.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the "Sublime" or "miraculous" style is the most primitive form of art, appealing to children and "barbarians" through shock and astonishment. It notes that this style is easily achieved through the heavy use of metaphors and figures of speech.</summary>
<keywords>Sublime, aesthetics, astonishment, metaphors, Aristotle, Poetics, literary style, cultural development</keywords>

and by abandoning as much as possible the natural and easy way of expression, for that which is most unlike humanity, or ordinary use. This the prince of critics assures us was the manner of the earliest poets, before the age of HOMER; or until such time as this father-poet came into repute, who deposed that spurious race, and gave rise to a legitimate and genuine kind. He retained only what was appropriate of the *figurative* or *metaphorical* style, introduced the *natural* and *simple*; and turned his thoughts toward the *real* beauty of composition, the unity of design, the truth of characters, and the just imitation of nature in each particular.

**The** style of this Father-Poet was afterwards variously imitated and divided into several shares, especially when it came to be copied in **dramatic** works. **Tragedy** came first and took what was most *solemn* and *sublime*. In this part, the poets succeeded sooner than in **Comedy** or the *humorous* kind, as was natural indeed to suppose, since this was in reality the easier manner of the *two* and capable of being brought the soonest to perfection. For so the same Prince of Critics sufficiently informs us. And it is highly worth remarking what this mighty genius and judge of art declares concerning **Tragedy**: that whatever idea might be formed of the utmost perfection of this kind of poem, it could in practice rise no higher than it had been already carried in his time; † “Having at length (says he) attained its ends, and being evidently

COMEDY * had at this time done little more than what the ancient † *parodies* had done before it. The good tragedians themselves could hardly escape its *lashes*. The pompous orators were its never-failing *subjects*. <meta>This page continues the discussion of how the natural growth of a free society provides its own remedies for cultural excesses, specifically describing how "comic genius" was used to temper the "high style" of the Greeks.</meta>

<margin>Part 2.</margin>provides for the cure of what has happened amiss in the growth and progress of a constitution. The affairs of this free people being on the rise, and their ability and judgment improving every day as letters and arts advanced, they would naturally find in themselves a strength of nature. This strength, with the help of good ferments and a wholesome opposition of humors, would correct in one way whatever was excessive or *peccant* (as *physicians* say) in another. Thus the florid and over-sanguine humor of the *high style* was tempered by something of a contrary nature. The *comic genius* was applied, like a kind of *caustic*, to those exuberances and *growths* of the swollen dialect and magnificent manner of speech. But after a while, even this remedy itself was found to turn into a disease, just as we know medicines become corrosive when the fouler matters they acted upon are sufficiently purged and the obstructions removed.

<margin>* Horace, *Art of Poetry*.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the refinement of Athenian comedy was an internal cultural evolution driven by an increase in civil liberty and the development of public taste.</meta>

<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>
Part 2.
</margin>

Nothing, therefore, could have been the cause of these public *decrees*, and of this gradual reform in the commonwealth of wit, besides the real reform of *taste* and *humor* in the commonwealth or government itself. Instead of any abridgment, it was in reality an increase of *liberty*, an enlargement of the security of *property*, and an advancement of private ease and personal *safety*, to provide against what was injurious to the good name and reputation of every citizen. As this intelligence in life and manners grew greater in that experienced people, so the relish of wit and humor would naturally, in proportion, be more refined. Thus Greece in general grew more and more polite; and as it advanced in this respect, was more averse to the obscene buffooning manner. The Athenians still went before the rest, and led the way in elegance of every kind. For even their first comedy was a refinement upon some irregular attempts which had been made in that dramatic way. And the grand * *Critic* shows us, that in his own time the PHALLICA, or *scurrilous and obscene farce*, prevailed still, and had the counte-

But what is yet more undeniable evidence of this *natural* and *gradual* refinement of styles and manners among the ancients—particularly in what concerned their stage—is that this very case of prohibition and restraint happened among the ROMANS themselves, where no effects of foreign power or of a domestic tyranny can be claimed. Their FESCENNINE and ATELLAN way of wit was prohibited in early days, and *laws* were made against it *for the public's sake and in regard to the welfare of the community*, such *licentiousness* having been found in reality contrary to the just liberty of the people.

## Part 2.
<image-desc>Decorative calligraphic flourish underlining "Part 2."</image-desc>
So the philosophical PATRIARCH, in the same manner, containing within himself the several characters of philosophy, gave rise to all those different methods in which that science was delivered.

HIS disciple of noble birth and lofty genius, who aspired to *poetry and rhetoric, took the *sublime* part, and shone above his other fellow students. He of humble birth and the poorest circumstances—whose temperament as well as condition inclined him most to the way we call *satirical*—took the reproving part. In his better-humored and more agreeable successor, this turned into the *comic* kind and followed the model of that † ancient comedy which was then prevalent. But another noble disciple, whose genius was directed toward action, and who later proved to be the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Socrates' disciples, focusing on the character and style of Xenophon, who represents the "gentlemanly" approach to philosophy, and then introduces the concept of the "simple" style of antiquity and the origins of formal criticism.</meta>

<margin>Sect. 2.</margin>
The greatest hero of his time took the more *genteel* part and *softer* manner. He joined what was deepest and most solid in philosophy with what was easiest and most refined in breeding, and in the character and manner of a gentleman. Nothing could be more remote than his genius was from the scholastic, the rhetorical, or the purely poetic kind. He was as distant, on the one hand, from the sonorous, high, and pompous strain as he was, on the other hand, from the ludicrous, mimical, or satirical.

THIS * was that natural and *simple* genius of antiquity, understood by so few and so little relished by the common people. This was that philosophical MENANDER of earlier times, whose works one may wonder to see preserved from the same fate; since in the darker ages through which they passed, they might probably have been equally neglected on account of their similar simplicity of style and composition.

THERE is, besides the several *manners* of writing described above, another of considerable authority and weight, which had its rise chiefly from the critical art itself and from the more accurate inspection into the works of preceding masters. The grand critic, of whom we have

already mentioned, was a chief and leader in this order of writers. For although the **SOPHISTS** of earlier times had treated many subjects *methodically* and *formally*, yet this writer was the first who gained a reputation in the *methodical* style. Since the talent of this great man was directed more toward polite learning and the arts than toward the deep and solid parts of philosophy, it happened that in his school, more care was taken of other sciences than of *ethics*, *dialectics*, or *logic*; these fields were chiefly cultivated by the successors of the Academy and the Porch.

IT has been observed of this *methodical* or *scholastic* manner that it naturally suited an author who, though endowed with a comprehensive and strong genius, was not in himself of a refined temperament, blessed by the *Graces*, or favored by any *Muse*; one who was not of a fruitful imagination, but rather dry and rigid; yet at the same time acute and piercing, accurate and distinct. For the chief strength and core of this style consists in the clear division and partition of the subjects. Though there is nothing *exalting* in this manner, it is naturally powerful and *commanding*; and, more than any other, it subdues the mind and strengthens its determinations. It is from this type of genius that firm conclusions and steady maxims are best formed: which, if solidly built, and

Now, though every other style and genuine manner of composition has its order and method, as well as this which, in a specific sense, we call *the methodical*; yet it is this manner alone which professes method, dissects itself into parts, and performs its own anatomy. *The Sublime* can in no way condescend like this, or bear to be suspended in its impetuous course. *The Comic*, or derisive manner, is even further from making a show of method. It is only then, if ever, that it presumes to give itself this air of wisdom—when its design is to expose the thing itself and ridicule the formality and sophistry so often sheltered beneath it. *The Simple Manner*, which, being the strictest imitation of nature, should by right be the most complete in the distribution of its parts and the symmetry of its whole, is yet so far from making any display of method that it conceals the artifice as much as possible: endeavoring only to express the effect of art under the appearance of the greatest ease and negligence. And even when it assumes the censuring or reproving part,

The authors of our age, indeed, are as little capable of receiving advice as they are of giving it in such a way as this; so little is the general palate yet formed to a taste of real SIMPLICITY. As for the SUBLIME, although it is often the subject of criticism, it can never be the manner itself, nor provide the means. The way of *form* and METHOD—the *didactic* or *preceptive* manner, as it has usually been practiced among us and as our ears have long been accustomed—has so little force in winning our attention that it is more likely to tire us than the meter of an old ballad. We no sooner hear the *theme* proposed and the *subject* divided and subdivided (with *first* of the *first*, and so forth, as *order* requires) than we instantly begin a struggle with *nature*, who might otherwise surprise us in the soft chains of sleep—to the great disgrace of the orator and the scandal of the audience. The only manner left in which criticism can have its proper force among us is the *ancient* COMIC; of this kind were the first *Roman Miscellanies* or *Satiric Pieces*. These were a sort of original writing of their own, later refined by the greatest genius and most polished poet of that nation, who, nevertheless, admits that the style

In whatever form or manner *criticism* may appear among us, or CRITICS choose to exert their talent, it is suitable for none but the grossly superstitious or ignorant to be alarmed by this *spirit*. For if it is poorly managed and has little wit, it will be destroyed by something wittier of its kind; if it is witty itself, it must of necessity advance wit.

And thus, from the consideration of ancient as well as modern times, it appears that the *cause* and *interest* of CRITICS is the same as that of wit, learning, and good sense.

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin>
THERE is nothing more certain than that a real *Genius* and thorough *Artist*, in whatever kind, can never, without the greatest unwillingness and shame, be induced to act below his character, or for mere interest be prevailed upon to prostitute his *Art* or *Science* by performing contrary to its known rules. Whoever has heard anything of the lives of famous *statuaries*, *architects*, or *painters* will call to mind many instances of this nature. Or whoever has made any acquaintance with the better sort of *mechanics*, such as are real lovers of their art and *masters* in it, must have observed their natural fidelity in this respect. Be they ever so idle, dissolute, or debauched; no matter how regardless of other rules; they abhor any transgression in their *art*, and would choose to lose customers and starve rather than, by a base compliance with *the* WORLD, act contrary to what they call the *justness* and *truth* of *work*.

If the early poets of GREECE had thus complimented their nation by complying with its first relish and appetite, they would not have done their countrymen such service, nor themselves such honor as we find they did, by conforming to truth and nature. The noble spirits who first attempted the way did not always have *the* WORLD on their side: but they soon drew the best *JUDGMENTS* after them; and soon afterward the WORLD itself. They forced their way into it, and by the weight of merit turned its judgment to their side.

Nor were they disappointed in their hope. The applause soon came and was lasting, for it was sound. They have justice done to them today. They have survived their nation and live, though in a dead language. The more the age is enlightened, the more they shine. Their fame must necessarily last as long as letters, and posterity will ever acknowledge their merit.

Our modern authors, on the contrary, are turned and modeled (as they themselves confess) by the public taste and the current humor of the times. They regulate themselves by the irregular fancy of the world and frankly own they are preposterous and absurd in order to accommodate themselves to the genius of the age. In our days, the *audience* makes the *poet*, and the *bookseller* makes the *author*. With what profit to the *public*, or what prospect of lasting fame and honor to the *writer*, let anyone who has judgment imagine.

But though our *writers* charge their faults thus freely on the PUBLIC, I suspect it will appear from many instances that this practice is mere imposture:

We are glad to accept what our language can offer us; and by a sort of *emulation* of other nations, we are forced to praise such writers of our own as may best serve us for comparison. But when we are not in this spirit, it must be admitted that we are not inclined to show any great fondness or admiration for our authors. Nor do we have any whom, by mutual consent, we make to be our *standard*. We go to *plays* or to other *shows*, and frequent the theater as if it were a market booth. We read *epics* and *dramas* just as we do *satires* and *lampoons*. For we must necessarily know what *wit* as well as what *scandal* is circulating. Read we must, however mediocre the writers may be. And this perhaps may be a cause of the laziness and negligence of our authors; who, observing this need which our curiosity brings upon us, and making an exact calculation in the way of trade to know precisely the quality and quantity of the public demand, feed us thus from hand to mouth. They resolve not to overstock the market or take the trouble of providing more correctness or wit than is absolutely necessary to carry on the trade.

Our satire, therefore, is scurrilous, buffoonish, and without morals or instruction, which are the majesty and life of this kind of writing. Our *encomium* or *panegyric* is just as fulsome and displeasing because of its debased and abandoned manner of praise. The worthy persons who are the subjects of it may well be considered sufferers by the manner of it. And the public, whether it wants to or not, is forced to make unpleasant reflections when led to them by such *satirizing panegyrists*. For in reality, the nerve and sinew of modern *panegyric* lies in a dull kind of *satire*; which the author, it is true, intends should turn to the advantage of his subject; but which, if I am not mistaken, will appear to have a very contrary effect.

The usual method which our *authors* take, when they would commend either a *fellow author*, a *wit*, a *hero*, a *philosopher*, or a *statesman*, is to look abroad to find, within the narrow compass of their learning, some eminent names of persons who answered to these characters in a former time. These they are sure to lash, as they imagine, with some sharp stroke of *satire*. And when they have stripped these reverend personages of all their share of merit, they think to clothe

I am certain that our *dramatic* poets are strongly inclined in this direction, and delight in creating *havoc* and *destruction* of every kind.

It is indeed alleged by our *stage poets*, as an excuse for vile ribaldry and other gross irregularities in both the plot and language of their plays, that their success—which depends chiefly on the ladies—is never so great as when this *havoc* is wreaked upon virtue and good sense, and their plays are publicly exhibited in this monstrous form. I do not know how they can justify this to the fair sex, speaking (as they claim to) *from experience* and with such a precise distinction regarding their audience. How far this excuse may serve them regarding *common amours* and *love adventures*, I will not take it upon myself to say. But I must admit, I have often wondered to see our *fighting* plays become such popular entertainment for that tender sex.

These same so-called judges, had they flourished in our **Britain** at the time when **Caesar** made his first landing, would have condemned as a *whimsical critic* the man who should have been bold enough to censure our lack of clothing, and laugh at the blue cheeks and multi-colored skins which were then in fashion among our ancestors. Such must necessarily be the judgment of those who are only *critics* by fashion. But to a just *naturalist* or *humanist*, who knows the creature MAN and judges his growth and improvement in society, it is clearly evident that we **British** men were as barbarous and uncivilized in comparison to the **Romans** under a **Caesar**, as the **Romans** themselves were in comparison to the **Grecians** when they invaded that nation under a **Mummius**.

The noble wits with a court education, who can go no further back into antiquity than their pedigree will carry them, are nonetheless able to recall the different state of manners in a few past reigns, when *chivalry* was in such repute. <margin>*</margin> *Is it a shame to bring a wife to a banquet? Or what matron does not hold the place of honor in her home and appear in public? This is quite different in* GREECE. *For there she is not invited to a banquet except among relatives, nor does she sit anywhere but in the interior part of the house called the gynaconitis* <note>The women's quarters in an ancient Greek house</note>*, where no one enters unless they are a close relative.* CORNELIUS NEPOS in his Preface. See also AELIAN, Book 10, Chapter 1, and the law in PAUSANIAS, Book 5, Chapter 6, where the story from AELIAN is better related regarding the circumstances. *From this, the law orders that women be cast down from a rock if they are found to have entered the Olympic Games, or even to have crossed the Alpheus on those forbidden days. However, they say that no woman was ever caught except one, Callipatira, whom others name Pherenice. After her husband died, she disguised herself as a male athletic trainer and led her son, Pisidorus, to the contest. When he won, she leaped over the barrier where the trainers are kept secluded, and her clothing fell off. When she was recognized as a woman, they cleared her of every crime. This was granted out of the judges' sense of fairness and the glory of her father, brothers, and son, all of whom had emerged as victors from the Olympic Games. From then on, the law decreed that the trainers themselves should also attend the games naked.*

<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<image-desc>A small decorative wavy line or flourish is placed at the beginning of the first paragraph, next to the section label.</image-desc>

Part 2. *English hearts*, and has perhaps been performed more often than any other play on our stage, is almost one continuous *moral*; a series of deep reflections, spoken by *one voice*, regarding a *single* accident and calamity, naturally suited to evoke horror and compassion. It may be accurately said of this play, if I am not mistaken, that it has only ONE *character* or *principal part*. It contains no adoration or flattery of *women*; no ranting at the *gods*; no blustering *heroism*; nor any of that strange mixture of the *fierce* and *tender* which serves as the turning point of modern tragedy, delicately shifting between the themes of *love* and *honor*.

OVERALL: Since in the two great poetic forms, the *epic* and *dramatic*, we can observe the moral spirit to be so naturally dominant; and since our * most approved *heroic poem* has neither softness of language nor the fashionable wit of the day, but relies solely on solid thought, strong reasoning, noble passion, and a continuous thread of moral doctrine, piety, and virtue to recommend it; we may rightly conclude that it is not so much the *public taste* as the *clumsy hand* and *corrupt style* of our poets which needs correction.
