### Sect. 3.
<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish line is drawn underneath the section heading.</image-desc>

AND thus, at last, we have returned to our old topic of ADVICE—that primary requirement of *self-study* and *inward dialogue* which we have found so lacking in the authors of our time. They should add the wisdom of the *heart* to the task and exercise of the *brain* in order to bring proportion and beauty into their works. So that their composition and style of writing may be natural and free, they should, in the first place, settle matters with themselves. And having gained mastery *here*, they may easily, with the help of their *genius* and a proper use of *art*, command their *audience* and establish a good *taste*.

IT is on *themselves* that everything depends. We have considered their other excuses. We have acquitted the GREAT MEN, their supposed patrons, whom we have left to their own discretion. We have proved the CRITICS to be not only an inoffensive but a highly useful group. And as for the AUDIENCE, we have found it not as bad as might perhaps have been feared at first.

IT remains for us to pass sentence on our *authors*, after having denied them their last refuge. Nor do we condemn

<sig>S 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: them</meta>

<summary>Shaftesbury returns to his central theme that authors must practice self-study and internal moral discipline to produce high-quality work. He argues that since patrons, critics, and audiences have been vindicated, the responsibility for the state of literature rests solely with the authors themselves.</summary>

<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Part 2.</margin>them for their lack of *wit* or *imagination*, but for their lack of *judgment* and *accuracy*, which can only be attained through thorough *diligence*, *study*, and an impartial *scrutiny* of themselves. It is \* MANNERS which are lacking. It is a proper sense of MORALS that alone can make us knowledgeable in order and proportion, and give us the correct tone and measure of human passion.

The *poet* must necessarily borrow enough from the *philosopher* to be a master of the *common* TOPICS of morality. He must at least be *seemingly* honest and, *to all appearances*, a friend to virtue throughout his poem. The *good* and the *wise* will grant him no concessions in this regard. And the *public*, though corrupt, are generally most satisfied with this conduct.

# PART III.

## S E C T. I.

It is esteemed the highest compliment which can be paid a *writer*, on the occasion of some new work he has made public, to tell him, "That he has undoubtedly *surpassed* HIMSELF." And indeed, when one observes how well this compliment is received, one would imagine it to contain some wonderful *hyperbole* of praise. For according to the strain of modern politeness, it is not an ordinary violation of truth which can afford a tribute sufficient to answer any common degree of *merit*. Now it is well known that the gentlemen whose merit lies toward *authorship* are unwilling to make the least abatement on the basis of this ceremonial. One would wonder, therefore, to find them so entirely satisfied with a form of praise which, in plain sense, amounts to no more than a bare affirmative, "That they have in some manner differed from themselves, and are

<meta>catchword: “become</meta>

<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Part 3.</margin>
are ourselves. It is not WE who change when our complexion or shape changes. But there is *that* which, being wholly metamorphosed and converted, causes US to be in reality transformed and lost.

SHOULD an intimate friend of ours, who had endured many sicknesses and undergone many bad adventures while he traveled through the remotest parts of the East and the hottest countries of the South, return to us so altered in his whole outward figure that, until we had conversed with him for a time, we could not recognize him as the same person; the matter would not seem so very strange, nor would our concern on this account be very great. But should a similar face and figure of a friend return to us with thoughts and humors of a strange and foreign turn, with passions, affections, and opinions wholly different from anything we had formerly known, we should say in earnest, and with the greatest amazement and concern, that this was *another creature*, and not the *friend* whom we once knew familiarly. Nor should we in reality attempt any renewal of acquaintance or correspondence with such a person, though perhaps he might preserve in his memory the faint marks or tokens of former transactions which had passed between us.

I once knew a notable *enthusiast* of the itinerant kind, who being upon a high spiritual adventure in a country where prophetic missions are treated as no joke, was, as he told me, committed as a close prisoner, and kept for several months where he saw no manner of light. In this banishment from letters and discourse, the man very wittily invented an amusement much to his purpose, and highly preservative both of health and humor. It may be thought perhaps, that of all seasons or circumstances here was one the most suitable to our oft-mentioned practice of SOLILOQUY; especially since the prisoner was one of those whom in this age we usually call *philosophers*, a successor of PARACELSUS, and a master in the occult sciences. But as to *moral science*, or anything relating to *self-converse*, he was a mere *novice*. To work therefore he went, after a different method. He tuned his natural pipes not after the manner of a musician, to practice what was melodious and agreeable in sounds, but to fashion and form all sorts of articulate voices as distinctly as possible. This he performed by

strenuously raising his voice and testing it in all the various arrangements and configurations of his throat and mouth. And thus bellowing, roaring, snarling, and otherwise variously exerting his organs of sound, he endeavored to discover what letters of the alphabet could best designate each species, or what new letters were to be invented to mark the undiscovered modifications. He found, for instance, the letter *A* to be a most genuine character, an original and pure vowel, and justly placed as principal at the front of the alphabetical order. For having duly extended his lower jaw to its utmost distance from the upper; and by a proper insertion of his fingers provided against the contraction of either corner of his mouth; he experimentally discovered it impossible for the human tongue under these circumstances to emit any other modification of sound than that which was described by this primitive character. The vowel *O* was formed by an orbicular arrangement of the mouth, as was aptly delineated in the character itself. The vowel *U* by a parallel protrusion of the lips. The other vowels and consonants by various other collisions of the mouth and operations of the active tongue upon the passive gum or palate. The result of this profound speculation and long exercise of our prisoner was a *Philosophical Treatise,*

He esteemed himself the only Master of Voice and Language on account of this, his *radical science*, and *fundamental knowledge* of sounds. But whoever had taken him to improve their voice, or teach them an agreeable or just manner of accent or delivery, would, I believe, have found themselves considerably deluded.

It is not that I would condemn this speculative science of *articulation* as useless. It has its place, no doubt, among the other sciences, and may serve *grammar*, as *grammar* serves *rhetoric*, and other arts of speech and writing. The solidity of *mathematics*, and its advantage to mankind, is proved by many effects in those beneficial arts and sciences which depend on it; though *astrologers*, *horoscopers*, and other such people, are pleased to honor themselves with the title of *mathematicians*. As for *metaphysics*, and that which in the schools is taught as *logic* or *ethics*; I shall willingly allow it to pass for *philosophy*, when by any real effects it is proved capable of refining our spirits, improving our understandings, or mending our manners. But if defining *material* and *immaterial substances*, and distinguishing their *properties* and *modes*, is recommended to us as the right manner

THE study of triangles and circles does not interfere with the study of *minds*. Nor does the student, in the meantime, suppose himself to be advancing in wisdom, or the knowledge of himself or mankind. All he desires is to keep his head as sound as it was before. And he thinks he has come off quite well, indeed, if by good fortune no crack has been made in it. As for other abilities or improvements in the knowledge of human nature or the world, he refers himself to other studies and practices. Such is the *mathematician’s* modesty and good sense. But for the *philosopher*, who pretends to be wholly taken up in considering his higher faculties, and examining the powers and principles of his understanding; if in reality his philosophy is foreign to the matter professed; if it misses the mark and reaches nothing we can truly call our interest or concern; it must be something worse than mere ignorance or idiocy. The most ingenious way of becoming foolish is *by a system*. And the surest method to prevent good sense is to set up something in its place. The more like any

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin>
**ONE** would expect of these *Physiologists* and searchers of *Modes* and *Substances* that, being so exalted in their understandings and enriched with science above other men, they should be as much above them in their passions and sentiments. The consciousness of being admitted into the secret recesses of nature and the inward resources of a human heart should, one would think, create in these gentlemen a sort of magnanimity which might distinguish them from the ordinary race of mortals. But if their pretended knowledge of the machine of *this world* and of *their own frame* is able to produce nothing beneficial either to the one or to the other, I know not to what purpose such a philosophy can serve, except only to shut the door against better knowledge and introduce impertinence and conceit with the best appearance of authority.

It is hardly possible for a student, but more especially *an author*, who has dealt in *Ideas* and treated formally of the *Passions* in a way of *natural philosophy*, not to imagine himself wiser on this account, and more knowledgeable in his own character and the genius of mankind.

But experience generally convinces us that he is mistaken in his calculation: none are found more weak in themselves, having less command over their passions, less free from superstition and vain fears, or less safe from common deception and delusion, than the famous thinkers of this type. Nor is this a surprise. The theory, in a way, indicates the practice. No formal proof is needed to make this evident. A small help from our familiar method of *soliloquy* may serve the purpose; and we may perhaps decide this matter in a more entertaining way by confronting this overly-theoretical philosophy with a more practical kind, which relates mainly to our acquaintance, friendship, and good relationship with *ourselves*.

On this account, it may not be to my reader’s disadvantage if, forgetting him for a while, I apply myself chiefly to *myself*; and, as the opportunity arises, take up that *practice of self-conversation* which I have claimed to reveal. It is hoped, therefore, that he will not consider it bad manners if I lose the usual regard for his presence. IF A passerby should happen to turn into a watchmaker’s shop and, intending to inform himself about *watches*, should inquire what metal or what material each part was composed of, what gave them their colors, or what made the sounds, without examining what the real use was of such an instrument, or by what movements its *purpose* was best achieved and its perfection acquired, it is clear that such an examiner would fall short of any understanding of the real nature of the instrument. Should a philosopher, in the same way, while employing himself in the study of human nature, discover only what effects each passion had on the body, what change of appearance or features they produced, and in what different manner they affected the limbs and muscles, this might possibly qualify him to give advice to an anatomist or a <term>limner</term> <note>A painter, specifically one who paints portraits or miniatures.</note>, but not to *mankind* or to *himself*. Since, according to this survey, he did not consider the real operation or energy of his subject, nor did he contemplate *man* as a *real* man and as a human agent, but rather as a *watch* or an *ordinary machine*.

> “The passion of *fear* (as a *modern

But whether the knocking together of the knees is any more the cowardly symptom of *flight* than the chattering of the teeth is the bold symptom of *resistance*, I shall not take it upon myself to determine. In this entire subject of inquiry, I shall find nothing of the least *personal concern*. And I may depend upon it that by the most refined speculation of this kind, I shall neither learn to diminish my fears nor raise my courage. This, however, I may be assured of: that it is the nature of fear, as well as of other passions, to have its increase and decrease as it is fed by *opinion* and influenced by custom and practice.

THESE passions, according to how they have the ascendency in me and differ in proportion to one another, affect my character and make me different with respect to *myself* and others. I must,

<page-num>295</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin>
therefore, of necessity find relief and improvement in this case, by reflecting correctly on the nature of my own *motion*, as guided by *feelings* which depend so much on apprehension and imagination. By examining the various turns, changes, declines, and inward revolutions of the *passions*, I must undoubtedly come to better understand the human heart, and judge both others and *myself* more effectively. It is impossible to make the slightest progress in such a study without gaining some advantage from the regulation and government of those passions on which the conduct of a life depends.

FOR instance, if SUPERSTITION is the type of fear which oppresses me most, it is not very important to inquire, on this occasion, to which parts or areas the blood or spirits are immediately sent, or where they are made to assemble. For it is no more important for me to understand this than it is within my power to regulate or change it. But when the foundations of this superstitious fear are understood to arise from *opinion*, and its subjects are thoroughly searched and examined, the passion itself must necessarily diminish as I discover more and more the deception that belongs to it.

<summary>The author argues that self-improvement is achieved by analyzing the psychological origins of passions like superstition rather than their physical manifestations in the body. By examining the beliefs and "opinions" that cause fear, an individual can diminish the emotion's power by recognizing its inherent falsehood.</summary>
<keywords>self-reflection, passions, superstition, moral philosophy, psychology, opinion, reason, self-government</keywords>

In the same manner, if **Vanity** comes from *Opinion*, and I consider how *Vanity* is conceived, from what imaginary **Advantages** and insignificant **Grounds**; if I view it in its excessive height, as well as in its opposite depression; it is impossible that I should not in some measure be relieved of this disorder.

> \* *Laudis amore tumes? Sunt certa Piacula——*
> *Sunt verba and voces quibus hunc lenire dolorem*
> *Possis, and magnam morbi deponere partem.*

<margin>\* Horace, *Epistles*, book 1, epistle 1.</margin>

**The** same must happen in respect of *Anger*, *Ambition*, *Love*, *Desire*, and the other passions from which I form the different notion I have of *Interest*. For as these passions shift, my *interest* shifts, my *steerage* varies; and I alternately make now this, now that, my *course* and *harbor*. The man in anger has a different *happiness* from the man in love. And the man who has recently become greedy has a different notion of *satisfaction* from what he had before, when he was generous. The examination, therefore, of my *humors*, and the *INQUIRY* after my *passions*, must necessarily involve the search and scrutiny of my *opinions*, and the sincere consideration of my *scope* and *end*. THIS is the *philosophy* which, by nature, has preeminence over all other science or knowledge. Nor can this surely be of the sort called † *vain* or *deceitful*; since it is the only means by which I can discover vanity and deceit. This is not of that kind which depends on † *genealogies* or *traditions*, and ‡ *promotes questions and meaningless bickering*. **AGAIN.** What are my *ideas of the world, of pleasure, riches, fame, life?* What judgment am I to make of mankind and human affairs? What sentiments am I to frame? What opinions? What maxims? > "Divide a solid body of whatever dimension (says a renowned modern philosopher), and it will be impossible for the parts to move within the bounds of its surfaces if there be not left in it * a void space as big as the smallest part into which the said body is divided." —

THUS the *atomist*, or *Epicurean*, pleading for a *vacuum*. The *plenitudinarian*, on the other side, brings his *fluid* into play and joins the idea of *body* and *extension*.

> "Of this," says one, "I have clear ideas."
> "Of this," says the other, "I can be certain."

“And what,” I say, “if in the whole matter there is no certainty at all?” For *mathematicians* are divided, and *mechanics* proceed as well on one *hypothesis* as on the other. I am satisfied that my *mind* will proceed either way alike, for it is concerned on neither side. —— “Philosopher! Let me hear about what is of some importance to me. Let me hear about *life*; what the right notion is, and what I am to stand by when the occasion arises, so that I may not—when *life* seems to be retreating or has run itself out to the very dregs—cry *'Vanity!'*, condemn the world, and at the same time complain that *life is short and passing!*” For why is it so *short* indeed, if it is not found *sweet*? Why do I complain both ways? Is *vanity*, mere *vanity*, a *happiness*? Or can *misery* *pass away too soon?*

This is of importance for me to examine. This is worth my while. If, on the other hand, I cannot find the *agreement* or *disagreement* of my *ideas* in this matter; if I can come to nothing *certain* here; what is all the rest to me? What does it signify how I come by my *ideas*, or how I *compound* them; which are *simple* and which are *complex*? > "to himself, without appearing in *public*,
> or before the *world*."

In answer to this, I shall only say that, regarding appearing in *public* or before the *world*, I do not easily understand what our worthy objector may mean by it. I can indeed recall among my acquaintances certain merchant-adventurers in the trade of writing who, in correspondence with their agent-booksellers, have entered into a notable commerce with *the world*. They have directly, and in the proper form of a *preface* and *dedicatory epistle*, solicited the *public* and lobbied *friends* for favor and protection in this regard. They have perhaps ventured to link some great man's reputation with their own, having obtained his permission to dedicate a work to him on the assumption that it would be seen as something significant in the eyes of *mankind*. One may easily imagine that such patronized and acknowledged *authors* as these would be deeply disappointed if *the public* took no notice of their labors. But for my own part, it is of no concern to me how the *public* regards my amusements, or in what way it becomes acquainted with what I write for *my* private entertainment, or as a way of giving *advice* to those of my acquaintances who have embarked on such a desperate course.

It is necessary that my friends, who read these *Advices*, should read them in better characters than those of my own handwriting. And by good luck, I have a very fair hand offered, which may save me the trouble of re-copying, and can readily furnish me with as many handsome copies as I would desire for my own and my friends' service. I have not, indeed, forbidden my *Amanuensis* from making as many as he pleases for his own benefit. What I write is not worth being made a mystery. And if it is worth anyone's purchasing, much good may it do the purchaser. It is a *trade I have no share in*, though I accidentally furnish the subject matter.

And thus I am in no way more of an author just for being in *print*. I am conscious of no additional *virtue* or dangerous quality from having lain at any time under the weight of that alphabetical engine called *the press*. I know of no conjuration in it, regarding either church or state. Nor can I imagine why the machine should appear so formidable to scholars and renowned *clerks*, whose very craft and foundation depend on the manufacture of letters. To allow *benefit of clergy* and yet to restrain *the press* seems to me to have something of a cross-purpose in it. I can hardly think that *the quality*

Care and caution are instilled into tender minds; yet the manner of this anticipating philosophy may make the later work of reason, and the inward exercise of the mind at a more mature age, proceed more heavily and with greater reluctance.

IT must certainly be a hard case for us, after having passed through such a learned childhood and been instructed in our own and other higher *natures*, *essences*, *incorporeal substances*, *personalities*, and the like, to stoop at a more mature age to ruminate and study this lesson a second time. It is hard, after having declared *who* and *what* we are through so many pertinent questions and decisive sentences, to come leisurely, from another perspective, to inquire concerning our real SELF and END; the *judgment* we are to make of INTEREST, and the *opinion* we should have of ADVANTAGE and GOOD. These are what must necessarily determine our conduct and prove to be the leading principles of our lives.

CAN we bear looking anew into these *mysteries*? Can we endure a new *schooling*, after having once learned our lesson from the *world*? Hardly, I presume. For by the lesson of this latter *school*, and according to the sense I acquire in conversation with *prominent men*, should I at any time

ask myself, *What governed me?* I should answer readily, *My interest.* "But what is *interest*? And how is it governed? By opinion and fancy. Is everything, therefore, my interest which I fancy to be such? Or may my fancy possibly be wrong? It may. If my fancy of interest, therefore, is wrong, can my pursuit or aim be right? Hardly so. Can I then be supposed to *hit* the mark, when I do not know, in reality, even *how to aim?*"

MY chief **interest**, it seems therefore, must be to *find an aim*; and to know certainly where my happiness and advantage lie. "Where else can it lie, than in *my pleasure*; since my advantage and good must always be *pleasing*: and what is *pleasing*, can never be anything other than my advantage and good? Excellent! Let fancy, therefore, govern, and let *interest* be what *we please*. For if that which *pleases* us is our good, *because it pleases us*; anything may be our INTEREST OR GOOD. Nothing can come amiss. That which we fondly make our *happiness* at one time, we may as readily unmake at another. No one can learn what *real* GOOD is. Nor can anyone on this basis be said to *understand his INTEREST*."

HERE, we see, are strange embroils!
—But let us try to deal more candidly with ourselves, and frankly own that \* *Pleasure is no rule of* GOOD; since when we follow *pleasure* merely, we are disgusted, and change from one sort to another: condemning that at one time, which at another we earnestly approve; and never judging equally of *happiness*, while we follow *passion* and mere *humor*.

A LOVER, for instance, when struck with the *idea* or *fancy* of his enjoyment, promises himself the highest felicity, if he succeeds in his *new amour*.—He succeeds in it; finds not the felicity he expected: but promises himself the same again *in some other*.—The same thing happens: He is disappointed as before; but still has *faith*.—Wearied with this game, he quits the chase; renounces the way of courtship and *intrigue*, and detests the ceremony and difficulty of the pleasure.—A *new* species of amours invites him. Here too he meets the same inquietude and inconstancy.—Scorning to grow *sottish*, and plunge into the lowest sink of vice, he shakes off his intemperance; despises *gluttony* and *riot*; and listens to *ambition*. He becomes a man of business, and seeks authority and fame.—

LEST this, therefore, should be my own case; let me see whether I can control my *Fancy*, and fix it, if possible, on something which may hold good. — When I exercise my reason in *moral* subjects; when I employ my affection in *friendly* and *social* actions, I find I can sincerely enjoy *myself*. If there is a pleasure, therefore, of this kind, why not indulge it? Or what harm would there be, supposing it should grow greater by indulgence? If I am *lazy*, and indulge myself in languid pleasure, I know the harm and can foresee the *drone*. If I am luxurious, I know the harm of this also, and have the plain prospect of *the sot*. If avarice is my pleasure, the end, I know, is being a *miser*. But if HONESTY is my delight, I know no other consequence from indulging such a passion than that of growing better *natured* and enjoying more and more the *pleasures of society*. On the other hand, if this honest pleasure is lost by knavish indulgence and immorality, there can hardly be a satisfaction left of any kind; since good nature and † social affection

If, therefore, the only pleasure I can freely and without reserve indulge is that of the *honest* and *moral* kind; if rational and social enjoyment is so constant in itself and so essential to happiness; why should I not bring my other pleasures to correspond and be friends with it, rather than create for myself other pleasures which are destructive of this foundation and have no manner of correspondence with one another?

UPON this foundation, let me try how I can bear the assault of FANCY and maintain myself in my moral fortress against the attacks which are raised on the side of corrupt *interest* and a wrong *self*. When the idea of pleasure strikes, I ask myself: “Before I was thus struck by the idea, was anything amiss with me? No. Therefore, remove the idea, and I am well. But having this idea such as I now have, I cannot lack the thing without regret. See, therefore, which is best: either to suffer under this lack until the idea is removed, or by satisfying the want, confirm not only this idea, but all others of the same stamp!”

<sig>4</sig>
<meta>catchword: IN</meta>

<summary>The author argues for aligning all pleasures with honesty and social enjoyment to create a stable foundation for happiness. He proposes a method of internal dialogue to resist the "assaults of fancy" and corrupt interests by recognizing that the desire for a pleasure is often more troublesome than its absence.</summary>

IN reality, does not *every* FANCY have a similar privilege of passing, if *any single one* is admitted on its own authority? And what must be the result of such an economy, if the whole fantastic *crew* is introduced, and the door is refused to none? What else is it but this management which leads to the most dissolute and profligate of characters? What is it, on the contrary, which raises us to any degree of worth or steadiness, besides a directly contrary practice and conduct? Can there be *strength of mind*; can there be *command over oneself*; if the ideas of pleasure, the suggestions of *fancy*, and the strong pleadings of appetite and desire are not often withstood, and the imaginations soundly reprimanded and brought under subjection?

Thus it appears that the method of examining our *ideas* is no pedantic practice. Nor is there anything un-gallant in the manner of thus questioning the *Lady-Fancies*, who present themselves as charmingly dressed as possible to solicit their cause and obtain a judgment by favor of that worse *part* and corrupt SELF to whom they make their application.

It may be justly said of these that they are very powerful *solicitresses*. They

The airs she borrows are from the tragic muse, MELPOMENE. Nor is she in her own person in any way amiable or attractive. Far from it. The poorest *means of life* grow in repute when the *ends* and *just conditions* of it are so little known, and the dread of parting with it is raised to such a high degree. The more eagerly we grasp at *life*, the more impotent we are in the enjoyment of it. By this avidity, its very lees and dregs are swallowed. The ideas of sordid pleasure are advanced. Worth, manhood, generosity, and all the nobler opinions and sentiments of *honest* GOOD and *virtuous pleasure* disappear and fly before this *Queen of Terrors*.

It is a mighty delight which a sort of counter-philosophers take in seconding this *phantom* and playing her upon our understandings whenever they would take occasion to confound them. The vicious poets employ this *specter* too on their side, though in a different manner. By the help of this tragic actress, they gain a fairer audience for luxurious fancies and give their Eratos and other playsome *muses* a fuller scope in the support of riot and debauchery. The gloomy prospect of death becomes the incentive to pleasures of the lowest order. *Ashes* and *shade*, the *tomb* and *cypress*, are made to serve as foils to *luxury*.

It is no wonder if *luxury* profits from the deformity of this specter-opinion. She supports her interest with this childish bugbear; and, like a mother by her infant, she is hugged so much the closer by her follower as the fear presses him and grows persistent. She invites him to *live fast*, according to her best measure of *life*. And well she might. Who would not willingly make *life* pass away as quickly as possible, when its nobler pleasures were already lost or corrupted by a wretched *fear of death*? The intense selfishness and meanness that accompany this fear must reduce us to a low ebb of enjoyment; and in a way bring to nothing that main sum of satisfactory sensation by which we commonly rate the happiness of our private condition and fortune.

shows us what real *Beauty* is, and what those harmonies are which make life perfect and bestow the chief *enjoyment*. She sets *Virtue* before our eyes and teaches us how to rate life based on the experience of the most heroic spirits. She brings her sisters CLIO and URANIA to support her. From the *former*, she borrows whatever is memorable in history and ancient times to confront the tragic specter and show the fixed contempt that the happiest and freest nations—as well as individual heroes and private men worthy of any note—have ever expressed for that impostress. From the *latter*, she borrows what is most sublime in *philosophy* to explain the laws of nature and the order of the universe, and to represent to us the justice of complying with this benevolent administration. She shows us that by this just compliance we are made happiest; and that the measure of a happy life is not taken from the fewer or more suns we behold, the fewer or more breaths we draw, or the meals we repeat, but from having once *lived well*, acted our *part* handsomely, and made our *exit* cheerfully, as was becoming of us.

THUS we retain on *Virtue's* side the noblest party of the *Muses*. Whatever is dignified among those sisters appears readily on our behalf. Nor are the more lighthearted ladies lacking in their assistance when they act in the perfection of their art,

<margin>* Virgil, *Aeneid*, Book 12.</margin>
<note>"Is it so very wretched a thing to die?"</note>

It is not to be imagined how easily we deal with the deluding *apparitions* and false ideas of happiness and good, when this frightful *specter* of misery and ill is, in this manner, well laid and by honest magic conjured down, so as not to give the least assistance to the other tempting *forms*. This is that *occult* science, or sort of counter-necromancy, which instead of ghastliness and horror, inspires only what is gentle and humane, and dispels the imposing phantoms of every kind. He may pass undoubtedly for no mean *conjurer* who can deal with spirits of this sort. But hold! Let us try the experiment in due form and draw the magic circle. Let us observe how the inferior *imps* appear, when the *head-goblin* is securely laid.

SEE! The *enchantress* INDOLENCE presents herself in all the pomp of ease and lazy luxury. > "Characters! Undoubtedly: or it will

This indeed is all too certain: that as long as we possess a mind, as long as we have *appetites* and *senses*, *fancies* of all kinds will be hard at work; and whether we are in company or alone, they must still range and be active. They must have their field. The question is whether they shall have it wholly to themselves, or whether they shall acknowledge some *controller* or *manager*. If none, it is this, I fear, which leads to *madness*. It is this, and nothing else, which can be called *madness* or the *loss of reason*. For if fancy be left as the judge of anything, she must be the judge of all. Everything is right, if anything is right, just because *I fancy it*.
"The house turns around. The view turns.
"No, but my head turns indeed: I
"have a giddiness; that is all. *Fancy*
"would persuade me of this or that: but
"I know better."
It is by means, therefore, of a *controller* and *corrector* of fancy that I am saved from being mad. Otherwise, it is the *house* that turns when I am giddy. It is *things* which change (for so I must suppose) when it is merely my *passion* or *temper* that changes.

“ me this? Who besides the cor-

EVERY man indeed who is not absolutely beside himself must of necessity hold his fancies under some kind of discipline and management. The *stricter* this discipline is, the more the man is rational and in his wits. The *looser* it is, the more fantastical he must be, and the nearer to the madman’s state. This is a business which can never stand still. I must always be a *winner* or *loser* at the game. Either I work upon my *fancies*, or they on *me*. If I give quarter, *they* won't. There can be no truce, no suspension of arms between us. The *one* or the *other* must be superior and have the command. For if the fancies are left to themselves, the government must of course be theirs. And then, what difference is there between such a state and madness?

THE question, therefore, is the same here as in a *family* or *household* when it is asked, “*Who rules?* or *Who is master?*” Learn by the voices. Observe who speaks aloud in a commanding tone; who talks, who questions; or who is talked with and who questioned. For if the servants take the former part, they

<page-num>324</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>
<sig>A</sig>

are the masters, and the government of the house will be found such as might naturally be expected in these circumstances.

<margin>Part 3. }</margin>
How does it stand, therefore, in my own economy, my principal province and command? How do my *fancies* stand? How do they deal with me? Or do I take it upon myself, rather, to deal with *them*? Do I talk, question, or arraign? Or am I talked with, arraigned, and content to listen without giving a reply? If I vote with FANCY, resign my opinion to her command, and judge of happiness and misery as *she* judges, how am I *myself*?

He who, on a plain, imagines *precipices* at his feet and impending *rocks* over his head; who fears bursting *clouds* in a clear sky; who cries *Fire! Deluge! Earthquake!* or *Thunder!* when all is quiet—does he not rave? But one whose eyes seem to strike fire from a blow, one whose head is giddy from the motion of a ship after having newly come ashore, or one who hears thundering noises because of an ailment in his ear, can readily correct these various perceptions and is, by this means, saved from madness.

---

<summary>The author examines the internal management of one's thoughts, arguing that true selfhood requires active command over one's "fancies" or mental impressions. <page-num>325</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author. Sect. 2.</header>

An ailment in my eye may make me see the strangest kind of figures: and when cataracts and other impurities are gathering in that organ, flies, insects, and other various forms seem to be playing in the air before me. But let my senses err ever so widely, I am not on this account *beside myself*: nor am I out of my own possession, while there is a person left within who has the power *to dispute* the appearances and *correct* the imagination.

I am accosted by *ideas* and striking *apprehensions*, but I take nothing on their report. I hear their story and return them an answer as they deserve. FANCY and I are not *one and the same*. The disagreement makes me *my own master*. When, on the contrary, I have no debate with her, no controversy, but accept as *happiness* and *misery*, or for *good* and *ill*, whatever she presents as such; I must then join voices with her and cry, *Precipice! Fire!* CERBERUS! *Elysium!* —

> “Seas of milk, and ships of amber!”

The story is well known. All the artifice employed against the Prince was a well-managed questioning of *what next?* *Lady-Fancy* was not aware of the design upon her; but let herself be drawn out by degrees. At first, she said the Prince’s design was only upon a stretch of land, which stood out like a promontory before him, and seemed to eclipse his glory. A fair rich island, which was close by, presented itself next, and as it were naturally invited conquest. The opposite coast came next in view. Then the continent on each side of the larger sea. And then (what was easiest of all, and would follow of course) the dominion of both sea and land. “And *what next?*” replied the friend. “What shall we do, when we have become thus happy, and have obtained our highest wish?” “Why then, we will sit down peaceably, and be good company over a bottle.” “Alas, Sir! What hinders us from doing the same where we now are? Will our mood, or our wine grow better? Shall we be more secure, or at heart’s

**SOLILOQUY** depends. And whether this is of any use towards making us either wiser or happier; I am confident it must help to make us *wittier* and *more polite*. It must, beyond any other science, teach us the turns of *humor* and *passion*, the variety of *manners*, the justness of *characters*, and the TRUTH of things; which, when we rightly understand them, we may naturally describe. And on this depends chiefly the skill and art of *a good writer*. So that if to *write well* is a just claim to merit; it is plain that *writers*, who are apt to set no small value on their art, must confess there is something valuable in this *self-examining practice*, and method of *inward colloquy*.

As for the *writer of these papers* (as modern *authors* are pleased modestly to style themselves), he is content, for his part, to take up with this practice purely for his own benefit, without regard to the high function or capacity of *Author*. It may be allowed him, in this particular, to imitate the best genius and most gentleman-like of *Roman poets* <note>Likely referring to the poet Horace, known for his conversational and self-reflective style.</note>. And though by an excess of dullness, it should be his misfortune to learn nothing of this poet's *wit*, he is persuaded he may learn something of his *honesty* and *good humor*.

In the gallant world, indeed, we easily find how far a *Humility* of this kind prevails. Those who hope to rise by MERIT are likeliest to be disappointed in their pretensions. The confessing lover, who ascribes everything to the bounty of the fair lady, meets his reward the sooner for having studied *less* how to deserve it. For MERIT is generally thought presumptuous, and supposed to carry with it a certain assurance and ease with which a *mistress* is not so well satisfied. The claim of being well-deserving seems to detract from the pure grace and favor of the *benefactress*; who then appears to herself most sovereign in power, and likeliest to be obeyed without reserve, when she bestows her bounty where there is the least title or claim.

THUS a certain adoration of the female sex, which passes in our age without the least charge of profaneness or idolatry, may, according to common imagination, serve to justify these *gallant devotees* in their imitation of the truly *religious* and *devout*. The method of *self-abasement may perhaps be thought the most proper to make approaches

approaches to the sacred shrines; and the entire resignation of *merit*, in each case, may be esteemed the only ground of well-deserving. But what we allow to *Heaven*, or to *the Fair*, should not, I think, be made a precedent in favor of *the world*. Whatever deference is due to that body of men whom we call *readers*, we may be supposed to treat them with sufficient honor if, with thorough diligence and pains, we endeavor to render our works *perfect*, and leave them to judge of the performance as they are able.

HOWEVER difficult or desperate it may appear in any artist to endeavor to bring *perfection* into his work, if he does not have at least the *idea* of PERFECTION to give him aim, he will be found very defective and mean in his performance. Though his intention is to please the world, he must nevertheless be, in a manner, *above it*, and fix his eye upon that consummate *grace*, that *beauty* of *nature*, and that *perfection* of numbers, which the rest of mankind—feeling only by the *effect* while ignorant of the cause—call the *je ne sais quoi*, the unintelligible, or the "I know not what," and suppose to be a kind of *charm* or *enchantment* of which the artist himself can give no account.

### Sect. 3.

BUT HERE, I find I am tempted to do what I have myself condemned. I can hardly refrain from making some *apology* for my frequent recourse to the rules of common artists, to the masters of exercise, to the academies of painters and statuaries, and to the rest of the *virtuoso* tribe. But in this, I am so fully satisfied that I have reason on my side that, however strong custom may be against me, I would rather resort to these inferior schools to search for TRUTH and NATURE than to some other places where higher arts and sciences are professed.

I AM persuaded that to be a *virtuoso* (so far as befits a gentleman) is a higher step toward becoming a man of virtue and good sense than being what in this age we call <note>It seems indeed somewhat improbable that, according to modern erudition and the way science is now distributed, our ingenious and noble youths should obtain the full advantage of a just and liberal education by uniting the *scholar-part* with that of the real *gentleman* and *man of breeding*. Academies for physical exercises, so useful to the public and essential in the formation of a genteel and liberal character, are unfortunately neglected. Letters are indeed banished—I know not where—to distant cloisters and *unpracticed cells*, as our poet has it, confined to the association and mean *fellowship* of bearded boys.</note> *a scholar*. *boys*. Our youth accordingly seem to have their only chance between two widely different roads: either that of *pedantry* and *school-learning*, which lies amidst the dregs and most corrupt part of ancient literature; or that of the *fashionable illiterate world*, which aims merely at the character of the *fine gentleman* and takes up with the foppery of modern languages and foreign wit. The frightful aspect of the former of these roads makes the journey appear desperate and impracticable. Hence that aversion so generally conceived against a learned character—wrongly shaped and hideously presented under such difficulties and in such seeming labyrinths and mysterious forms. As if a HOMER or a XENOPHON, imperfectly learned in youthful years, might not afterwards, in a riper age, be studied as well in a *capital city* and amidst *the world* as at a *college* or *country town*! Or as if a PLUTARCH, a TULLY, or a HORACE could not accompany a young man in his travels.

They are such Geniuses as have been formed upon the natural Model of the Ancients, and willingly own their Debt to those great Masters. But for the rest, who draw from another Fountain, as the *Italian Authors* in particular; they may be reckoned no better than the Corruptors of true Learning and Erudition; and can indeed be relished by those alone, whose Education has unfortunately denied them the Familiarity of the noble Ancients, and the Practice of a better and more natural *Taste*. And thus the sense of inward numbers, the knowledge and practice of the social *virtues*, and the familiarity and favor of the *moral* GRACES, are essential to the character of a deserving artist and a true favorite of the MUSES. Thus are the *arts* and *virtues* mutual friends: and thus the science of *virtuosos* and that of *virtue* itself become, in a manner, one and the same.

ONE who aspires to the character of a man of breeding and politeness is careful to form his judgment of arts and sciences upon right models of *perfection*. If he travels to ROME, he inquires which are the truest pieces of architecture, the best remains of statues, the best paintings of a RAPHAEL or a CARACHE. However antiquated, rough, or dismal they may appear to him at first sight, he resolves to view them over and over, until he has brought himself to relish them and finds their hidden *graces* and *perfections*. He takes particular care to turn his *eye* from everything which is gaudy, luscious, and of a *false taste*. Nor is he less careful to turn his *ear* from every sort of music except that which is of the best manner and truest harmony.

IT WERE to be wished we had the same regard for a right TASTE in life and manners.

<summary>The author argues that moral virtue and aesthetic expertise are inextricably linked, requiring an artist or person of breeding to study perfected models of art to refine their taste. Just as one learns to appreciate the masters of painting and architecture through repeated study, one should also cultivate a refined taste for virtuous living.</summary>

Manners. What mortal being, once convinced of a difference in *inward Character*, and of a preference due to *one kind* above *another*, would not be concerned to make *his own the best*? > "I please. Grotesque and monstrous figures often *please*. Cruel spectacles and barbarities are also found to *please*, and, in some temperaments, to *please beyond all other subjects*. The luscious colors and glossy paint gain upon my fancy. A *French* or *Flemish style* is highly liked by me at first sight, and I pursue my liking. How is it possible that I should thus come to taste the beauties of an *Italian master*, or of a hand happily formed on nature and the ancients? particular circumstances belonging to the profession. — Antidotus, a disciple of Euphranor, *was more diligent than prolific, and severe in his colors.* — Athenion the Maronite *is compared and somewhat preferred to* Nicias; he was a disciple of Glaucion of Corinth, *and was both more austere in color and more pleasing in his austerity, so that his learning shines out in the painting itself. . . . If he had not died in his youth, no one would be compared to him.* — Aristolaus, *both the son and disciple* of Pausias, *was among the most severe of painters.* — *There was also recently the grave and severe painter* Amulius. . . . *He painted for only a few hours of the day, and even that with gravity, for he always wore a toga, even when on the scaffolding.* One of the mortal symptoms upon which PLINY pronounces the sure death of this noble art (which did not long survive him) was what belonged in common to all the other perishing arts after the fall of liberty; I mean the *luxury* of the ROMAN Court, and the change of *taste* and *manners* naturally resulting from such a change of government and dominion. This excellent, learned, and polite critic represents to us the *false taste* springing from the Court itself, and from that opulence, splendor, and affectation of magnificence and expense proper to the place. Thus in the statuary and architecture then in vogue, nothing could be admired besides what was costly in the mere matter or substance.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the choice of reading materials affects a scholar's taste, while the lower section continues the commentary on the decline of art due to Roman luxury, focusing on the distinction between the "austere" and "florid" styles of painting.</meta>

<page-num>342</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

<margin>
Part 3.
</margin>
...or however polite and agreeable the characters of those with whom we converse or correspond may be: if the *authors* we read are of another kind, we shall find our taste strangely turned their way. We are the unhappier in this respect for being *scholars* if our studies are poorly chosen. For this reason, I cannot think it proper to call a man *well-read* who reads many *authors*, since he must necessarily encounter more bad models than good ones, and be more filled with bombast, poor imagination, and distorted

---

substance of the work. Precious rock, rich metal, glittering stones, and other luscious materials—poisonous to art—came into greater demand every day and were imposed as necessary materials on the best masters. It was in favor of these courtly beauties and gaudy appearances that all good *drawing*, accurate *design*, and *truth of work* began to be despised. Care was taken to procure from distant parts the most gorgeous, splendid colors of the most costly origin or composition—not the kind used by APELLES and the great masters, who were justly severe, loyal, and faithful to their art. This newer coloring our critic calls *the florid kind*. The materials were too expensive to be provided by the painter but were ordered or provided at the expense of the person who employed him <note>which the master provides for the painter</note>. The other he calls *the austere kind*. And thus (says he), "*Value is sought in things, not in the soul: The cost, and not the life and art, is studied.*" He shows, on the contrary, what care APELLES took to subdue the *florid* colors with a darkening varnish, <note>so that the same process might secretly give austerity to the overly bright colors</note>. And he says just before, regarding some of the finest pieces by APELLES, "That they were created in four colors only." So great and venerable was SIMPLICITY held among the ancients, and so certain was the ruin of all true elegance in life or art wherever this mistress was once abandoned or condemned! See PLINY, *Book* 35. See also, above, *page* 144 in the notes, and *page* 222.

</margin>

But despite this hazard to our *taste* from a multiplicity of reading, we are not, it seems, in the least bit scrupulous in our choice of subject. We read whatever comes next to us. What was first put into our hands when we were young serves us afterward for serious study and wise research when we are old. Many of us, indeed, are so grave as to continue this exercise of youth throughout the remainder of our lives. The authors who provide exercise of this kind have been described above* in the beginning of this treatise. 

The manner of this exercise is called *meditation*, and it is of a sort so solemn and profound that we do not dare so much as to thoroughly examine the subject on which we are told to meditate. This is a kind of *task-reading* in which a TASTE is not permitted. However little we take of this diet, it is enough to give full exercise to our grave mood and satisfy the appetite for further research and solid contemplation. The rest is holiday, diversion, play, and fancy. We reject all *rules*, thinking it an injury to our *diversions* to have regard for *truth* or *nature*—without which, however, no-

<meta>This page continues the argument that without a regard for truth or nature, reading becomes neither agreeable nor instructive. It explores how a distaste for poorly-conceived serious reading leads to a preference for ridiculous and exotic fictions.</meta>

<page-num>344</page-num>

<margin>Part 3.</margin>
nothing can be truly agreeable or entertaining; much less instructive or improving. Through a certain *surfeit taken in a wrong kind of *serious reading*, we apply ourselves, with full satisfaction, to the most *ridiculous*. The more remote our pattern is from anything moral or profitable, the more freedom and satisfaction we find in it. We do not care how *Gothic* or *barbarous* our models are; what ill-designed or monstrous figures we view; or what false proportions we trace or see described in *history, romance*, or *fiction*. And thus our *eye* and *ear* are lost. Our *relish* or *taste* must of necessity grow barbarous while *barbarian customs, savage manners, Indian wars*, and wonders of the *terra incognita* <note>Unknown land</note> employ our leisure hours and are the chief materials that furnish a *library*.

THESE are in our present days what *books of chivalry* were in those of our forefathers. I do not know what *faith* our valiant ancestors may have had in the stories of their giants, their dragons, and St. Georges. But regarding our *faith*, indeed, as well as our *taste* in this other way of reading, I must confess I cannot consider it without astonishment.

It must certainly be something other than *incredulity* that shapes the taste and judgment of many gentlemen whom we hear censored as *atheists* for attempting to philosophize in a newer way than any known recently. For my own part, I have always thought this sort of men to be generally more credulous, though in a different way, than the common people. Besides what I have observed in conversation with men of this character, I can point to many anathematized <note>condemned or cursed</note> authors who, if they lack a true *Israelitish* faith, make up for it with a *Chinese* or *Indian* one. If they are short of faith regarding *Syria* or *Palestine*, they have their full measure of it for *America* or *Japan*. *Histories* of *Incas* or *Iroquois*, written by *friars* and *missionaries*, pirates and renegades, sea captains and "trustworthy" travelers, pass for authentic records and are *canonical* to the *virtuosos* <note>intellectuals or collectors with a specialized interest in the arts and sciences</note> of this sort. Though *Christian* miracles may not satisfy them, they dwell with the highest contentment on the *prodigies* <note>marvels or supernatural events</note> of *Moorish* and *pagan* *countries*. They take far more pleasure in hearing monstrous accounts of monstrous men and manners than in the most refined and best narrations of the affairs, governments, and lives of the wisest and most civilized people.

It is the same *taste* which makes us prefer a *Turkish History* to a *Grecian* or a *Roman* one; an ARIOSTO to a VIRGIL; and a romance or novel to an *Iliad*. We have no regard for the character or genius of our *author*; nor are we curious enough to observe how capable he is in the judgment of *facts*, or how ingenious in the texture of his *lies*. For *facts* poorly related, even with the greatest sincerity and good faith, may prove to be the worst sort of deceit. And mere *lies*, judiciously composed, can teach us the *truth of things* better than any other method. But to amuse ourselves with such authors as neither know how to *lie* nor *tell the truth* reveals a TASTE which, it seems to me, one should not be inclined to envy. Yet we are so enchanted with the *traveling memoirs* of any casual adventurer that, whatever his character or genius may be, we have no sooner turned over a page or two than we begin to interest ourselves highly in his affairs. No sooner has he taken a ship at the mouth of the *Thames*, or sent his baggage before him to *Gravesend* or the *Buoy* in the *Nore*, than straightaway our atten-

### ONE WOULD imagine that * our *philosophical writers*, who pretend to treat of

---

* Considering what has been so often said on this subject of philosophy, learning, and the *sister-arts*, after that ancient model which has since been so much corrupted; it may not be out of place, perhaps, to hear the confession of one of the greatest and most learned of moderns upon this head. "To be sure, those will agree with the ancient sages that poetry is a companion to the majesty of philosophy, a roommate of the most serious philosophy—those whom we see, having set aside all concern for morals (which is true philosophy), placing the highest wisdom in I-know-not-what petty arguments, in sophisticated trifles, in childish subtleties, and finally in the maimed words of dialectic, as Euphrades Themistius complained even in his own time! To be sure, the manly strength of Persius's eloquence, or that hidden erudition, will captivate those for whom it seems better and preferable to retain ancient barbarism tenaciously and to live in ignorance of all antiquity, rather than to reclaim for themselves the possession of literature (once extinguished by similar laziness, but truly recalled to light from deep human forgetfulness by the great benefit of immortal God in the time of our fathers) and for each to assert it for posterity to the best of his power! Arrian writes, however, that the very wise old man Epictetus accused those of impiety toward God who, in the study of philosophy, [the power of expression]..."

> "...[rational] power, or despising the *care of speech* as if it were a trivial matter: since indeed, as that divine man said, it is impious for a man to dishonor the graces received from God. *Behold a true Philosopher! Behold a golden voice!* Nor is the prophecy of the most excellent philosopher Synesius less memorable—confirmed as it was by a sad outcome—which was published by him long before, when he saw the method of study similarly perverted by his contemporaries. For arguing against those who brought childishness and sophistry to the most holy studies of Theology instead of solid erudition, he uttered this fateful prophecy: *There is a danger*, he said, *that these people, falling into a certain abyss of nonsense, may be destroyed.* The danger is that men of this sort, having slipped into a certain abyss of foolishness, may be utterly corrupted. If only this oracle had proven false! But indeed, the invasions of the Goths and Alans provided the opportunity for that corruption of this Queen of Sciences, and of all others which followed; yet the more immediate and true cause of it is the perverted method of study, a corrupt education in the liberal arts, and a simultaneous ignorance of languages and of all better literature. . . . However, great men certainly did not commit the precepts and examples of virtue to memory and pass them down to posterity so that we might learn them for the idle pleasure of the ears, or for the vain boasting of useless erudition; rather, they did so that they might rouse us by their diligent studies to dig up and bring into action the seeds of **RIGHT AND HONORABLE CONDUCT**; which, although we received them from Nature, yet because they are surrounded by vices and nearly buried, they lie hidden in our souls—unless a better cultivation is applied—as if buried deep in some pit. This is the aim of all those volumes that Philosophers have written on Moral Discipline. Most of the collection of Greek and Latin Poets tends toward the same goal, though by different paths. For however many types of Poets there are (and there are very many), there are nearly as many diversions and winding paths leading to that same end." Isaac Casaubon in the Preface of his Commentary on Persius. See above, page 190, 191, etc. and 207, 208, 286, and 298, 299, and 333, etc. and 338, etc. And VOL. III. p. 61, 78, 79, etc. and 239, 240, 241. in the Notes.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the moral duties of authors and philosophers, moving from the need to represent virtue to a critique of contemporary moralists who undermine the natural basis of ethics.</meta>

Actions. One would imagine that if they turned their eye toward remote countries (of which they like so much to speak), they should search for that simplicity of manners and innocence of behavior which has been often known among mere savages before they were corrupted by our commerce and, by sad example, instructed in all kinds of treachery and inhumanity. It would be advantageous to us to hear the causes of this strange corruption in ourselves, and be made to consider our deviation from nature and from that just purity of manners which might be expected, especially from a people so assisted and enlightened by religion. For who would not naturally expect more justice, fidelity, temperance, and honesty from *Christians* than from *Mahometans* or mere *Pagans*? But our modern moralists are so far from condemning any unnatural vices or corrupt manners—whether in our own or foreign climates—that they would have **VICE** itself appear as natural as **VIRTUE**. From the worst examples, they would represent to us:

"That all actions
are naturally indifferent; that they have
no note or character of good or ill
in themselves, but are distinguished by
mere **FASHION**, **LAW**, or arbitrary
**DECREE**."

Wonderful philosophy! Raised from the dregs of an illiterate, mean kind,

<meta>This page continues Shaftesbury's critique of moral relativism, transitioning from the previous page's discussion of modern moralists to an argument that virtue, like harmony in music or symmetry in architecture, is rooted in nature rather than human caprice.</meta>

<page-num>353</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>
<sig>Z 2</sig>

<margin>Section 3.</margin>
kind, which was always despised among the great ancients and rejected by all men of action or sound learning; but, in these times, it is imperfectly copied from the original and, with much disadvantage, imitated and adopted commonly by both religious and non-religious thinkers in the moral field.

Should a writer on *music*, addressing himself to the students and lovers of the art, declare to them, "That the measure or rule of HARMONY was *caprice* or *will*, *whim* or *fashion*"; it is not very likely he would be heard with great attention or treated with real seriousness. For HARMONY is harmony by *nature*, no matter how ridiculously men judge *music*. Similarly, *symmetry* and *proportion* are still founded *in nature*, no matter how barbarous men's imagination proves to be, or how *Gothic* their fashions become in architecture, sculpture, or any other visual art. It is the same case where *life* and MANNERS are concerned. *Virtue* has the same fixed standard. The same *numbers*, *harmony*, and *proportion* have a place in MORALS; and they are discoverable in the *characters* and *dispositions* of mankind; in which are laid the true foundations of an art and science superior to every other form of human practice and understanding.

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that morality is not a matter of whim or social fashion but is rooted in natural principles of harmony and proportion, much like music or architecture. He posits that virtue follows a fixed standard discoverable within human character and natural dispositions.</summary>
<keywords>moral philosophy, natural law, harmony, virtue, aesthetics, human nature, ethics, proportion, symmetry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that there is an objective standard in nature for art and morality, shifting the focus from the principles of harmony to the responsibility of the writer as a copyist of nature.</meta>

<page-num>354</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>
<sig>4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Standard</meta>

<margin>
Part 3.
</margin>

This, I suppose therefore, is highly necessary for a *writer* to comprehend. For things are stubborn, and will not be as we fancy them, or as fashion varies, but as they stand in nature. Now, whether the writer is a *poet*, *philosopher*, or of whatever kind, he is in truth nothing other than a *copyist after* NATURE. His style may be differently suited to the different times he lives in, or to the different humor of his age or nation; his manner, his dress, and his *coloring* may vary. But if his *drawing* is incorrect, or his *design* contrary to nature, his work will be found ridiculous when it is thoroughly examined. For nature will not be mocked. Prejudice against her can never be very lasting. Her *decrees* and *instincts* are powerful, and her sentiments are *inborn*. She has a strong *party abroad* and as strong a one *within ourselves*; and when any slight is put upon her, she can soon turn the reproach around and make large reprisals on the *taste* and judgment of her antagonists.

WHATEVER *philosopher*, *critic*, or *author* is convinced of this prerogative of nature will easily be persuaded to apply himself to the great work of reforming his TASTE; which he will have reason to suspect if he is not the kind of person who has deliberately endeavored to frame it by the just

<summary>Shaftesbury argues that all writers are essentially copyists of nature and must adhere to its immutable laws rather than fleeting fashions. He asserts that nature’s inherent instincts are so powerful that any artistic design or judgment that defies them will eventually be exposed as ridiculous.</summary>
<keywords>nature, aesthetics, literary theory, taste, copyist, artistic design, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that an author must align their taste with the objective standard of nature rather than following the whims of fashion. It emphasizes the need for a rigorous internal self-examination to achieve this alignment.</meta>

<page-num>355</page-num>
<header>ADVICE to an Author.</header>

*Standard of Nature*. <margin>Section 3.</margin> Whether this is his case, he will easily discover by appealing to his memory. For *custom* and *fashion* are powerful seducers; and he must of necessity have fought hard against these to have attained that justness of *taste* which is required in one who pretends to *follow nature*. But if no such conflict can be called to mind, it is a certain sign that the party has a *taste* very little different from the vulgar. And on this account, he should instantly betake himself to the wholesome practice recommended in this *treatise*. He should set in motion the most powerful faculties of his mind and assemble the best forces of his wit and judgment in order to make a formal descent on the territories of the *heart*, resolving to decline no combat nor listen to any terms until he has pierced into its inmost provinces and reached the seat of empire. No treaties should distract him; no advantages lead him aside. All other speculations should be suspended, all other mysteries resigned, until this necessary campaign was made and these inward conflicts learned; by which he would be able to gain at least some tolerable insight into *himself* and knowledge of his own natural *principles*.

IT MAY here perhaps be thought that, notwithstanding the particular *ad-*

vice we have given, in relation to the forming of a **TASTE** in *natural characters* and manners; we are still defective in our performance while we remain silent on *supernatural cases*, and do not bring into our consideration the manners and characters delivered to us in *Holy Scripture*. But this objection will soon vanish when we consider that there can be no rules given by *human wit* for that which was never humanly conceived, but *divinely* dictated and inspired.

**FOR** this reason, it would be in vain for any * *poet* or ingenious author to form his characters after the models of our sacred writers. And whatever certain *critics* may have advanced concerning the structure of *a heroic poem of this kind*, I will be bold to prophesy that the success will never meet expectation.

**IT** must be admitted that in our sacred history we have leaders, conquerors, founders of nations, deliverers, and patriots who, even in a human sense, are in no way behind the chief of those so much celebrated by the *ancients*. There is nothing in the story of **AENEAS** which is not equaled or exceeded by a **JOSHUA** or a **MOSES**. But as illustrious as the acts of these sacred chiefs are, it would be

**NOTWITHSTANDING** the pious endeavors which, as devout Christians, we may have used in order to separate ourselves from the interests of mere *heathens* and *infidels*; notwithstanding the true pains we may have taken to arm our hearts on behalf of a *chosen people* against their neighboring nations of a false religion and worship; there will still be found such a partiality remaining in us toward creatures of the same make and figure as ourselves as will hinder us from viewing with satisfaction the punishments inflicted by human hands on such *aliens* and *idolaters*.

**IN** mere *poetry*, and the pieces of wit and literature, there is a liberty of thought and easiness of humor indulged to us, in which perhaps we are not so well able to contemplate the *divine judgments* and see clearly into the *justice* of those *ways* which are declared to be so far from *our ways* and above our highest thoughts or understandings. In such a situation of mind, we can hardly endure to see *heathen*

There is a certain perverse humanity in us which inwardly resists the divine commission, even if it is ever so plainly revealed. The wit of the best poet is not sufficient to reconcile us to the campaign of a Joshua, or the retreat of a Moses by the assistance of an Egyptian *loan*. Nor will it be possible, by the *Muses’* art, to make that *royal hero* appear amiable in human eyes who found such favor in the eye of heaven. Such are mere *human hearts* that they can hardly find the least sympathy with that only one which had the character of being after the *pattern* of the Almighty’s.

It is apparent, therefore, that the manners, actions, and characters of *Holy Scripture* are in no way the proper subject of authors other than *theologians* themselves. They are matters incomprehensible in philosophy; they are above the reach of the mere human *historian*, the *politician*, or the *moralist*, and are too sacred to be submitted to the *poet’s* fancy when inspired by no other *spirit* than that of his profane mistresses, the MUSES.

I should be unwilling to examine rigorously the performance of our great * poet,

The <margin>Section 3.</margin> *War in Heaven*, and the *Catastrophe* of that original pair from whom the generations of mankind were propagated, are matters so abstrusely revealed, and with such a resemblance to *mythology*, that they can more easily bear whatever figurative construction or fantastic turn the poet may think fit to give them. But should he venture further into the lives and characters of the patriarchs, the holy matrons, heroes, and heroines of the chosen seed; should he employ the sacred *machine*, the exhibitions and interventions of divinity according to Holy Writ, to support the *action* of his piece; he would soon find the weakness of his pretended *orthodox* MUSE and prove how little those divine patterns were capable of human imitation, or of being raised to any other majesty or sublime than that in which they originally appear.

THE *theology*, or THEOGONY, *of the heathens* could admit of such different turns and figurative expressions as suited the fancy and judgment of each philosopher or poet. But the purity of our faith will admit of no such variation. The *Christian* THEOLOGY—the *birth*, *procedure*, *generation*, and *personal distinction* of the DIVINITY—are mysteries only to be determined by the *initiated* or *ordained*;

ordained; to whom the state has assigned the guardianship and promulgation of the divine oracles. It is not fitting for those who are uninspired from heaven and uncommissioned from earth to search with curiosity into the origin of those holy rites and records, *established by law*. If we were to make such an attempt, we would in all probability find less satisfaction the further we presumed to carry our speculations. Having once dared to leave the authority and direction of the *law*, we would easily be subject to *heterodoxy* and *error*, since we would have no better warrant left to us for the authority of our sacred SYMBOLS than the integrity, candor, and disinterestedness of their compilers and registrars. How great that candor and disinterestedness may have been, we have no other histories to inform us than those of their own licensing or composing. But busy persons, who meddlesomely search into these records, are ready even from this to draw proofs very disadvantageous to the fame and character of this *succession* of men. And persons moderately read in these histories are apt to judge the temperament of ancient *councils* no differently than that of later *synods* and modern *convocations*.

But though the explanation of such deep *mysteries* and religious duties is allotted as the peculiar province of the *sacred order*, it is presumed, nevertheless, that it may be lawful for other *authors* to retain their ancient privilege of instructing mankind in a way of pleasure and entertainment. *Poets* may be allowed their fictions, and *philosophers* their systems. It would go hard with mankind if the patentees for religion were commissioned for all instruction and advice relating to manners or conversation. *The stage* may be allowed to instruct as well as the *pulpit*. The way of *wit* and *humor* may be as serviceable as that of *gravity* and *seriousness*, and the way of plain *reason* as well as that of exalted *revelation*. The main matter is to keep these provinces distinct and settle their just boundaries. And on this account it is that we have en-

# Advice to an Author.
<page-num>362</page-num>

<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish or wavy line.</image-desc>

endeavored to represent to modern *authors* the necessity of making this separation justly, and in due form.

It would be somewhat hard, it seems to me, if RELIGION, *as established by law* \*, were not allowed the same privilege as HERALDRY. It is agreed on all sides that particular persons may *design* or *paint*, in their private capacity, in whatever manner they think fit; but they must *blazon* only as the public directs. Their *lion* or *bear* must be figured as the science appoints; and their *supporters* and *crest* must be such as their wise and gallant ancestors have procured for them. It does not matter whether the shapes of these animals maintain a just proportion with nature. It does not matter though different or contrary forms are joined in one. That which is denied to *painters* or *poets* is permitted to HERALDS. *Naturalists* may, in their separate and distinct capacity, inquire, as they think fit, into the real existence and natural truth of things; but they must by no means dispute the authorized forms. *Mermaids* and *griffins* were the wonder of our forefathers and, as such, delivered down to us by the authentic traditions and delineations mentioned above. We ought not even to criticize the features or di-

But as worshipful as are the persons of the illustrious heralds CLARENCIEUX, GARTER, and the rest of those eminent sustainers of *British* honor and antiquity; it is to be hoped that in a more civilized age, such as we currently have the good fortune to live in, they will not attempt to strain their privileges to the same height as formerly. Having been reduced by law, or settled practice, from the power they once enjoyed, they will not, it is presumed, in defiance of the magistrate and civil power, erect their stages and lists anew, introduce the manner of civil combat, set us to tilt and tournament, and raise again those defiances and mortal frays of which their *order* were once the chief managers and promoters.
